**English Animated B2.1 · *Reading Between Lines*** — a newsroom, Toronto.

This volume contains **10 of 10 units**, written in full: every text, every task, every
figure drawn. Units are 20 pages each at B-level and 22 at A-level, which is the extent specified
in the series plan.

## Part 0 · The Big Picture ◆ **A · THE WORLD**


![An open-plan newsroom at twenty to four in the afternoon, with a wall clock, a market ticker, a draft headline on a screen, a telephone on speaker and an editor standing over a seated reporter.](figures/fig_b21_u01_p00_v01.png){width=6.6in}

*The newsroom at 15.40 — Twelve things to find. Four of them are counting down to the same moment.*


# What is a fact worth?

**By the end of this unit you can choose between the simple and the continuous deliberately, and
say what each one claims. Two hundred words.**

| ◆ **A · THE WORLD** | A newsroom at 15.40, forty minutes before a market closes, holding a story it cannot stand up |
|---|---|
| ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM** | aspect as meaning, across every tense · 28 words for verification and evidence · 22 words that carry aspect in their own right · prominence on the new information |
| ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE** | Two hundred words in which every tense choice is defensible |

---

### 0A · Find it

**Find twelve things in this picture.** Four of them are counting down to the same moment.

> a wall clock at 15.40 · a market ticker · a screen with a draft headline · a telephone on
> speaker · a printed email · a desk with nobody at it · a second screen showing a rival's site ·
> a whiteboard with two names on it · a lawyer's number on a note · a cup of cold coffee ·
> a standing editor · a sitting reporter

### 0B · Guess it

**Two of the twelve exist only because of one of the others.** Which, and what is the causal
chain?

### 0C · Say it ⏺

**Record sixty seconds.** *Describe something you believe is true and cannot prove. What would it
take to prove it, and who would have to do the work?* You will record it again at the end of this
unit.

## Part 1 · Vocabulary Lab 1 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**


![A cutaway of a single news claim in four layers, from the person who said it, through corroboration and verification, to publication and what happens afterwards.](figures/fig_b21_u01_p01_v02.png){width=6.6in}

*One claim, in section — Twelve words, and each of them lives on exactly one layer.*


### 1A · Meet — the words of a claim

Match each word to one layer of the cutaway.

> **claim** · **source** · **evidence** · **corroboration** · **verification** ·
> **attribution** · **provenance** · **correction** · **retraction** · **embargo** ·
> **stringer** · **dateline**

### 1B · Sort — before publication, after publication, or both?

Three columns.

> correction · source · embargo · corroboration · retraction · claim · provenance ·
> verification · evidence · attribution

### 1C · Meet — the words that describe a story's state

| | |
|---|---|
| **a single source** | One person says it. Nobody else has said it. |
| **a verified account** | More than one independent route to the same fact. |
| **a working assumption** | Believed, acted on, and not yet evidence. |
| **a story with legs** | It will still be a story on Thursday. |
| **the cost of being first** | Measured in corrections, and paid later. |

### 1D · Collocation Lab

Complete each one with a word from the box, and say which of the five is the only one that is
not about publishing.

> **stand a story up** · **run a correction** · **hold the line** · **on the record** ·
> **off the record**

1. We cannot ______ ______ ______ ______ with one caller and a screenshot.
2. If we are wrong on Friday we ______ ______ ______ on Monday, on page two.
3. She would say it ______ ______ ______ and not with her name on it.
4. He said the same thing ______ ______ ______ , twenty minutes later, after we asked twice.
5. Everybody else has gone. We ______ ______ ______ .

### 1E · The phrasal verbs, and the three fixed expressions

> **stand up** · **back up** · **hold off**
>
> **f** *we can't stand it up* · **f** *it checks out* · **f** *who else has it?*

**One of the three fixed expressions is a question that is never really a question.**
Which, and what is it actually doing?

### 1F · Own it

Write one sentence about something you know that you could not currently **stand up**, and one
about something you could.

## Part 2 · Listening ◆ **A · THE WORLD**


![A news editor standing over a seated business reporter in a newsroom with a phone on speaker between them, each with two speech balloons and a thought balloon.](figures/fig_b21_u01_p02_v07.png){width=6.6in}

*Fifteen forty — She is standing. He has filed it. Neither of them has twenty minutes.*


### 2A · Fifteen forty 🔊 Track 1.1

**Before you listen.** The market closes at 16.00. The paper has one source and a claim that
would move a share price.

**Listen once.** What exactly does the source claim, and what exactly can the paper prove?

**Listen again. Complete the table.**

| | What is claimed | What is corroborated | What is assumed |
|---|---|---|---|
| the job cuts | | | |
| the timing | | | |
| the board meeting | | | |

**Listen again. True or false?**

1. The reporter has one source and a screenshot. ☐ T ☐ F
2. The claim was made on the record. ☐ T ☐ F
3. A rival outlet has the same story. ☐ T ☐ F
4. The editor decides to publish before the close. ☐ T ☐ F

**Noticing.** Four sentences from the recording.

1. *The company is cutting four hundred jobs.*
2. *The company cuts four hundred jobs.*
3. *The company has been cutting jobs since March.*
4. *The company had been going to announce it on Thursday.*

**All four are about the same event. Which of the four makes the strongest factual claim, and
which commits the paper to the least?**

### 2B · Decoding clinic 🔊 Track 1.2

Thirty seconds at speed. Three jobs.

1. In *we can't stand it up*, which syllable carries the main prominence, and why that one?
2. The editor repeats a phrase three times with the prominence in a different place each time.
   Write the three versions.
3. One sentence ends on a word that is not new information. What effect does that have?

## Part 3 · Grammar Lab 1 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### Aspect is not about time

### 3A · Notice

Look at the four sentences in Part 2 again.

1. Which of the four presents the event as a whole, closed and countable?
2. Which presents it as in progress, and therefore incomplete and possibly reversible?
3. What does the perfect in sentence 3 add that sentence 1 does not have?
4. What does sentence 4 claim about something that did not happen?

### 3B · See


![A timeline showing the same event rendered four ways: as a closed point, as an open band, as a point seen from a later vantage, and as a band running up to a later vantage.](figures/fig_b21_u01_p03_v09.png){width=6.6in}

*Aspect is a claim about shape, not about time — The same event, drawn four ways. Time is the horizontal axis and aspect is the shape.*


### 3C · Know

| | | |
|---|---|---|
| **simple** | the event as a complete unit | *The company cut four hundred jobs.* |
| **continuous** | the event as in progress, unfinished | *The company is cutting four hundred jobs.* |
| **perfect** | the event seen from a later point | *The company has cut four hundred jobs.* |
| **perfect continuous** | duration up to a later point | *The company has been cutting jobs since March.* |

> **The choice is a claim, not a decoration.** *Is cutting* says the process is live and the
> number may change. *Cut* says it is done and the number is final. A newspaper that writes
> *cuts* when it means *is cutting* has published a fact it does not have.

> **Four tenses, one distinction.** Aspect is independent of time: *was cutting*, *is cutting*,
> *will be cutting* make the same claim about completeness at three different times.

| | |
|---|---|
| **state verbs resist the continuous** | *owns*, *contains*, *consists of* — and when they take it, the meaning shifts |
| **the continuous softens** | *I'm hoping you can help* is less committed than *I hope* |
| **the perfect links** | the event is being presented **because of its consequence now** |
| **the future in the past** | *was going to*, *had been going to* — a plan that did not survive |

> **Why it matters.** Every sentence in a news report is a claim about how finished something is,
> and that is the claim most likely to be wrong.

### 3D · Use — controlled

Complete with the most defensible aspect.

> The company (1) ______ (cut) four hundred jobs — the board minutes exist and we have seen
> them.
> According to one person, the company (2) ______ (consider) cuts, and the number (3) ______
> (move) all week.
> It (4) ______ (lose) money since the third quarter, which nobody disputes.
> On Tuesday the board (5) ______ (go to announce) it on Thursday, and then it did not.

### 3E · Use — guided

Rewrite each one so that it commits the paper to less, changing only the aspect.

1. The company closes the Thunder Bay plant.
2. The regulator rejected the application.
3. The minister has resigned.
4. Three directors sold shares in the week before the announcement.
5. The union accepts the offer.

### 3F · Use — open

**In pairs.** One of you has a claim and a single source. The other is the standards editor.
**Negotiate a sentence that can be published.** The aspect is the negotiation.

### 3G · Error autopsy

> ✗ *The company is owning four hundred patents.*
> ✗ *By Friday the number was changing six times.*
> ✗ *She is knowing the figure since March.*

For each one, say what the writer was trying to claim and what the sentence actually claims.

### 3H · Check — eight items

> 1. The company ______ (cut) 400 jobs. *(confirmed, complete)*
> 2. The company ______ (cut) jobs since March. *(duration up to now)*
> 3. The number ______ (change) all week. *(in progress, unfinished)*
> 4. The board ______ (go to announce) it on Thursday. *(a plan that failed)*
> 5. ✗ *The report is containing three errors.* → correct it.
> 6. ✗ *We are knowing this since Tuesday.* → correct it.
> 7. ✗ *The company was cutting 400 jobs and the figure is final.* → why do these contradict?
> 8. Rewrite *The minister resigns* so that it claims less.

## Part 4 · Speaking ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

### 4A · Fluency starter — the aspect swap

One person makes a statement in the simple. The next repeats it in the continuous and says what
has changed about the claim. **Round the room. Nobody may say *nothing*.**

### 4B · The functional core — saying what you can prove


![Two published sentences about the same story set side by side, one unhedged and published before the market closed, the other hedged and published the next morning.](figures/fig_b21_u01_p04_v04.png){width=6.6in}

*Two sentences, one source — The same information, published forty minutes apart by two newspapers.*


**Language Bank**

| | |
|---|---|
| **the claim** | *We understand that… · It is thought to have… · One source says…* |
| **the hedge** | *as it stands · at the time of writing · is being treated as* |
| **the commitment** | *it checks out · a verified account · on the record* |
| **the refusal** | *we can't stand it up · who else has it?* |

**Information gap.** Learner A has what the source said. Learner B has what the documents show.
**Draft one sentence that both of you would sign.**

### 4C · The performance — three minutes ⏺

You are the reporter. Your partner is the standards editor and will stop you the moment you
claim more than you can prove. **Three minutes.** You must:

> state the claim · say what is corroborated and what is a working assumption · propose a
> publishable sentence · say what would change it

**Record it.**

### 4D · Observer

One person tracks every tense choice and marks the ones that claimed more than the evidence.

### 4E · Three criteria

> ☐ Was every aspect choice defensible against the evidence?
> ☐ Did I separate *corroborated* from *assumed* out loud, in those words?
> ☐ Did I say what would change the sentence?

## Part 5 · Reading ◆ **A · THE WORLD**

### 5A · Predict

The title is **"Forty minutes behind."**
**What do you think the paper lost, and what do you think it gained?**

### 5B · Read — two minutes

---

**Forty minutes behind**

At 15.40 on a Tuesday the Herald had a single source, a screenshot and a claim that four hundred
jobs were going at a plant in Thunder Bay. The market closed at 16.00. Everybody in the room knew
what the story was worth before the close and what it was worth after it.

The stringer who filed it has lived in Thunder Bay for eleven years and has never been wrong. That
is not the same as corroboration and everybody in the room knew that too.

At 15.52 a rival outlet published. The share price moved four per cent in the last eight minutes
of trading. The Herald was still asking a second plant for a second account, and did not publish
until 09.10 the next morning.

On Thursday the company confirmed three hundred and ten jobs, not four hundred, and said no
decision had been taken on the timing. The rival ran a correction on Friday, two column
centimetres, on page eleven.

Nobody in the Herald newsroom believes the paper made money on Tuesday. The argument in the room
since has been about a different question: whether being right on Wednesday is a product anybody
is buying.

---

### 5C · Comprehension — eight questions

1. What did the paper have at 15.40?
2. What time did the market close?
3. How long had the stringer lived in Thunder Bay?
4. When did the rival publish?
5. How much did the share price move?
6. What figure did the company confirm, and how does it differ from the published one?
7. **Why does the writer say the stringer's record "is not the same as corroboration"?**
8. **The last paragraph changes the question. What is the new question, and why can nobody in the newsroom answer it?**

### 5D · Vocabulary in context

Infer from the text, then check.

> going at a plant · filed it · a second account · no decision had been taken · two column
> centimetres

### 5E · The counter-text


![The rival newspaper's original claim and its subsequent correction set out with their word counts, placement, aspect and what each one does and does not say.](figures/fig_b21_u01_p05_v05.png){width=6.6in}

*The claim and the correction — The original ran at 15.52 on Tuesday. The correction ran on Friday, on page eleven.*


**Read the rival's correction and the Herald's story, and answer.**

1. What does the correction say, and where is it placed?
2. **The original claim and the correction use different aspects. Identify both and say what each one claims.**
3. How many words are in the original claim and how many in the correction?
4. **What does the correction not say?**

### 5F · Two texts

The Herald's story at 09.10 uses *is understood to be considering*.
The rival's story at 15.52 used *is cutting*.

**Both turned out to be wrong about the number. Were they wrong in the same way?**
Write two sentences.

## Part 6 · Vocabulary Lab 2 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### Words that carry aspect without a verb

### 6A · Sort — closed, running, or unsettled?

Three columns.

> settled · ongoing · emergent · provisional · iterative · habitual · unfolding ·
> momentary

### 6B · Chunk completion

1. **as it ______** — what is true at this moment and may not be later
2. **at the time of ______** — the writer dates the claim to the act of writing
3. **is being ______ as** — somebody else has classified it, and the writer has not
4. **is ______ to** — a claim with the source removed
5. **had been ______** — a plan that was overtaken
6. **is ______ to have** — a past event, attributed to nobody

### 6C · Three that are not interchangeable

> *is understood to* · *is thought to have* · *is being treated as*

**Say which one attributes to an unnamed source, which attributes to nobody at all, and which
attributes to an institution's procedure.**

### 6D · The phrasal verbs and the fixed expressions

> **play out** · **bear out** · **pan out**
>
> **f** *the situation is developing* · **f** *we understand that* · **f** *it now appears that*

**Two of these three fixed expressions can be written before anything is known.** Which two, and
what does the third require?

### 6E · Contrast Clinic

Eight items. For each, write the sentence with the aspect that matches the evidence given.

1. A plan for Thursday that was abandoned on Tuesday. *(announce)*
2. Losses in every quarter since March. *(lose)*
3. Board minutes seen. 400 confirmed. *(cut)*
4. A single event last March, closed. *(close)*
5. A process that began in 2019 and has not finished. *(restructure)*
6. A number that changed three times on Monday. *(change)*
7. A regulator's decision published this morning. *(reject)*
8. One caller, no documents. *(cut)*

## Part 7 · Pronunciation & Fluency Lab ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**


![One sentence drawn five times as beats with a different syllable enlarged in each, with the implied question beside it.](figures/fig_b21_u01_p07_v10.png){width=6.6in}

*Prominence falls on what is new — The same seven words, five times. Each version answers a different unasked question.*


### Prominence falls on what is new

### 7A · Hear 🔊 Track 1.3

> *We can't stand it UP.* — the problem is the verification
> *WE can't stand it up. (somebody else can)* — the problem is us
> *We can't stand IT up. (we can stand the other one up)* — the problem is this story

### 7B · Say

Say each one three times. Then say this:

> *The company is cutting jobs. It isn't cutting FOUR HUNDRED jobs.* — the second prominence is
> the whole correction.

### 7C · Make it mean something

Say these two:

> *The company IS cutting jobs.* — somebody has denied it
> *The company is CUTTING jobs.* — somebody expected something else

**English puts the main prominence on the information the listener does not already have.**
Moving it is not emphasis for its own sake; it is a claim about what the listener knows.

### 7D · Fluency 30 ⏺

Thirty seconds: **one claim, stated four times with four different aspects**, each followed by
the evidence it would require. Three times, faster.

## Part 8 · Writing ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

### A story you can defend · 200 words

### 8A · Read the model

> **Thunder Bay plant: 400 jobs under review** `[the headline hedges with two words]`
>
> The Herald understands that the company is considering cuts of up to four hundred jobs at its
> Thunder Bay plant. `[*understands* and *is considering* — a claim with a source removed and a
> process presented as unfinished]`
>
> A single source, who was not authorised to speak, described a board paper circulated on Monday.
> The Herald has not seen the paper. `[what is not known, said in the second paragraph, not the
> ninth]`
>
> The company has been losing money at the plant since the third quarter of last year, which is a
> matter of public record. `[the perfect continuous, on the one fact that needs no source]`
>
> The company declined to comment. No decision is understood to have been taken on timing.
> `[the second hedge, and the thing the rival did not write]`

**Questions about the writer's choices:**

1. *is considering* rather than *is cutting*. **What exactly does the change in verb and aspect buy?**
2. *The Herald has not seen the paper.* **Why in paragraph two?**
3. The only perfect continuous in the piece is on the public-record fact. **Why there?**

### 8B · The anti-model

> The company is cutting 400 jobs at its Thunder Bay plant, the Herald can reveal. The cuts will be announced on Thursday. Workers were told nothing. The company refused to comment.

Four sentences, four unhedged claims, and three of them turned out to be wrong.
**Rewrite it so that every claim is defensible against the evidence described in 5B.**

### 8C · Plan

> the hedged headline → the claim with its hedge → what is not known →
> the one fact that needs no source → the refusal

### 8D · Write — 200 words
### 8E · Peer review — three criteria

> ☐ Is every aspect choice defensible against stated evidence?
> ☐ Is what is *not* known in the first half?
> ☐ Is there at least one fact that requires no source, and is it marked as such?

### 8F · Write it again · 8G · Publish

Rewrite after review. **Publish the two versions side by side with the evidence list between
them.**

## Part 9 · The File ◆ **A · THE WORLD + C · THE PERFORMANCE**


![A table of four claims learners commonly make about their own English with the evidence, corroboration, date of last test and the cost of each.](figures/fig_b21_u01_p09_v05.png){width=6.6in}

*Four claims a learner makes, and the evidence behind them — Each claim was made this term. Each has been acted on for years.*


# STUDY FILE · What you can stand up about your own English

### 9A · A claim nobody has corroborated 🔊 Track 1.4

Learners make claims about their own English constantly — *my grammar is bad*, *I can't
understand films*, *my pronunciation is the problem* — and almost none of them has ever been
checked against evidence.

| | |
|---|---|
| **the claim** | *I can't understand native speakers.* |
| **the evidence** | one film, in 2022, with the sound low |
| **the corroboration** | none |
| **what it costs** | three years of avoiding the thing |

### 9B · Stand one up

Take one claim you make about your own English. **Write the evidence you actually have for it,
with dates.** Most learners find the evidence is a single occasion.

### 9C · Role play — the standards editor

In pairs. One of you states three claims about your own English. The other is the standards
editor and may ask only: *What is your evidence? Who else has said it? When was it last tested?*

### 9D · The working assumption

Some claims cannot be tested cheaply and still have to be acted on. **Mark yours as working
assumptions, in writing.** A working assumption you have labelled is a different thing from a
belief you have forgotten you adopted.

### 9E · Transfer — before next week

**Test one claim.** Watch twenty minutes of something unscripted, with no subtitles, and write
down the percentage you followed. Bring the number.

## Part 10 · Mediation & Interaction ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**


![An infographic of the twenty minutes before the market close showing what each paper knew, what each published, the traffic each received, and the permanence of each piece of text.](figures/fig_b21_u01_p10_v12.png){width=6.6in}

*Twenty minutes, two newspapers, one correction — The three bands the newsroom argued about, and the fourth it never measured.*


### 10A · Relay

Half the class has the source's account. Half has the documents.
**Produce one agreed sentence by speaking only.** Ten minutes.

### 10B · Simplify — for one reader

A reader emails: *Why did you publish a day late?*
**Answer in four sentences** without blaming the rival and without claiming virtue.

### 10C · Online

Write **one message** to a colleague explaining why a sentence in their draft claims more than
the evidence. **Three sentences.** Name the aspect.

### 10D · Your language

Does your language mark the difference between *cuts* and *is cutting* grammatically?
**Translate all four sentences from 2A.** Which distinctions survive?

---

## Part 11 · The Decision ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

# Forty minutes

It is 15.40. One source, a screenshot, a claim that moves a share price, and twenty minutes of
trading left.

**Option one:** publish now with hedges. *Is understood to be considering.* Defensible, fast,
and a hedge in a headline is read by nobody.

**Option two:** hold until a second account. Costs the exclusive and about forty thousand
page views, and is what the paper's own standards policy requires.

**Option three:** publish the fact that you are holding it — a line saying the Herald has heard
the claim and has not been able to corroborate it.

### 11A · The evidence


![A line chart of corrections published per year against the proportion of readers saying they trust the paper, over six years, with a note about the lag between the two.](figures/fig_b21_u01_p11_v06.png){width=6.6in}

*Corrections, trust and the cost nobody can price — Six years of one newspaper, with the trust survey plotted against the correction count.*


🔊 **Track 1.6.** The standards editor, 30 seconds: *"We ran four hundred corrections last year.
Nobody can tell me what one costs, because the cost is not on the day you correct it. It is six
months later, in a different story, when somebody decides on balance not to believe us."*

### 11B · Talk about it

In threes. Use the frame. Everybody takes one position and must state the evidence it rests on.

> *I would ______ , because ______ , and I would be wrong if ______ .*

### 11C · Decide

Write **three sentences**: the decision, the evidence, and the condition under which you would
reverse it.

### 11D · Model decision

> Option three. The first two are a choice between being wrong and being late, and both of those
> have been priced. Nobody has ever priced the third, which is the only one that tells the reader
> what the paper actually knows at 15.40 — and what a reader buys from a newspaper, if they buy
> anything, is the difference between those two states.

### 11E · The Reveal

> **What happened.** The Herald held. It published at 09.10 with *is understood to be
> considering*, and it has never published a line saying what it is holding.
>
> Then something nobody in the room predicted: the rival's correction, two column centimetres on
> page eleven, was screenshotted and shared about forty thousand times. The correction travelled
> further than the story it corrected, and it is still the first result for the company's name.

**One question. You do not have to answer it out loud.**

> The newsroom argued for two days about whether being right on Wednesday is a product. What did
> the forty thousand screenshots suggest about who was buying it?

---

## Part 12 · Landing ◆ **all three lines**

### 12A · Recycle — fifteen items

> 1. The company ______ (cut) 400 jobs. *(confirmed, complete)*
> 2. The company ______ (cut) jobs since March. *(duration up to now)*
> 3. The number ______ (change) all week. *(in progress)*
> 4. The board ______ (go to announce) it on Thursday. *(a plan that failed)*
> 5. ______ it ______ , we have one source. *(two words — what is true at this moment)*
> 6. The claim ______ ______ ______ as unverified. *(three words — somebody else classified it)*
> 7. It ______ ______ ______ have begun in March. *(three words — attributed to nobody)*
> 8. We cannot ______ ______ ______ ______ . *(four words — the refusal)*
> 9. It ______ ______ . *(two words — the opposite of 8)*
> 10. We will have to ______ ______ ______ on Monday. *(three words — page two)*
> 11. The documents ______ ______ what she told us. *(two words)*
> 12. Let us see how it ______ ______ before we publish. *(two words)*
> 13. *(Unit 0)* Everybody else has gone. We ______ ______ ______ . *(three words)*
> 14. She said it ______ ______ ______ , without her name. *(three words)*
> 15. The Herald ______ that a board paper exists. *(the verb that removes the source)*

### 12B · Consolidate — one task, everything in it

**Write a 150-word account of something you witnessed.** Every verb must be defensible: mark in
the margin, for each one, whether you are claiming it is complete, in progress, or inferred.

### 12C · Can-do, with evidence

> ☐ I can choose aspect to match evidence. **Evidence:** 3H, 8D
> ☐ I can hedge a claim without weakening the sentence. **Evidence:** 4C, 6C
> ☐ I can hear and place prominence on new information. **Evidence:** 7C
> ☐ I can say what would change my sentence. **Evidence:** 11C

### 12D · Replay ⏺

Record 0C again. **Compare the two.** How many of your original claims had evidence attached?

### 12E · Glossary — fifty items, as a map

> **THE CLAIM** · claim · source · stringer · dateline · attribution · provenance
> **THE CHECK** · verification · evidence · corroboration · embargo
> **AFTERWARDS** · correction · retraction
> **THE STATES OF A STORY** · a single source · a verified account · a working assumption · a story with legs · the cost of being first
> **ON OR OFF** · on the record · off the record
> **WHAT YOU DO** · stand a story up · run a correction · hold the line · stand up · back up · hold off
> **IN THE ROOM** · we can't stand it up · it checks out · who else has it?
> **ASPECT, IN A WORD** · ongoing · momentary · habitual · iterative · provisional · settled · unfolding · emergent
> **ASPECT, IN A PHRASE** · as it stands · at the time of writing · has been developing · is being treated as · was being described as · is understood to · had been expecting · is thought to have
> **WHAT HAPPENS NEXT** · play out · bear out · pan out
> **WHAT YOU WRITE** · the situation is developing · we understand that · it now appears that

### 12F · Next

> **Unit 2 · Who is "we"?**



# B2.1 · Unit 2 · We Have Always Said

> **English Animated** · *Reading Between Lines* · a newsroom, Toronto
> Student's Book pages 28–49 · 22 pp · ~11 hours

## Part 0 · The Big Picture ◆ **A · THE WORLD**


![The editorial floor of a newspaper with a masthead on the wall, a bound archive volume, a framed first column, an unsigned leader proof on a screen and an empty chair at the conference table.](figures/fig_b21_u02_p00_v01.png){width=6.6in}

*The leader floor, Thursday afternoon — Twelve things to find. Three carry a name and two deliberately do not.*


# Who is "we"?

**By the end of this unit you can place yourself in a text deliberately, and say who you are
claiming to speak for. Two hundred words.**

| ◆ **A · THE WORLD** | An unsigned editorial that says *we have always said*, and an archive that disagrees |
|---|---|
| ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM** | the perfect across every tense, as a discourse move · 28 words for voice and readership · 22 words for inclusive and exclusive reference · stress on a pronoun |
| ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE** | Two hundred words in which the reader always knows who is speaking |

---

### 0A · Find it

**Find twelve things in this picture.** Three of them carry somebody's name and two deliberately
do not.

> the masthead on the wall · a leader-page proof with no byline · a photograph of the 1974 staff ·
> a bound archive volume · a columnist's framed first piece · a screen showing a reader comment ·
> an empty chair at the leader conference · a style guide open at page 9 · a list of this week's
> op-eds · a letter from a reader · a map of the city with one ward circled · a clock

### 0B · Guess it

**One thing in this picture is the reason another one is unsigned.** Which two, and in which
direction does the causation run?

### 0C · Say it ⏺

**Record sixty seconds.** *Describe an opinion you hold that a group you belong to also holds.
Say which parts are yours and which parts you inherited.* You will record it again at the end of
this unit.

## Part 1 · Vocabulary Lab 1 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**


![A cutaway of a newspaper's voice in four layers, from the named individual through the desk and the institution to the readership it claims to speak for.](figures/fig_b21_u02_p01_v02.png){width=6.6in}

*One voice, in section — Twelve words, and the layer each one speaks from.*


### 1A · Meet — the words of a voice

Match each word to one layer of the cutaway.

> **byline** · **masthead** · **readership** · **constituency** · **columnist** ·
> **editorial** · **outlet** · **audience** · **bureau** · **syndication** ·
> **correspondent** · **voice**

### 1B · Sort — a person, an institution, or a group of readers?

Three columns. **Two of the twelve belong in two columns and that is the point of the unit.**

> editorial · outlet · bureau · voice · columnist · constituency · audience · masthead ·
> readership · correspondent

### 1C · Meet — the phrases that claim a mandate

| | |
|---|---|
| **speak for** | A claim about authority that is almost never examined. |
| **on behalf of** | The same claim, in the passive voice of institutions. |
| **our readers** | A set nobody has counted, invoked to settle arguments. |
| **the paper's view** | A view with no person in it. |
| **the community affected** | The group most often written about and least often quoted. |

### 1D · Collocation Lab

Complete each one, and say which of the five is the only one that could be checked.

> **a named writer** · **an unsigned piece** · **local to the story** · **parachute
> journalism** · **whose paper is it**

1. The leader page carries ______ ______ ______ every day and has done since 1911.
2. A reporter flown in for three days produces ______ ______ , whatever the quality.
3. The stringer is ______ ______ ______ ______ and was not asked to write it.
4. Readers can argue with ______ ______ ______ ; nobody can argue with an institution.
5. The question under all of this is ______ ______ ______ ______ .

### 1E · The phrasal verbs, and the three fixed expressions

> **speak up** · **stand in** · **write up**
>
> **f** *we have always said* · **f** *this paper believes* · **f** *who is the we here?*

**Two of these fixed expressions assert and one interrogates.** Which, and what does the third
one do to the other two?

### 1F · Own it

Write one sentence beginning *We have always…* about a group you belong to, and then write down
the earliest date on which you can prove it.

## Part 2 · Listening ◆ **A · THE WORLD**


![A news editor and a researcher at a conference table with a bound archive volume open between them, each with two speech balloons and a thought balloon.](figures/fig_b21_u02_p02_v07.png){width=6.6in}

*The leader conference — She has a draft that says always. He has the volume for 2017.*


### 2A · The leader conference 🔊 Track 2.1

**Before you listen.** Tomorrow's editorial begins *We have always said*. A researcher has been
through the archive.

**Listen once.** What did the paper actually say in 2017, and what is being claimed now?

**Listen again. Complete the table.**

| | The claim | The archive | Who the *we* includes |
|---|---|---|---|
| 2017 | | | |
| 2021 | | | |
| today | | | |

**Listen again. True or false?**

1. The paper took the same position in 2017 as it takes now. ☐ T ☐ F
2. The editorial will carry a byline. ☐ T ☐ F
3. Nobody in the room was at the paper in 2017. ☐ T ☐ F
4. The researcher is asked to find a form of words. ☐ T ☐ F

**Noticing.** Four sentences from the recording.

1. *We have always said this.*
2. *We said this in 2017.*
3. *We had said the opposite, until 2019.*
4. *By next year we will have said it for a decade.*

**All four are perfects or simples about the same institution. Which one makes a claim about
continuity, and which one makes a claim about a point?**

### 2B · Decoding clinic 🔊 Track 2.2

Thirty seconds at speed. Three jobs.

1. The editor says *we* eleven times and stresses it twice. Write the two stressed instances.
2. What changes when *we* is stressed?
3. One speaker uses *one* where another would use *we*. What does that do to the claim?

## Part 3 · Grammar Lab 1 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### The perfect is a move, not a tense

### 3A · Notice

Look at the four sentences in Part 2 again.

1. Which sentence closes the event at a point in the past?
2. Which one connects a past event to the present moment, and why is that useful here?
3. What does the past perfect in sentence 3 do to the reader's timeline?
4. What does the future perfect in sentence 4 allow the writer to assert?

### 3B · See


![A timeline showing a present perfect reaching to now, a past perfect reaching to an earlier point, and a future perfect reaching to a point ahead of now.](figures/fig_b21_u02_p03_v09.png){width=6.6in}

*Three perfects, three vantage points — Each perfect imports a past event into a different present.*


### 3C · Know

| | | |
|---|---|---|
| **present perfect** | the event is relevant **now** | *We have always said this.* |
| **past perfect** | the event is relevant to a **past** point | *We had said the opposite, until 2019.* |
| **future perfect** | the event is complete by a **future** point | *By next year we will have said it for a decade.* |
| **the discourse function** | the perfect imports a past event into the present argument | and that is why institutions like it |

> **The perfect hides the date.** *We have always said* contains no year. The simple past
> requires one, which is why an archive is dangerous to a paper that prefers the perfect.

> **Inclusive and exclusive *we*.** English does not mark the difference and most languages that
> do mark it find English maddening here: *we have always said* can mean the institution, the
> editorial board, the staff, or the staff and the readers together, and the sentence does not
> say which.

| | |
|---|---|
| **institutional** | *this paper believes · the paper's view* |
| **impersonal** | *one might say · it is widely held · the general view* |
| **personal** | *speaking personally · the present writer · as a reader* |
| **generic** | *we all know* — the most expansive and least checkable |

> **Why it matters.** Every piece of persuasive writing places the writer somewhere relative to
> the reader, and the placement is done almost entirely by pronouns and perfects.

### 3D · Use — controlled

Complete with the most defensible form.

> The paper (1) ______ (say) this since 2019, and we (2) ______ (say) the opposite before that.
> By March we (3) ______ (hold) the position for six years.
> At the time of the 2017 piece the board (4) ______ (not yet discuss) it.
> One (5) ______ (might say) that nothing has changed, but the archive disagrees.

### 3E · Use — guided

Rewrite each one so that the claim is about continuity rather than a point, or the reverse.

1. We took this position in 2019.
2. We have always opposed the plan.
3. The paper had supported it until the inquiry.
4. We will hold this view for a decade by next spring.
5. The board decided in March.

### 3F · Use — open

**In pairs.** One of you writes an editorial sentence using *we*. The other asks, four times,
*who is the we here?* **Each answer must narrow the set.**

### 3G · Error autopsy

> ✗ *We have said this in 2017.*
> ✗ *The paper has been believing this since 1911.*
> ✗ *By the time we published, we have already corrected it.*

For each, name the rule and say what the writer was reaching for.

### 3H · Check — eight items

> 1. We ______ (say) this since 2019. *(continuity to now)*
> 2. We ______ (say) the opposite in 2017. *(a point, dated)*
> 3. The board ______ (not discuss) it before the piece ran. *(earlier than a past point)*
> 4. By March we ______ (hold) it for six years. *(complete by a future point)*
> 5. ✗ *We have said this in 2017.* → correct it.
> 6. ✗ *The paper has been believing this since 1911.* → correct it.
> 7. Rewrite *We have always said this* so that it can be checked.
> 8. Rewrite *This paper believes* so that a named person is responsible.

## Part 4 · Speaking ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

### 4A · Fluency starter — narrow the *we*

One person makes a claim with *we*. Everybody else asks *who is the we here?* until the speaker
names a number of people. **Nobody may answer with another pronoun.**

### 4B · The functional core — positioning yourself in a text


![Two newspaper editorials set side by side, one from 2017 supporting a plan and one from today claiming the paper has always opposed it.](figures/fig_b21_u02_p04_v04.png){width=6.6in}

*Two editorials, six years apart — The same page, the same institution, and no name on either of them.*


**Language Bank**

| | |
|---|---|
| **the institution** | *this paper believes · the paper's view · on behalf of* |
| **the hedge** | *it is widely held · the general view · one might say* |
| **the person** | *speaking personally · the present writer · as a reader* |
| **the challenge** | *who is the we here? · speak for* |

**Information gap.** Learner A has the 2017 editorial. Learner B has today's draft.
**Agree on a sentence that both archives can live with.**

### 4C · The performance — three minutes ⏺

Argue a position you hold, in three minutes, **changing your position in the text three times**:
once as an institution, once impersonally, and once as yourself. Your partner names each shift as
it happens.

**Record it.**

### 4D · Observer

One person marks every *we* and writes, beside each, the number of people it includes.

### 4E · Three criteria

> ☐ Did I make all three positions, and were they audible?
> ☐ Did every perfect carry a claim about relevance now, not just about the past?
> ☐ Could my partner name the set behind each *we*?

## Part 5 · Reading ◆ **A · THE WORLD**

### 5A · Predict

The title is **"The ward that is always in the paper."**
**Who do you expect to be quoted in a story about a neighbourhood, and who usually is?**

### 5B · Read — two minutes

---

**The ward that is always in the paper**

Ward 7 has appeared in the Herald eighty-one times in two years. The paper has a bureau four
kilometres away and has never had a correspondent who lives there.

Of the eighty-one pieces, sixty-two quote a police source, forty-one quote a city councillor,
and nine quote somebody who lives in the ward and is not an official. Two of those nine are the
same person, who runs a shop on the main street and has now been quoted in four pieces, in each
of which he is described as a local resident.

The paper's editorials have said, four times in those two years, that the ward has been
neglected. They have not said by whom.

Last month a reporter spent six days in Ward 7 and filed nine hundred words. The readership
figures for that piece were the lowest of the month. The eighty other pieces, filed from the
bureau in an afternoon, did better.

Nobody at the paper thinks that is an argument. Everybody at the paper knows what it will be
used as.

---

### 5C · Comprehension — eight questions

1. How many times has Ward 7 appeared?
2. How far away is the bureau?
3. How many pieces quote a resident who is not an official?
4. How many of those nine quote the same person?
5. What have the editorials said four times?
6. **Why does the writer point out that the editorials "have not said by whom"?**
7. **What is the function of the perfect in "has now been quoted in four pieces"?**
8. **The last two sentences separate what people think from what will happen. Why end there?**

### 5D · Vocabulary in context

Infer from the text, then check.

> a bureau four kilometres away · described as a local resident · neglected · filed from the
> bureau · what it will be used as

### 5E · The counter-text


![Two editorials set out with every first-person plural pronoun marked and the set each one claims, together with the perfects in each and what they import.](figures/fig_b21_u02_p05_v05.png){width=6.6in}

*Two leaders, every pronoun marked — Six years apart, on the same page, about the same plan.*


**Read the two editorials — 2017 and today — and answer.**

1. What position does each take?
2. **Find every *we* in both and say what set each one includes.**
3. How many perfects are in each, and what does each perfect import?
4. **Neither editorial has a byline. What would change if one did?**

### 5F · Two texts

The 2017 editorial says *the plan deserves a hearing*.
Today's says *we have always opposed it*.

**Is the second sentence false?** Write two sentences, and use the word *continuity* in one of
them.

## Part 6 · Vocabulary Lab 2 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### Who is included, and who is being spoken about

### 6A · Sort — includes the reader, excludes the reader, or avoids the question?

Three columns.

> institutional · exclusive · collective · inclusive · generic · anonymous · impersonal ·
> corporate

### 6B · Chunk completion

1. **the ______ we** — the plural used by a single person with authority
2. **one ______ say** — the most distancing construction in formal English
3. **it is widely ______** — a claim with the holders removed
4. **the present ______** — the writer, referring to the writer, in the third person
5. **speaking ______** — the writer stepping out from behind the institution
6. **the general ______** — a consensus nobody has measured

### 6C · Three that are not interchangeable

> *in this paper* · *as a reader* · *the general view*

**Say which one claims institutional authority, which claims none, and which claims a consensus.**

### 6D · The phrasal verbs and the fixed expressions

> **own up** · **take over** · **sign off**
>
> **f** *speaking for myself* · **f** *we take the view that* · **f** *that is not our position*

**One of these fixed expressions can only be said by somebody with authority.** Which, and how do
you know?

### 6E · Contrast Clinic

Eight items. Rewrite each sentence so that responsibility moves to the place in brackets.

1. The paper regrets the error. *(to the desk that made it)*
2. One might say the figures are unreliable. *(to a named analyst)*
3. The community affected was consulted. *(to the three people who were asked)*
4. It is widely held that the plan will fail. *(to the writer)*
5. Mistakes were made. *(to a named person)*
6. We have always opposed it. *(to a dated decision)*
7. This paper believes in transparency. *(to an editorial board, with a date)*
8. Our readers expect better. *(to a survey, with a number)*

## Part 7 · Pronunciation & Fluency Lab ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**


![One editorial sentence drawn four times as beats with a different word stressed in each, and the implied contrast beside it.](figures/fig_b21_u02_p07_v10.png){width=6.6in}

*A stressed pronoun is a contrast — The same sentence, four times, and each stress names somebody who is not in the room.*


### A stressed pronoun is a contrast

### 7A · Hear 🔊 Track 2.3

> *WE have always said this.* — somebody else has not
> *We have ALWAYS said this.* — the continuity is the claim
> *We have always said THIS.* — not the other thing

### 7B · Say

Say each one three times. Then say this:

> *I didn't say it. WE said it.* — the stress moves responsibility from a person to a body, and
> that is the whole function of the editorial *we*.

### 7C · Make it mean something

Say these two:

> *We take the view that…* — unstressed *we*: the institution, routinely
> *WE take the view that…* — stressed: and somebody else takes a different one

**An unstressed pronoun is a reference. A stressed one is a contrast,** and the contrast always
implies a party who is not in the room.

### 7D · Fluency 30 ⏺

Thirty seconds: **one position, stated four times — as the institution, impersonally, as
yourself, and as a reader.** Three times, faster.

---

## Part 8 · Writing ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

### A position, with its holders named · 200 words

### 8A · Read the model

> This paper opposed the Ward 7 plan in a leader on 14 March 2019. `[the institution, with a
> date — the perfect would have hidden it]`
>
> We had supported an earlier version of it in 2017, and we were wrong about the costs.
> `[the past perfect places the earlier position, and the admission is in the same sentence]`
>
> The present writer was not on the board in 2017 and has read the file. `[the person,
> distinguished from the institution, and a perfect doing proper work]`
>
> It is widely held in this building that the ward has been neglected. By whom is a question we
> have not answered, and by March we will have been asking it for six years. `[the impersonal,
> the challenge to it, and a future perfect that dates the failure]`

**Questions about the writer's choices:**

1. The first sentence uses a simple past with a date. **What would *we have always opposed* have cost?**
2. *The present writer* appears once. **Why not *I*?**
3. The last sentence turns the paper's own phrase against it. **Which word does the work?**

### 8B · The anti-model

> We have always believed that Ward 7 deserves better. Our readers expect this paper to speak for the communities it serves. Mistakes were made in the past but we have learnt from them. This paper believes in accountability.

Four sentences, four unexaminable claims, and not one date or person in any of them.
**Rewrite it so that every claim has either a date or a name attached.**

### 8C · Plan

> the institution, with a date → the earlier position, placed → the person, separated →
> the impersonal claim, and the challenge to it

### 8D · Write — 200 words
### 8E · Peer review — three criteria

> ☐ Does every *we* have a number behind it that the writer could state?
> ☐ Does every perfect import something into the present argument, or is it hiding a date?
> ☐ Is there at least one sentence where the writer appears as a person?

### 8F · Write it again · 8G · Publish

Rewrite after review. **Publish both, and mark every pronoun in both.**

## Part 9 · The File ◆ **A · THE WORLD + C · THE PERFORMANCE**


![A table of four first-person-plural claims learners make about their own English with the set each one imports, the evidence behind it, and what it licenses.](figures/fig_b21_u02_p09_v05.png){width=6.6in}

*Four sentences a learner says with we — Each imports a group. Each borrows that group's excuse.*


# STUDY FILE · Whose standard are you measuring against?

### 9A · The *we* in your own account 🔊 Track 2.4

Learners describe their English in the first person plural more often than they notice: *in my
country we don't study speaking*, *we were never taught this*, *we all have this problem*. Each
one imports a group and borrows its excuse.

| | |
|---|---|
| **the claim** | *we were never taught to speak* |
| **the set** | about forty million people |
| **the evidence** | one school, 2009 to 2015 |
| **what it licenses** | not speaking, indefinitely |

### 9B · Narrow it

Take three sentences you have said about your English that begin with *we* or *in my country*.
**Rewrite each with the smallest honest set and a date.**

### 9C · Role play — who is the we here?

In pairs. One of you describes a difficulty using *we*. The other may only ask *who is the we
here?* and *since when?* **Four rounds each.**

### 9D · The inherited position

Everybody has at least one belief about language learning they did not arrive at themselves.
**Name yours and name who gave it to you.** Then decide whether you still hold it.

### 9E · Transfer — before next week

**Write the sentence *I have always…* about your English, and then find the earliest evidence.**
Bring both. Most people find the *always* is about four years old.

## Part 10 · Mediation & Interaction ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**


![An infographic of two years of coverage of one ward showing who was quoted, where the reporters were based, the readership of each kind of piece, and what the editorials claimed.](figures/fig_b21_u02_p10_v12.png){width=6.6in}

*Ward 7, two years, eighty-one pieces — Who is quoted, who writes, and who the editorials say the paper speaks for.*


### 10A · Relay

Half the class has the archive. Half has the draft.
**Produce one sentence both can sign, by speaking only.** Ten minutes.

### 10B · Simplify — for one reader

A reader writes: *You say you have always opposed this. I have the 2017 paper in front of me.*
**Reply in four sentences.** Do not use the perfect.

### 10C · Online

Write **one message** to a colleague about a *we* in their draft that is doing too much work.
**Three sentences.** Name the set it is claiming.

### 10D · Your language

Does your language distinguish an inclusive *we* from an exclusive one?
**Translate *we have always said this* both ways.** Which does the English sentence mean?

## Part 11 · The Decision ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

# The byline on the leader page

The Herald's editorials have been unsigned since 1911.

**Option one:** keep it. An unsigned leader is the paper speaking, which is the point of a leader
page, and a byline makes it a column.

**Option two:** sign them. The person who wrote it is named, as everywhere else in the paper, and
*we have always said* becomes harder to write.

**Option three:** keep them unsigned and publish, once a year, the names of everybody who sat on
the board and the dates of every position change.

### 11A · The evidence


![A bar chart comparing signed columns and unsigned leaders on corrections issued, corrections unprompted, replies to readers and position reversals acknowledged.](figures/fig_b21_u02_p11_v06.png){width=6.6in}

*Signed and unsigned, and what happens afterwards — Five years of the Herald, comparing signed columns with unsigned leaders on the same four measures.*


🔊 **Track 2.6.** The columnist, 30 seconds: *"I have written under my own name for nineteen
years and I have been wrong in public about four things. The leader page has been wrong about
rather more and has never apologised, because there is nobody there to apologise. That is not a
bug in the system. It is the system."*

### 11B · Talk about it

In threes. Use the frame, and state the set behind every *we* you use.

> *I would ______ , because ______ , and the people affected are ______ .*

### 11C · Decide

Write **three sentences**: the decision, the evidence, and who would be accountable under it.

### 11D · Model decision

> Option three. Signing the leader turns it into a column and loses the one thing a leader page
> can do, which is commit an institution. Publishing the board and the dates keeps the
> institutional voice and makes *we have always said* checkable — which is the only part of the
> current arrangement that is doing damage.

### 11E · The Reveal

> **What happened.** The Herald published the board list and the position changes. The leader page
> stayed unsigned.
>
> Then something nobody predicted: the phrase *we have always said* disappeared from the leader
> page within four months, without any instruction. Writers who knew a reader could check the
> date simply stopped reaching for the construction.

**One question. You do not have to answer it out loud.**

> Nobody banned the phrase. What did the list of dates actually change?

---

## Part 12 · Landing ◆ **all three lines**

### 12A · Recycle — fifteen items

> 1. We ______ (say) this since 2019. *(continuity to now)*
> 2. We ______ (say) the opposite in 2017. *(a dated point)*
> 3. The board ______ (not discuss) it before the piece ran.
> 4. By March we ______ (hold) it for six years.
> 5. The ______ we — a single person using a plural with authority
> 6. ______ might say that nothing has changed.
> 7. It is widely ______ that the ward has been neglected.
> 8. The present ______ was not on the board in 2017.
> 9. ______ personally, I disagree.
> 10. This paper ______ in accountability.
> 11. ______ ______ ______ ? *(four words — the challenge)*
> 12. That is not ______ ______ . *(two words — the institutional refusal)*
> 13. She was asked to ______ ______ for the editor on Friday. *(two words)*
> 14. Somebody has to ______ ______ to the error. *(two words)*
> 15. The piece was ______ ______ overnight by a stringer. *(two words)*

### 12B · Consolidate — one task, everything in it

**Write 150 words taking a position**, in which you appear once as an institution, once
impersonally and once as yourself. Mark the three in the margin.

### 12C · Can-do, with evidence

> ☐ I can use the perfect to import a past event into a present argument. **Evidence:** 3H, 8D
> ☐ I can place myself in a text at three distances. **Evidence:** 4C
> ☐ I can say what set a *we* includes. **Evidence:** 6E, 9C
> ☐ I can hear and use contrastive pronoun stress. **Evidence:** 7C

### 12D · Replay ⏺

Record 0C again. **Compare.** How many of your inherited opinions did you name the first time?

### 12E · Glossary — fifty items, as a map

> **THE PEOPLE** · columnist · correspondent · stringer · voice
> **THE INSTITUTION** · byline · masthead · editorial · outlet · bureau · syndication
> **THE READERS** · readership · audience · constituency
> **THE CLAIMS** · speak for · on behalf of · our readers · the paper's view · the community affected
> **THE DESCRIPTIONS** · a named writer · an unsigned piece · local to the story · parachute journalism · whose paper is it
> **WHAT PEOPLE DO** · speak up · stand in · write up · own up · take over · sign off
> **IN THE ROOM** · we have always said · this paper believes · who is the we here?
> **THE REFERENCE** · inclusive · exclusive · collective · impersonal · generic · institutional · corporate · anonymous
> **THE FORMULAE** · the royal we · one might say · it is widely held · the present writer · in this paper · speaking personally · as a reader · the general view
> **POSITIONING YOURSELF** · speaking for myself · we take the view that · that is not our position

### 12F · Next

> **Unit 3 · How sure are you?**



# B2.1 · Unit 3 · A Doubling of Risk

> **English Animated** · *Reading Between Lines* · a newsroom, Toronto
> Student's Book pages 50–71 · 22 pp · ~11 hours

## Part 0 · The Big Picture ◆ **A · THE WORLD**


![The health desk of a newspaper with a printed press release, a preprint on screen, a whiteboard carrying a base rate, a phone on speaker and a headline draft on a sticky note.](figures/fig_b21_u03_p00_v01.png){width=6.6in}

*The health desk, twenty past four — Twelve things to find. Two of them carry the same number and one says what it is out of.*


# How sure are you?

**By the end of this unit you can set your certainty exactly where the evidence puts it, and
defend the setting. Two hundred words.**

| ◆ **A · THE WORLD** | A press release that says *doubles*, a preprint that says *four in ten thousand*, and a headline due at six |
|---|---|
| ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM** | the full epistemic range of modals · 28 words for risk and evidence · 22 words and phrases for calibrating certainty · stress on a modal |
| ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE** | Two hundred words in which every claim is pitched at its evidence |

---

### 0A · Find it

**Find twelve things in this picture.** Two of them contain the same number and only one of them
says what it is out of.

> the printed press release · the preprint on the screen · the whiteboard with the base rate on it ·
> the phone on speaker to an epidemiologist · the headline draft on a sticky note ·
> the health desk's correction log · a calculator · a chart with a shaded band on it ·
> the style guide open at RISK · the clock showing 16:40 · the first edition deadline on the wall ·
> a mug with a university logo

### 0B · Guess it

**One object in this picture would change the headline if anybody read it.** Which one, and why
has nobody opened it?

### 0C · Say it ⏺

**Record sixty seconds.** *Describe something about your own future that you are sure of, and
something you are only fairly sure of. Make the difference audible.* You will record it again at
the end of this unit.

## Part 1 · Vocabulary Lab 1 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**


![A cutaway of a risk claim in four layers, from the data through the analysis and the statement of uncertainty to the headline, where none of the technical words survives.](figures/fig_b21_u03_p01_v02.png){width=6.6in}

*One number, in section — Twelve words, and the layer each one lives on. Count how many reach the top.*


### 1A · Meet — the words under a number

Match each word to one layer of the cutaway.

> **risk** · **hazard** · **exposure** · **incidence** · **prevalence** · **baseline** ·
> **confidence** · **interval** · **significance** · **correlation** · **causation** · **cohort**

### 1B · Sort — counted in people, a relation between numbers, or a measure of doubt?

Three columns. **Two of the twelve are swapped for one another in print almost daily, and that
swap is the subject of this unit.**

> confidence · risk · hazard · incidence · prevalence · significance · baseline ·
> exposure · correlation · interval · causation · cohort

### 1C · Meet — the five phrases a risk story turns on

| | |
|---|---|
| **absolute risk** | How many people out of how many. The only figure a reader can act on. |
| **relative risk** | One risk divided by another. Large, dramatic, and meaningless alone. |
| **a doubling of risk** | Two in a million, or four in ten. The phrase does not distinguish them. |
| **the base rate** | What the risk was before anything happened. Almost never printed. |
| **statistically significant** | Unlikely to be chance. Not a synonym for large, important or true. |

### 1D · Collocation Lab

Complete each one, and say which of the five has been checked by somebody with no stake in it.

> **a causal link** · **the margin of error** · **peer reviewed** · **a preprint** · **a press release**

1. The university's communications office sent ______ ______ ______ at nine this morning.
2. The study itself is ______ ______ , posted online and not yet assessed by anybody.
3. Nothing in it has been ______ ______ , which the release mentions in the last line.
4. The authors are careful never to claim ______ ______ ______ ; the release is not.
5. The estimate carries ______ ______ ______ ______ wide enough to include almost no effect at all.

### 1E · The phrasal verbs, and the three fixed expressions

> **scale up** · **rule out** · **bear in mind**
>
> **f** *the evidence does not support that* · **f** *it may well be* · **f** *we cannot rule it out*

**Two of these fixed expressions leave a door open and one closes it.** Which closes it, and which
of the two open doors is doing the more honest work?

### 1F · Own it

Write one sentence about your own health, work or study that contains a number, and then write
underneath it what that number is out of.

---

## Part 2 · Listening ◆ **A · THE WORLD**


![A health reporter and a news editor at a desk with a printed press release between them, each with two speech balloons and a thought balloon.](figures/fig_b21_u03_p02_v07.png){width=6.6in}

*The four o'clock conference — She has the preprint. He has a release, a deadline and a space for six words.*


### 2A · The four o'clock conference 🔊 Track 3.1

**Before you listen.** A press release says night shifts double the risk of a rare heart-rhythm
condition. The health reporter has read the preprint. The news editor has forty minutes.

**Listen once.** What does the study show, and what does the release say it shows?

**Listen again. Complete the table.**

| | The number | What it is out of | How sure the authors are |
|---|---|---|---|
| the release | | | |
| the preprint | | | |
| the epidemiologist | | | |

**Listen again. True or false?**

1. The reporter has spoken to one of the authors. ☐ T ☐ F
2. The release gives the base rate. ☐ T ☐ F
3. The confidence interval runs from 1.2 to 3.3. ☐ T ☐ F
4. The editor wants the word *doubles* kept. ☐ T ☐ F

**Noticing.** Five sentences from the recording.

1. *It must be a real effect.*
2. *It is likely to be a real effect.*
3. *It may well be a real effect.*
4. *It could hardly have been chance, at that interval.*
5. *We cannot rule it out.*

**Five settings on one scale, about one finding. Which of the five could the paper defend in
court, and which could it defend only in the bar afterwards?**

### 2B · Decoding clinic 🔊 Track 3.2

Thirty seconds at speed. Three jobs.

1. The epidemiologist says *must have* four times. Write what you hear, not what is written.
2. One modal is stressed. Which, and what is the speaker contrasting it with?
3. The reporter says *that is overstating it* once. What exactly is she rejecting?

## Part 3 · Grammar Lab 1 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### A modal is a promise about how sure you are

### 3A · Notice

Look at the five sentences in Part 2 again.

1. Which one commits the speaker most, and what evidence would it need?
2. Which one commits the speaker least while still asserting something?
3. Sentence 4 is about the past. How does the form show it?
4. Sentence 5 says nothing about how probable the effect is. **What does it say instead?**

### 3B · See


![A vertical ladder of epistemic modals from must and is bound to at the top, through will, should, may and might, to cannot at the bottom, each with the commitment it makes.](figures/fig_b21_u03_p03_v09.png){width=6.6in}

*The certainty ladder — Six settings, one finding. Add a perfect to any rung and the deduction moves to the past: must have been, might well have been, could hardly have been.*


### 3C · Know

| | | |
|---|---|---|
| **must · is bound to** | the strongest inference short of proof | *It must be a real effect.* |
| **will · is likely to** | confident, and routine in forecasting | *That is likely to be the explanation.* |
| **should** | expected, with room to be wrong | *The replication should settle it.* |
| **may · might · could** | possible; no commitment at all | *It may well be chance.* |
| **can't · could hardly have** | excluded by the evidence | *It could hardly have been chance.* |

> **The epistemic modals are a scale, and the scale is public.** A reader who sees *must* and
> later learns the interval ran from 1.2 to 3.3 has caught the writer overclaiming, and will not
> forget it.

> **Deduction about the past takes the perfect.** *must have been*, *might well have been*,
> *could hardly have been*. The modal sets the certainty; the perfect sets the time. The two
> choices are independent, which is why every combination exists.

| | |
|---|---|
| **adverbs run the same scale** | *conceivably · arguably · presumably · demonstrably · unequivocally* |
| **and some of them do not** | *ostensibly · allegedly · reportedly* — these report somebody else's certainty, not yours |
| **the passive reporting frame** | *is said to · is reported to · has yet to be confirmed* |
| **the balanced statement** | *may or may not* — which is a refusal to place the claim at all |

> **Why it matters.** Every sentence about the future, about a cause, or about somebody else's
> data is a bet, and the modal is the size of the bet. Writers who always bet large are read once.

### 3D · Use — controlled

Complete with the modal that the evidence in brackets will support.

> The finding (1) ______ be chance, given the interval. *(it cannot be excluded)*
> At that cohort size the effect (2) ______ be real. *(as sure as the data allows)*
> The authors (3) ______ have seen the release before it went out. *(the evidence suggests not)*
> A replication (4) ______ settle it within two years. *(expected, with room)*
> Whether it applies to day workers (5) ______ or ______ ______ be the same question. *(explicitly balanced)*

### 3E · Use — guided

Rewrite each one so that the certainty matches the evidence in brackets.

1. Night shifts cause the condition. *(one cohort, not peer reviewed)*
2. The release was written by somebody who had not read the paper. *(a guess, past)*
3. The effect is chance. *(excluded by the interval)*
4. The paper will replicate. *(a hope, not a forecast)*
5. The authors disagree with the release. *(you have not asked them)*

### 3F · Use — open

**In pairs.** One of you states a claim at the top of the scale. The other asks *what would you
need to have seen?* **Keep going until the claim comes down to where the evidence actually is.**

### 3G · Error autopsy

> ✗ *It must to be a real effect.*
> ✗ *It might be chance last year, we cannot say.*
> ✗ *The evidence doesn't support that it is demonstrably possible.*

For each, name the rule and say what the writer was reaching for.

### 3H · Check — eight items

> 1. It ______ be chance; the interval includes 1.2. *(possible, uncommitted)*
> 2. At that cohort size it ______ be real. *(strongest inference)*
> 3. They ______ ______ seen the release first. *(past, strong inference, negative evidence)*
> 4. It ______ ______ ______ been chance at that interval. *(past, all but excluded)*
> 5. It ______ ______ ______ be the same in day workers. *(explicitly balanced, five words)*
> 6. ✗ *It must to be a real effect.* → correct it.
> 7. Rewrite *Night shifts cause it* so that it survives peer review.
> 8. Rewrite *We cannot rule it out* so that a reader can tell how likely it is.

## Part 4 · Speaking ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

### 4A · Fluency starter — move one notch

One person makes a claim. The next person repeats it **one notch weaker**, the next one notch
weaker again, until nothing is left. Then climb back up. **No notch may be skipped.**

### 4B · The functional core — calibrating certainty


![Two accounts of the same finding side by side, one from a university press release and one from the preprint it describes.](figures/fig_b21_u03_p04_v04.png){width=6.6in}

*The same study, written twice — One release, one abstract, one figure between them.*


**Language Bank**

| | |
|---|---|
| **committing** | *demonstrably · unequivocally · is bound to · must surely be* |
| **hedging** | *conceivably · arguably · presumably · may or may not* |
| **sourcing it elsewhere** | *ostensibly · allegedly · reportedly · is said to · has yet to be* |
| **refusing the frame** | *that is overstating it · the evidence does not support that* |

**Information gap.** Learner A has the press release. Learner B has the preprint's own abstract.
**Agree on one sentence that both documents can support.**

### 4C · The performance — three minutes ⏺

Present the finding to a room, in three minutes, **stating your certainty three times at three
different settings**: once at the top of the scale about something you can prove, once in the
middle, and once at the bottom. Your partner writes down the evidence each setting would need.

**Record it.**

### 4D · Observer

One person marks every modal and every adverb of certainty, and writes beside each one the
evidence it is claiming.

### 4E · Three criteria

> ☐ Was every claim pitched where its evidence actually is?
> ☐ Did I use at least one past-deduction form, with a perfect?
> ☐ Could my partner tell, by ear, which claims I would defend?

---

## Part 5 · Reading ◆ **A · THE WORLD**

### 5A · Predict

The title is **"Four in ten thousand."**
**What do you expect the headline on this story to have said instead, and why?**

### 5B · Read — two minutes

---

**Four in ten thousand**

The release arrived at nine. Night work, it said, doubles the risk of a rare heart-rhythm
condition, in a cohort of sixty-one thousand Danish adults followed for nine years.

The preprint says something narrower. In the group that worked no night shifts, the incidence was
four cases per ten thousand people per year. In the group working four or more nights a week it
was eight. That is a doubling of risk, and it is also four extra people in ten thousand.

The confidence interval runs from 1.2 to 3.3, which means the study is consistent with an effect
of almost nothing and with an effect three times larger than the one reported. The result is
statistically significant, just. The authors say, twice, that they cannot establish a causal link
and that the cohort was not designed to.

The release says *doubles* three times. It does not give the base rate anywhere in three hundred
and ten words.

By four o'clock the health reporter had the epidemiologist on the phone. "It may well be real,"
she said. "We cannot rule it out, and we cannot scale it up to the whole workforce either. Bear in
mind what it is out of."

The first edition closes at six. The desk has two numbers, both true, and room for one of them in
a headline.

---

### 5C · Comprehension — eight questions

1. How many people were in the cohort, and for how long?
2. What was the incidence in the group that worked no night shifts?
3. What does the confidence interval run from and to?
4. How many times does the release use the word *doubles*?
5. What do the authors say twice?
6. **Why does the writer give the length of the release in words?**
7. **"Both true" — what is the difference between the two numbers, if neither is wrong?**
8. **The last sentence says *room for one of them*. What is the real constraint being described?**

### 5D · Vocabulary in context

Infer from the text, then check.

> consistent with an effect of almost nothing · just · designed to · what it is out of ·
> room for one of them

### 5E · The counter-text


![A university press release annotated to show every word that sets a level of certainty, what each one claims, and the figures the release does not contain.](figures/fig_b21_u03_p05_v05.png){width=6.6in}

*The release, every certainty word marked — Three hundred and ten words, and the only number in it is a ratio.*


**Read the press release and answer.**

1. Where in the release does the base rate appear?
2. **Find every word in it that sets a level of certainty, and say what evidence each one claims.**
3. The release and the preprint use the same figure. **What does each one leave out?**
4. **Who wrote the release, and what were they measured on?**

### 5F · Two texts

The release says *doubles the risk*.
The preprint says *an increase from four to eight cases per ten thousand person-years*.

**Is the release false?** Write two sentences, and use the phrase *the base rate* in one of them.

## Part 6 · Vocabulary Lab 2 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### Saying how sure you are, in one word

### 6A · Sort — the writer is unsure, somebody else said it, or the writer is committed?

Three columns.

> arguably · demonstrably · conceivably · unequivocally · reportedly · allegedly ·
> ostensibly · presumably

### 6B · Chunk completion

1. The replication ______ ______ ______ take two years. *(three words — expected, confident)*
2. At that sample size the result ______ ______ ______ hold. *(three words — as sure as you can be)*
3. They ______ ______ ______ read the preprint before writing the release. *(three words — past, strong)*
4. It ______ ______ ______ been chance at that interval. *(four words — past, all but excluded)*
5. There ______ ______ ______ some effect, on these numbers. *(three words — strong inference)*
6. Day workers ______ ______ ______ ______ show the same pattern. *(five words — balanced)*
7. The release ______ ______ ______ have been approved by the authors. *(three words — reported, unverified)*
8. The finding ______ ______ ______ ______ replicated. *(four words — not yet, and that matters)*

### 6C · Three that are not interchangeable

> *is said to* · *is likely to* · *has yet to be*

**Say which one gives a probability, which one gives a source, and which one gives a date that has
not arrived.**

### 6D · The phrasal verbs and the fixed expressions

> **hedge about** · **come down on** · **hold back**
>
> **f** *on the balance of probability* · **f** *to the best of our knowledge* ·
> **f** *that is overstating it*

**One of these three fixed expressions is a legal standard, one is an institutional limit and one
is a correction.** Match them, and say which one a scientist would use about their own work.

### 6E · Contrast Clinic

Eight items. Rewrite each sentence so that the certainty moves to the level in brackets.

1. Night shifts cause the condition. *(one cohort, no causal claim possible)*
2. Perhaps the release was written by the press office. *(strong inference about the past)*
3. Everybody knows night work is dangerous. *(what you can actually demonstrate)*
4. The effect is chance. *(all but excluded, past form)*
5. The study is wrong. *(you have read the abstract only)*
6. There is no effect. *(the interval includes both almost nothing and a large effect)*
7. It will be replicated next year. *(a hope with no evidence)*
8. The authors approved the release. *(you have been told, by one person)*

## Part 7 · Pronunciation & Fluency Lab ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**


![Five perfect modals written out and then transcribed as they are said, with have reduced to a schwa and a v in each one.](figures/fig_b21_u03_p07_v10.png){width=6.6in}

*The modal keeps its stress and loses its have — Written in full above, said at half the length below. The shaded letters are not heard.*


### A modal you stress is a modal you are defending

### 7A · Hear 🔊 Track 3.3

> *It must have been chance.* → /ɪt ˈmʌstəv bɪn ˈtʃɑːns/ — *have* has gone entirely
> *They might have read it.* → /ðeɪ ˈmaɪtəv ˈred ɪt/
> *It could hardly have been real.* → /ɪt kəd ˈhɑːdli əv bɪn ˈrɪəl/

### 7B · Say

Say each one three times, fast. Then say this:

> *They might have read it, they should have read it, and they could have asked.* — three
> perfect modals in one breath, and not one full *have* among them.

### 7C · Make it mean something

Say these two:

> *It might be real.* — unstressed modal: the ordinary, uncommitted statement
> *It MIGHT be real.* — stressed: and somebody in the room has just said it definitely is

**An unstressed modal reports your certainty. A stressed one disputes somebody else's,** which is
why a stressed modal in a meeting is never neutral.

### 7D · Fluency 30 ⏺

Thirty seconds: **one finding, described at four settings on the scale, from excluded to certain**,
with the modal reduced every time it carries a perfect. Three times, faster.
