## Part 8 · Writing ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

### A risk story, with every claim at its evidence · 200 words

### 8A · Read the model

> A Danish cohort of sixty-one thousand adults, followed for nine years, reports four extra cases
> of a rare heart-rhythm condition per ten thousand night workers per year. `[the absolute figure
> first, with what it is out of]`
>
> That is a doubling of the baseline, which was four in ten thousand. `[the relative figure
> second, and it is now interpretable]`
>
> The result is statistically significant, but the confidence interval runs from 1.2 to 3.3, so
> the effect may well be very small. `[the modal is set at the interval, not at the headline]`
>
> The authors could hardly have been clearer that they cannot show a causal link; the university's
> press release, which has yet to be corrected, says *doubles* three times and gives no base rate.
> `[past deduction with a perfect, then a reporting frame that places the blame precisely]`

**Questions about the writer's choices:**

1. The absolute figure comes first. **What would the reader have done with the relative one alone?**
2. *May well be* appears once. **Why not *is*?**
3. The last sentence does not accuse anybody of lying. **What does it do instead, and how?**

### 8B · The anti-model

> Night work doubles your risk of a serious heart condition, a major new study has found. Experts say the findings are highly significant and could have implications for millions of workers. The research, which has yet to be published, is said to be the largest of its kind.

Three sentences, one real number hidden inside a ratio, and *significant* used in the wrong sense.
**Rewrite it so that a reader could decide what to do on Monday.**

### 8C · Plan

> the absolute figure, with its base rate → the ratio, now interpretable → the uncertainty, with
> the modal set at it → who said what, and what has yet to be done

### 8D · Write — 200 words
### 8E · Peer review — three criteria

> ☐ Does every number say what it is out of?
> ☐ Is each modal set where the interval puts it, not where the story would like it?
> ☐ Is every claim you did not verify attributed to whoever made it?

### 8F · Write it again · 8G · Publish

Rewrite after review. **Publish both, and underline every modal in each.**

---

## Part 9 · The File ◆ **A · THE WORLD + C · THE PERFORMANCE**


![A study sheet setting out four claims learners make about their own English, the evidence behind each, the setting the evidence supports and what the strong form licenses.](figures/fig_b21_u03_p09_v05.png){width=6.6in}

*Four claims a learner makes about themselves — Each one is a hypothesis stated as a result, and a result is not tested again.*


# STUDY FILE · The certainty in your own account

### 9A · Claims you make about yourself 🔊 Track 3.4

Learners describe their own ability at the very top of the certainty scale and almost never at the
middle: *I can't hear the difference*, *I will never pronounce this*, *my grammar is terrible*.
Each one is a hypothesis stated as a result, and a hypothesis stated as a result stops being
tested.

| | |
|---|---|
| **the claim** | *I can't hear the difference* |
| **the evidence** | four attempts, all in week one, none recorded |
| **the honest setting** | *I could not hear it in October* |
| **what the strong form licenses** | never trying again |

### 9B · Lower the setting

Take three sentences you have said about your English at the top of the scale.
**Rewrite each at the setting its evidence supports, with a date.**

### 9C · Role play — what would you need to have seen?

In pairs. One of you states a claim about your own English. The other may only ask *what would you
need to have seen to be that sure?* **Four rounds each.**

### 9D · The thing you are genuinely sure of

Everybody has one thing about their English they can demonstrate.
**Name yours and name the evidence.** Then say it at the top of the scale, out loud, once.

### 9E · Transfer — before next week

**Take one claim you made at the top of the scale and test it properly.** Three attempts,
recorded, one week apart. Bring the recordings and the original sentence.

---

## Part 10 · Mediation & Interaction ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**


![An infographic showing the baseline incidence, the incidence among night workers, the confidence interval the study cannot exclude, and what the press release printed.](figures/fig_b21_u03_p10_v12.png){width=6.6in}

*Four in ten thousand, and what became of it — The whole study on one scale: cases per ten thousand people per year.*


### 10A · Relay

Half the class has the release. Half has the preprint.
**Produce one headline both documents support, by speaking only.** Ten minutes.

### 10B · Simplify — for one reader

A reader writes: *I work nights. Should I stop?*
**Reply in four sentences.** Give the absolute figures and set your modals honestly.

### 10C · Online

Write **one message** to the press office about the missing base rate. **Three sentences.** Do not
accuse; describe what a reader cannot do without it.

### 10D · Your language

How does your language mark the difference between *it must be* and *it may be*?
**Translate all five sentences from Part 2.** Which distinctions survive, and which collapse?

## Part 11 · The Decision ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

# The headline at six o'clock

Two numbers, both true, and room for one.

**Option one:** *Night work doubles heart risk, study finds.* True, accurate to the release, and
the number readers will remember.

**Option two:** *Night work linked to four extra cases in ten thousand.* True, accurate to the
preprint, and nobody will click on it.

**Option three:** run option one, and adopt a standing rule that no risk figure appears anywhere
in the paper without its base rate in the same sentence.

### 11A · The evidence


![A bar chart comparing four headline styles on the share of readers who estimated the absolute risk correctly and the share who opened the story.](figures/fig_b21_u03_p11_v06.png){width=6.6in}

*What two hundred readers understood, by headline style — One year of the Herald, a hundred and forty risk stories, four ways of writing the number.*


🔊 **Track 3.6.** The standards editor, 30 seconds: *"We have run a hundred and forty risk stories
in a year. I asked two hundred readers what a doubling meant, and the median answer was fifty per
cent. Not fifty per cent more — fifty per cent of people. We are not informing anybody. We are
producing a number that happens to be correct and a belief that is wildly wrong, and then
defending the number."*

### 11B · Talk about it

In threes. Use the frame, and state your certainty setting every time you use it.

> *I would ______ , because ______ , and the evidence for that is ______ .*

### 11C · Decide

Write **three sentences**: the decision, the evidence, and the thing you would accept as proof you
were wrong.

### 11D · Model decision

> Option three. Option two is honest and unread, which helps nobody, and option one on its own
> produces the fifty-per-cent belief the standards editor measured. The base-rate rule costs nine
> words a story and is the only version where the figure the reader remembers is a figure they can
> use. It also has a cost worth naming: some stories will not survive their own base rate, and
> those are the stories the rule exists for.

### 11E · The Reveal

> **What happened.** The Herald ran the first headline and adopted the rule. Nine months later the
> preprint failed peer review on the exposure definition and was withdrawn.
>
> Then something nobody predicted: the health reporter began returning press releases that gave no
> base rate, with a one-line note. Within a year, three university press offices in the city were
> putting the base rate in the first paragraph — not because anybody asked them to, but because
> the alternative was not being covered.

**One question. You do not have to answer it out loud.**

> The rule was about the Herald's own sentences. Why did it change what other people wrote?

---

## Part 12 · Landing ◆ **all three lines**

### 12A · Recycle — fifteen items

> 1. It ______ be chance; the interval includes 1.2. *(possible, uncommitted)*
> 2. At that size the effect ______ be real. *(strongest inference)*
> 3. It ______ ______ ______ been chance at that interval. *(past, all but excluded)*
> 4. Day workers ______ ______ ______ ______ show the pattern. *(five words, balanced)*
> 5. The finding ______ ______ ______ ______ replicated. *(four words, not yet)*
> 6. ______ , the release was written by somebody who had not read the paper. *(one word — a guess)*
> 7. The effect is not ______ large, only unlikely to be chance. *(one word)*
> 8. ______ ______ ______ was four cases in ten thousand. *(three words)*
> 9. The study is ______ ______ , posted online and not yet assessed. *(two words)*
> 10. ______ ______ ______ ______ ______ ______ , night work is the likelier explanation. *(six words — the legal standard)*
> 11. ______ ______ ______ ______ ______ ______ , no replication exists. *(six words — the institutional limit)*
> 12. ______ ______ ______ ______ ______ . *(five words — the correction you make to an overclaim)*
> 13. You cannot ______ ______ one cohort to a whole workforce. *(two words)*
> 14. Somebody has to ______ ______ ______ one side or the other by six. *(three words)*
> 15. He tends to ______ ______ when the numbers are thin. *(two words)*

### 12B · Consolidate — one task, everything in it

**Write 150 words about a claim in the news this week**, placing every assertion at the setting its
evidence supports. Mark three settings in the margin: one top, one middle, one bottom.

### 12C · Can-do, with evidence

> ☐ I can use the full range of epistemic modals, including past deduction. **Evidence:** 3H, 8D
> ☐ I can calibrate a claim to the evidence behind it. **Evidence:** 4C, 6E
> ☐ I can read a risk figure and say what it is out of. **Evidence:** 5C, 10B
> ☐ I can reduce and stress a modal for effect. **Evidence:** 7C

### 12D · Replay ⏺

Record 0C again. **Compare.** How many settings did you use the first time, and how many now?

### 12E · Glossary — fifty items, as a map

> **THE MEASUREMENTS** · incidence · prevalence · cohort · baseline · exposure
> **THE RELATIONS** · risk · hazard · correlation · causation
> **THE DOUBT** · confidence · interval · significance
> **THE FIVE PHRASES** · absolute risk · relative risk · a doubling of risk · the base rate · statistically significant
> **THE PAPER TRAIL** · a causal link · the margin of error · peer reviewed · a preprint · a press release
> **WHAT PEOPLE DO WITH EVIDENCE** · scale up · rule out · bear in mind · hedge about · come down on · hold back
> **IN THE ROOM** · the evidence does not support that · it may well be · we cannot rule it out
> **THE ADVERBS** · conceivably · arguably · presumably · ostensibly · allegedly · reportedly · demonstrably · unequivocally
> **THE FRAMES** · is likely to · is bound to · might well have · could hardly have · must surely be · may or may not · is said to · has yet to be
> **THE STANDARDS** · on the balance of probability · to the best of our knowledge · that is overstating it

### 12F · Next

> **Unit 4 · What was going to happen?**



# B2.1 · Unit 4 · It Was Going to Open in 2019

> **English Animated** · *Reading Between Lines* · a newsroom, Toronto
> Student's Book pages 72–93 · 22 pp · ~11 hours

## Part 0 · The Big Picture ◆ **A · THE WORLD**


![A newspaper business desk with a framed 2014 announcement, a wall of forecasts, a budget board crossed out four times and a model of a station that was never built.](figures/fig_b21_u04_p00_v01.png){width=6.6in}

*The business desk, and the wall behind it — Twelve things to find. Three are dated and two of those three disagree.*


# What was going to happen?

**By the end of this unit you can narrate a plan that did not happen, from the vantage point of
the people who made it. Two hundred words.**

| ◆ **A · THE WORLD** | A transit line announced in 2014, due in 2019, and a wall of the paper's own forecasts |
|---|---|
| ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM** | future in the past · 28 words for forecasting and failure · 22 words and frames for looking backwards and forwards · *was gonna* |
| ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE** | Two hundred words about something that was supposed to happen |

---

### 0A · Find it

**Find twelve things in this picture.** Three of them are dated and two of those three contradict
each other.

> the 2014 announcement, framed · the forecast wall · the budget board, crossed out four times ·
> a model of the station that was never built · the business desk's prediction log ·
> a hard hat with no dust on it · the first-edition page from 2014 · a calendar with 2019 circled ·
> the contractor's letter · a graph with a line that keeps moving right ·
> a photograph of the groundbreaking · an empty display case

### 0B · Guess it

**Two objects in this picture were made on the same day and now disagree.** Which two, and which
of them has been quietly replaced since?

### 0C · Say it ⏺

**Record sixty seconds.** *Describe something you were going to do last year and did not do. Say
what you thought at the time, not what you think now.* You will record it again at the end of this
unit.

## Part 1 · Vocabulary Lab 1 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**


![A cutaway of a forecast in four layers, from the assumptions through the modelling and the range to the single number that reaches the headline.](figures/fig_b21_u04_p01_v02.png){width=6.6in}

*One forecast, in section — Twelve words, and the layer each one belongs to. Only the top layer is ever quoted.*


### 1A · Meet — the words of a prediction

Match each word to one layer of the cutaway.

> **forecast** · **projection** · **scenario** · **trajectory** · **revision** · **hindsight** ·
> **foresight** · **contingency** · **overrun** · **shortfall** · **horizon** · **assumption**

### 1B · Sort — made beforehand, the shape of the thing, or counted afterwards?

Three columns. **Two of the twelve are the same word looking in opposite directions.**

> forecast · trajectory · revision · horizon · foresight · hindsight · scenario ·
> projection · contingency · assumption · overrun · shortfall

### 1C · Meet — the five phrases that carry a forecast

| | |
|---|---|
| **on current trends** | A sentence that presents a straight line as a fact about the future. |
| **a worst case** | The number nobody plans for and everybody later says they published. |
| **the central estimate** | The middle of a range, reported as though the range were not there. |
| **revised downwards** | The passive that removes whoever did the revising. |
| **an optimistic forecast** | A phrase only ever used afterwards, and never by its author. |

### 1D · Collocation Lab

Complete each one, and say which of the five could have been written in 2014.

> **with hindsight** · **as things turned out** · **the plan was to** · **a missed deadline** ·
> **the gap between**

1. ______ ______ ______ ______ open the line in the spring of 2019.
2. ______ ______ ______ ______ , the tunnelling took four years longer than the contract allowed.
3. ______ ______ , the 2014 schedule assumed nothing would be found under the harbour.
4. ______ ______ ______ the announcement and the first train is now seventeen years.
5. ______ ______ ______ is not news until there have been four of them.

### 1E · The phrasal verbs, and the three fixed expressions

> **fall through** · **come off** · **hold up**
>
> **f** *it was supposed to be* · **f** *that never happened* · **f** *nobody saw it coming*

**One of these fixed expressions is almost always false when it is said.** Which, and what is it
usually doing for the speaker?

### 1F · Own it

Write one sentence beginning *The plan was to…* about your own year, and underneath it write the
month in which you stopped believing it.

## Part 2 · Listening ◆ **A · THE WORLD**


![A business journalist and a long-serving subeditor at a desk with a printed prediction log between them, each with two speech balloons and a thought balloon.](figures/fig_b21_u04_p02_v07.png){width=6.6in}

*The forecast audit — He has five years of the desk's own predictions. He set the 2014 front page himself.*

**Before you listen.** The business desk has gone back through five years of its own predictions.
The subeditor has been at the paper since 1995 and remembers the announcement.

**Listen once.** What were the paper's own forecasts, and how many of them came off?

**Listen again. Complete the table.**

| | What was going to happen | When | What actually happened |
|---|---|---|---|
| the line | | | |
| the budget | | | |
| the paper's forecast | | | |

**Listen again. True or false?**

1. The line was originally due to open in 2019. ☐ T ☐ F
2. The paper predicted the first delay. ☐ T ☐ F
3. The budget has been revised four times. ☐ T ☐ F
4. The subeditor wrote the 2014 front page. ☐ T ☐ F

**Noticing.** Five sentences from the recording.

1. *It was going to open in 2019.*
2. *It was due to open in 2019.*
3. *It would have opened in 2019, but for the harbour.*
4. *It was to have opened in 2019.*
5. *It would later open in 2031, if it opens.*

**All five look back at a future that never arrived. Which one blames something, which one is the
most formal, and which one is a plain report?**

### 2B · Decoding clinic 🔊 Track 4.2

Thirty seconds at speed. Three jobs.

1. The subeditor says *was going to* five times. Write what you hear, not what is written.
2. One speaker says *would have* twice, in two different ways. Transcribe both.
3. Somebody says *that was the plan*. What is the tone, and how do you know?

## Part 3 · Grammar Lab 1 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### Standing in 2014 and looking forward

### 3A · Notice

Look at the five sentences in Part 2 again.

1. Where is the speaker standing in each one — in 2014, or now?
2. Which sentence contains an obstacle, and which word puts it there?
3. Sentence 4 is the most formal of the five. **What register does *was to have* belong to?**
4. Sentence 5 uses *would* about something still ahead. **How is that possible?**

### 3B · See


![A timeline from 2012 to 2034 showing the 2014 vantage point, the future that was expected from it, the opening that did not happen, and the current forecast.](figures/fig_b21_u04_p03_v09.png){width=6.6in}

*Standing in 2014, looking forward — The same stretch of time, seen from inside the plan and from here.*


### 3C · Know

| | | |
|---|---|---|
| **was going to** | the plan or expectation, neutral | *It was going to open in 2019.* |
| **was due to** | a scheduled commitment, often contractual | *It was due to open in 2019.* |
| **was about to** | on the point of happening, interrupted | *They were about to sign.* |
| **would have** | and something prevented it | *It would have opened, but for the harbour.* |
| **was to have been** | formal, written, and faintly reproachful | *It was to have been the first line in forty years.* |
| **would later** | the narrator, who knows how it ends | *It would later be revised four times.* |

> **Future in the past is a vantage point, not a tense.** You stand at a moment in the past and
> look forward from it, and the reader knows you also know what happened. That double knowledge is
> the whole effect.

> **The narrator's *would* is the most useful form in this unit.** *It would later overrun by nine
> years* is only available to somebody telling the story afterwards, and it tells the reader there
> is more coming.

| | |
|---|---|
| **standing at the start** | *originally · formerly · the plan was to · was due to* |
| **moving through** | *subsequently · latterly · would later · as it would turn out* |
| **arriving at the end** | *eventually · ultimately · in the event · as things turned out* |
| **choosing a direction** | *prospectively · retrospectively · with hindsight* |

> **Why it matters.** Every institution has a record of things that were going to happen. The
> grammar that narrates those honestly is the grammar that stops a newsroom repeating them.

### 3D · Use — controlled

Complete so that each sentence looks forward from 2014.

> The line (1) ______ ______ ______ open in 2019. *(a schedule, contractual)*
> It (2) ______ ______ ______ be the first new line in forty years. *(formal, written)*
> The contractor (3) ______ ______ ______ sign when the survey came back. *(on the point of)*
> It (4) ______ ______ opened on time, but for the harbour. *(and something prevented it)*
> The budget (5) ______ ______ be revised four times. *(the narrator, who knows)*

### 3E · Use — guided

Rewrite each one from the vantage point of 2014, looking forward.

1. The line did not open in 2019.
2. They nearly signed in March.
3. The budget was eventually six point one billion.
4. The paper got one forecast in four right.
5. The station model is still in its case.

### 3F · Use — open

**In pairs.** One of you describes a plan from inside it, in 2014. The other may only reply with a
sentence containing *would later*. **Six turns each, and the second speaker must not reveal the
ending until the last one.**

### 3G · Error autopsy

> ✗ *It was going to opened in 2019.*
> ✗ *It would have open on time if the harbour was not there.*
> ✗ *Eventually, the line was about to open in 2031.*

For each, name the rule and say what the writer was reaching for.

### 3H · Check — eight items

> 1. The line ______ ______ ______ open in 2019. *(a contractual schedule)*
> 2. It ______ ______ ______ ______ the first new line in forty years. *(formal and written)*
> 3. They ______ ______ ______ sign when the survey came back. *(on the point of)*
> 4. It ______ ______ opened on time, but for the harbour. *(prevented)*
> 5. The budget ______ ______ be revised four times. *(the narrator)*
> 6. ✗ *It was going to opened in 2019.* → correct it.
> 7. Rewrite *They did not sign* so that the reader can feel how close it came.
> 8. Rewrite *The line opened in 2031* so that a reader standing in 2014 could have said it.

## Part 4 · Speaking ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

### 4A · Fluency starter — the plan and the ending

One person states a plan in the past. The next adds a sentence with *would later*. The next adds
another. **The fifth person has to supply the ending, and it must follow from all four.**

### 4B · The functional core — narrating a thwarted plan


![Two front-page announcements of the same transit line side by side, one from 2014 and one from this year, with their dates and costs.](figures/fig_b21_u04_p04_v04.png){width=6.6in}

*The same line, announced twice — October 2014 and March this year, on the same front page, ten years apart.*


**Language Bank**

| | |
|---|---|
| **standing in the plan** | *was going to · was due to · had intended to · that was the plan* |
| **the near miss** | *was about to · would have been · it would be years before* |
| **the narrator** | *would later · as it would turn out · subsequently · latterly* |
| **the ending** | *eventually · ultimately · in the event · as things turned out* |

**Information gap.** Learner A has the 2014 announcement. Learner B has this year's schedule.
**Agree on a four-sentence account that neither document contradicts.**

### 4C · The performance — three minutes ⏺

Tell the story of something that did not happen, in three minutes, **spending the first minute
entirely inside the plan**. Your partner must not be able to tell how it ends until you say so.

**Record it.**

### 4D · Observer

One person marks every point at which the speaker leaves the plan and speaks as the narrator.

### 4E · Three criteria

> ☐ Did the first minute stay inside the plan, with no hindsight leaking in?
> ☐ Did I use at least three different future-in-the-past forms?
> ☐ Could my partner say, afterwards, what the people at the time believed?

## Part 5 · Reading ◆ **A · THE WORLD**

### 5A · Predict

The title is **"The line that was going to open in 2019."**
**How many times do you think a seventeen-year project is re-announced, and by whom?**

### 5B · Read — two minutes

---

**The line that was going to open in 2019**

In October 2014 the city announced a new transit line to the east end. It was to have been the
first new line in forty years. It was due to open in the spring of 2019, at a cost of two point
four billion dollars.

The Herald's front page that morning carried a projection of journey times and a photograph of a
model of the station. The model is still in the lobby of the planning office. The station is not
built.

The opening date has since been revised to 2023, to 2027 and, in March, to 2031. The budget has
been revised downwards once, in 2016, and upwards three times. The central estimate is now six
point one billion.

The paper has published two hundred and forty pieces about the line. Thirty-one of them contain a
forecast date. None of the thirty-one mentions the forecast it replaced.

Nobody saw it coming, the city's transport chair said in 2023, of the second delay. The 2016
contingency documents, released under access law in 2024, model the harbour problem on page
eleven and put a worst case at 2029.

---

### 5C · Comprehension — eight questions

1. When was the line announced, and when was it due to open?
2. What was the original cost, and what is the central estimate now?
3. How many of the paper's pieces contain a forecast date?
4. How many of those mention the forecast they replaced?
5. What is on page eleven of the 2016 documents?
6. **Why does the writer mention the model in the lobby twice as often as the station?**
7. **The last paragraph sets a quotation against a document. What is the writer doing, and why not say it outright?**
8. **"Revised downwards once and upwards three times" — what would be lost by writing "revised four times"?**

### 5D · Vocabulary in context

Infer from the text, then check.

> it was to have been · has since been revised · released under access law · of the second delay ·
> model the harbour problem

### 5E · The counter-text


![A city transit announcement from 2014 annotated to show its dates, its costs, its one conditional clause and which of its figures have survived.](figures/fig_b21_u04_p05_v05.png){width=6.6in}

*The 2014 announcement, read from here — Every date in it, and whether it still stands.*


**Read the 2014 announcement and answer.**

1. Which verbs in it are future in the past, read from today?
2. **Find every date in it, and say which of them still stands.**
3. The announcement contains one conditional. **What was it protecting?**
4. **Who signed it, and where are they now?**

### 5F · Two texts

The 2014 notice says *the line will open in spring 2019*.
The 2024 documents say *a worst case of 2029 was modelled in 2016*.

**Is "nobody saw it coming" a lie?** Write two sentences, and use *with hindsight* in one of them.

## Part 6 · Vocabulary Lab 2 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### Where you are standing when you say it

### 6A · Sort — at the start, somewhere along the way, or looking from one end?

Three columns.

> retrospectively · eventually · ultimately · latterly · subsequently · prospectively ·
> originally · formerly

### 6B · Chunk completion

1. The line ______ ______ ______ open in 2019. *(three words — the plan)*
2. It ______ ______ ______ open in 2019. *(three words — the schedule)*
3. They ______ ______ ______ sign when the survey came back. *(three words — on the point of)*
4. It ______ ______ ______ the first new line in forty years. *(four words — and it was not)*
5. The city ______ ______ ______ build it in two phases. *(three words — a stated intention)*
6. It ______ ______ ______ ______ ______ opened by now. *(five words — formal, reproachful)*
7. The budget ______ ______ be revised four times. *(two words — the narrator)*
8. ______ ______ ______ ______ ______ , the harbour was the least of it. *(five words — the narrator again)*

### 6C · Three that are not interchangeable

> *was going to* · *was due to* · *was to have been*

**Say which one reports a plan, which one reports a commitment, and which one carries a complaint.**

### 6D · The phrasal verbs and the fixed expressions

> **back out** · **pull out** · **call off**
>
> **f** *it would be years before* · **f** *that was the plan* · **f** *in the event*

**Two of these three phrasal verbs describe leaving an agreement and one describes ending an
event.** Which is which, and what is the difference between the other two?

### 6E · Contrast Clinic

Eight items. Rewrite each sentence so that the vantage point moves to the place in brackets.

1. Nobody expected the second delay. *(and the documents did)*
2. The paper got one forecast in four right. *(looking back deliberately)*
3. It opened in 2031. *(as a prediction made in 2014)*
4. The harbour caused a delay. *(formal, written, reproachful)*
5. The budget rose to six point one billion. *(the narrator, with more to come)*
6. The line did not open in 2019. *(standing in 2014)*
7. The contractor left the project. *(a stated intention that changed)*
8. They did not sign. *(a near miss)*

## Part 7 · Pronunciation & Fluency Lab ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**


![Four future-in-the-past phrases written out and then transcribed as they are said, with going to reduced to gonna and would have to woulduv.](figures/fig_b21_u04_p07_v10.png){width=6.6in}

*Going to becomes one sound — Written in full above, said at half the length below.*


### Going to becomes one sound and takes the stress with it

### 7A · Hear 🔊 Track 4.3

> *It was going to open.* → /ɪt wəz ˈɡənə ˈəʊpən/
> *They were going to sign.* → /ðeɪ wə ˈɡənə ˈsaɪn/
> *It would have opened.* → /ɪt ˈwʊdəv ˈəʊpənd/

### 7B · Say

Say each one three times, fast. Then say this:

> *It was going to open, they were going to sign, and it would have been finished.* — three
> reduced forms in one breath, and one stress in each.

### 7C · Make it mean something

Say these two:

> *It was going to open in 2019.* — the plain report, the stress on the date
> *It WAS going to open in 2019.* — stressed: and somebody has just denied that it ever was

**A stressed auxiliary is almost always an argument,** which is why the archive matters: you can
only stress *was* if you can produce the document.

### 7D · Fluency 30 ⏺

Thirty seconds: **one plan, told from inside it, with four reduced forms and one stressed
auxiliary.** Three times, faster.

## Part 8 · Writing ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

### A plan that did not happen · 200 words

### 8A · Read the model

> In October 2014 the line was due to open in the spring of 2019, at two point four billion
> dollars. `[the schedule and the number, as they stood, with no hindsight in the sentence]`
>
> It was to have been the first new line in forty years, and the model of the station was
> photographed for the front page. `[the formal form carries the reproach without the writer
> stating one]`
>
> The 2016 contingency documents would later put a worst case at 2029, which the city did not
> publish. `[would later: the narrator steps in, and the reader now knows there is more]`
>
> As things turned out, the opening has been revised three times since, and the model is still in
> the lobby. With hindsight, the only figure in the 2014 announcement that has held is the length
> of the tunnel. `[the ending, and one concrete detail doing the work of an adjective]`

**Questions about the writer's choices:**

1. The first paragraph has no hindsight in it at all. **What does that buy, and what does it cost?**
2. *Would later* appears once. **What would happen if it appeared four times?**
3. The last sentence names the one forecast that held. **Why end on the thing that worked?**

### 8B · The anti-model

> The disastrous transit project has been a catalogue of failures from the start. Experts warned the timeline was unrealistic, but the city pressed ahead anyway. Now, years late and billions over budget, it stands as a monument to incompetence. Nobody saw it coming.

Four sentences, three unnamed sources, no dates, and a final sentence that contradicts the second.
**Rewrite it from inside 2014, with a date in every sentence.**

### 8C · Plan

> the schedule as it stood → the formal expectation → the narrator, once → the ending, with one
> concrete survival

### 8D · Write — 200 words
### 8E · Peer review — three criteria

> ☐ Does the opening stay inside the plan, with no hindsight leaking backwards?
> ☐ Is every date the date somebody actually wrote down?
> ☐ Does the narrator step in deliberately, and not more than twice?

### 8F · Write it again · 8G · Publish

Rewrite after review. **Publish both, and mark the moment the narrator first appears in each.**

## Part 9 · The File ◆ **A · THE WORLD + C · THE PERFORMANCE**


![A study sheet setting out four learner study plans, the horizon each one had, the contingency it did not have, and the day each one stopped.](figures/fig_b21_u04_p09_v05.png){width=6.6in}

*Four study plans and what became of them — The plan is almost never the problem. The horizon is.*


# STUDY FILE · The study plan that fell through

### 9A · What you were going to do 🔊 Track 4.4

Every learner has a plan that did not come off: thirty minutes a day, a podcast every morning, a
notebook. The plan is almost never the problem. The horizon is.

| | |
|---|---|
| **the plan** | *I was going to study thirty minutes every day* |
| **the horizon** | indefinite |
| **the contingency** | none |
| **what actually held** | the two days a week with a fixed time |

### 9B · Rebuild it

Take one plan of yours that fell through.
**Write what it was, what the first missed day was, and what you would need to have had in place.**

### 9C · Role play — the 2014 version of you

In pairs. One of you describes a learning plan as you believed it at the time, with no hindsight.
The other may only ask *and what was the contingency?* **Four rounds each.**

### 9D · The one that held

Everybody has one habit that survived. **Name yours and say what was different about it.** Nine
times in ten the answer is a fixed time, not a quantity.

### 9E · Transfer — before next week

**Write next month's plan with a worst case beside it,** in the same number of words. Bring both.
The worst case is the only part that will still be true in four weeks.

![An infographic of a transit project from 2014 to 2031 showing the forecast opening dates, the construction period, the budget revisions and the coverage.](figures/fig_b21_u04_p10_v12.png){width=6.6in}

*Seventeen years, four forecasts, one tunnel — The whole line on one scale, from the announcement to the current opening date.*


### 10A · Relay

Half the class has the 2014 announcement. Half has the 2024 documents.
**Produce one paragraph both sides accept, by speaking only.** Ten minutes.

### 10B · Simplify — for one reader

A reader writes: *I bought a flat near the station in 2015. What should I have been told?*
**Reply in four sentences,** from inside 2015, and then once from now.

### 10C · Online

Write **one message** to a colleague whose article says *nobody saw it coming*. **Three
sentences.** Cite the page number and leave the judgement to them.

### 10D · Your language

How does your language look forward from a point in the past?
**Translate all five sentences from Part 2.** Which of the five distinctions does your language
keep, and which does it collapse into one form?

## Part 11 · The Decision ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

# The forecast audit

The business desk has proposed that the paper publish, once a year, every forecast it made twelve
months earlier and what became of each one.

**Option one:** do not. A newspaper's job is to report what people say, and a public scoreboard of
its own wrong guesses will be quoted against it for years.

**Option two:** publish it in full, including the names of the writers.

**Option three:** publish it in full, without names, and require that every new forecast in the
paper carry the date and value of the forecast it replaces.

### 11A · The evidence


![A line chart comparing the year each forecast was made with the opening year it predicted, showing the gap between them staying roughly constant.](figures/fig_b21_u04_p11_v06.png){width=6.6in}

*The horizon that walks away — Every time the forecast was made, the opening stayed about six years ahead of it.*


🔊 **Track 4.6.** Laran Bakare, business desk, 30 seconds: *"We ran forty forecasts in five years
and eleven came off. That is worse than a coin, and I am not embarrassed by it, because the ones
we got wrong were the ones everybody got wrong and the chart shows exactly why. What I am
embarrassed by is that we never once printed the number we were replacing."*

### 11B · Talk about it

In threes. Use the frame, and state which vantage point you are speaking from each time.

> *I would ______ , because ______ , and in five years it would ______ .*

### 11C · Decide

Write **three sentences**: the decision, the evidence, and what you would expect to see in the
first audit if you were right.

### 11D · Model decision

> Option three. Names turn an audit into a disciplinary record and the desk will quietly stop
> forecasting, which is the one outcome that helps nobody. The replacement rule is the part that
> does the work: a forecast printed beside the forecast it supersedes cannot be re-announced as
> news, and it is the re-announcement, not the error, that has cost this paper its credibility on
> the line.

### 11E · The Reveal

> **What happened.** The Herald published the audit without names and adopted the replacement rule.
> Eleven of forty was the headline figure, and it was quoted against the paper for a fortnight.
>
> Then something nobody predicted: the city's own press office began including the superseded date
> in its announcements, because the Herald printed it anyway and the office preferred to be the
> one who said it. The number of re-announcements fell by half in a year.

**One question. You do not have to answer it out loud.**

> The rule applied only to the Herald's own pages. Why did it change what the city published?

## Part 12 · Landing ◆ **all three lines**

### 12A · Recycle — fifteen items

> 1. The line ______ ______ ______ open in 2019. *(three words — the schedule)*
> 2. It ______ ______ ______ ______ the first in forty years. *(four words — formal)*
> 3. They ______ ______ ______ sign when the survey came back. *(three words)*
> 4. It ______ ______ opened on time, but for the harbour. *(two words)*
> 5. The budget ______ ______ be revised four times. *(two words — the narrator)*
> 6. ______ , the plan was two phases, not three. *(one word — at the start)*
> 7. ______ , it opened in 2031. *(one word — at the end)*
> 8. ______ , the 2016 documents say it all. *(one word — looking back deliberately)*
> 9. ______ ______ ______ ______ , the harbour was the least of it. *(five words)*
> 10. ______ ______ ______ ______ ______ ______ a train ran on it. *(six words — the long wait)*
> 11. ______ ______ ______ ______ . *(four words — the resigned confirmation)*
> 12. ______ ______ ______ , only one of the four forecasts held. *(three words — the outcome)*
> 13. The contractor ______ ______ in March, three weeks before signing. *(two words)*
> 14. The opening event was ______ ______ for the second time. *(two words)*
> 15. The whole financing ______ ______ when the bank withdrew. *(two words)*

### 12B · Consolidate — one task, everything in it

**Write 150 words about a public project in your own city that was going to happen.** Spend the
first half inside the plan and mark in the margin the sentence where you become the narrator.

### 12C · Can-do, with evidence

> ☐ I can narrate a plan from inside it, in the past. **Evidence:** 3H, 8D
> ☐ I can use *would later* and *as it would turn out* to manage what the reader knows. **Evidence:** 4C
> ☐ I can distinguish a plan, a schedule and a reproach. **Evidence:** 6C, 6E
> ☐ I can reduce *going to* and stress an auxiliary for argument. **Evidence:** 7C

### 12D · Replay ⏺

Record 0C again. **Compare.** Did you stay inside the plan this time, and for how long?

### 12E · Glossary — fifty items, as a map

> **WHAT YOU MAKE BEFOREHAND** · forecast · projection · scenario · assumption · contingency
> **THE SHAPE AND THE DISTANCE** · trajectory · horizon · foresight
> **WHAT YOU COUNT AFTERWARDS** · revision · hindsight · overrun · shortfall
> **THE FIVE FORECAST PHRASES** · on current trends · a worst case · the central estimate · revised downwards · an optimistic forecast
> **LOOKING BACK AT IT** · with hindsight · as things turned out · the plan was to · a missed deadline · the gap between
> **WHAT HAPPENS TO PLANS** · fall through · come off · hold up · back out · pull out · call off
> **IN THE ROOM** · it was supposed to be · that never happened · nobody saw it coming
> **THE ADVERBS** · eventually · ultimately · subsequently · originally · formerly · latterly · prospectively · retrospectively
> **THE FRAMES** · was going to · was due to · was about to · would have been · had intended to · was to have been · would later · as it would turn out
> **THE ENDINGS** · it would be years before · that was the plan · in the event

### 12F · Next

> **Unit 5 · On what condition?**



# B2.1 · Unit 5 · Clause Seven

> **English Animated** · *Reading Between Lines* · a newsroom, Toronto
> Student's Book pages 94–115 · 22 pp · ~11 hours

## Part 0 · The Big Picture ◆ **A · THE WORLD**


![A newspaper negotiating room with a draft contract, a redline in four colours, a side letter face down, a flip chart in two columns and an untouched plate of sandwiches.](figures/fig_b21_u05_p00_v01.png){width=6.6in}

*The negotiating room, nine hours in — Twelve things to find. Two of them are the same document and one has been signed.*


# On what condition?

**By the end of this unit you can set a condition precisely, and hear the difference between a
condition and a threat. Two hundred words.**

| ◆ **A · THE WORLD** | A new contributor contract, four hundred and ten freelancers, and one clause nobody will sign |
|---|---|
| ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM** | the full conditional system, including *provided*, *but for* and *supposing* · 28 words for terms and negotiation · 22 connectors and frames · the tune over a long conditional |
| ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE** | Two hundred words that make an offer and name its conditions |

---

### 0A · Find it

**Find twelve things in this picture.** Two of them are the same document and only one of them has
been signed.

> the draft contract, clause 7 flagged · the side letter, face down · a redline with four colours ·
> the association's counter-proposal · a list of four hundred and ten names ·
> the insurance quotation · the nine-year claims log · a flip chart with two columns ·
> a jug of water nobody has touched · the governing-law page, tabbed · a clock showing 19:20 ·
> a plate of sandwiches, untouched

### 0B · Guess it

**One document in this picture explains why the negotiation has lasted nine hours.** Which, and
why is it face down?

### 0C · Say it ⏺

**Record sixty seconds.** *Describe something you would agree to do, and state two conditions. Make
one of them a condition you would actually enforce.* You will record it again at the end of this
unit.

## Part 1 · Vocabulary Lab 1 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**


![A cutaway of a contract in four layers, from the operative clauses through what each side gives up and what happens on a breach, to the law outside the document.](figures/fig_b21_u05_p01_v02.png){width=6.6in}

*One agreement, in section — Twelve words, and the layer each one lives on. Only one of them is not in the document.*


### 1A · Meet — the words of an agreement

Match each word to one layer of the cutaway.

> **clause** · **term** · **undertaking** · **waiver** · **indemnity** · **precedent** ·
> **covenant** · **breach** · **remedy** · **settlement** · **arbitration** · **concession**

### 1B · Sort — part of the document, something one side gives up, or what happens when it goes wrong?

Three columns. **One of the twelve is not in the document at all, and is the reason the other
eleven are written the way they are.**

> remedy · arbitration · concession · undertaking · covenant · breach · clause ·
> settlement · term · precedent · indemnity · waiver

### 1C · Meet — the five phrases that keep a negotiation alive

| | |
|---|---|
| **subject to contract** | Nothing said before signature binds anybody. The sentence that allows talking. |
| **on the understanding that** | A condition stated in words, not in the document. Dangerous, and everywhere. |
| **without prejudice** | An offer that cannot be quoted against you if it is refused. |
| **in good faith** | Unenforceable, and the only reason most contracts work. |
| **the governing law** | Which country's courts, which nobody reads until they need it. |

### 1D · Collocation Lab

Complete each one, and say which of the five is the only one a court would look at first.

> **a material breach** · **a break clause** · **terms and conditions** · **a side letter** ·
> **to the extent that**

1. Eleven named contributors were offered ______ ______ ______ , face down, at eight o'clock.
2. Nobody reads ______ ______ ______ ______ ; everybody is bound by them.
3. Either side may leave after twelve months under ______ ______ ______ .
4. Only ______ ______ ______ ends the contract; everything smaller is a remedy question.
5. The indemnity applies ______ ______ ______ ______ the contributor knew and did not say.

### 1E · The phrasal verbs, and the three fixed expressions

> **hammer out** · **draw up** · **water down**
>
> **f** *that is not what we agreed* · **f** *we can live with that* · **f** *it depends what you mean by*

**One of these fixed expressions ends a negotiation and one restarts it.** Which is which, and
what does the third one buy?

### 1F · Own it

Write one condition you would put on something you do for other people, and then write what would
happen if somebody broke it.

## Part 2 · Listening ◆ **A · THE WORLD**


![A standards editor and a freelancers' negotiator across a table with a draft contract and an envelope between them, each with two speech balloons and a thought balloon.](figures/fig_b21_u05_p02_v07.png){width=6.6in}

*Clause seven, at twenty past seven — Eight of the nine pages are agreed. She has just turned the envelope over.*


### 2A · Clause seven, at seven twenty 🔊 Track 5.1

**Before you listen.** The paper's standards editor and the freelancers' negotiator have been in
the room since ten. Clause 7 makes contributors liable, without limit, for any claim arising from
their work.

**Listen once.** What does each side want, and what is the first thing either of them offers?

**Listen again. Complete the table.**

| | The condition offered | What it costs them | What they want in return |
|---|---|---|---|
| the paper | | | |
| the association | | | |
| the side letter | | | |

**Listen again. True or false?**

1. The paper has had three claims in nine years. ☐ T ☐ F
2. All three were about freelance work. ☐ T ☐ F
3. Insurance costs a freelancer about nineteen hundred dollars. ☐ T ☐ F
4. The association knows about the side letter. ☐ T ☐ F

**Noticing.** Six sentences from the recording.

1. *We will cap it, provided the piece was fully disclosed.*
2. *We will cap it, as long as the piece was fully disclosed.*
3. *We will cap it only if the piece was fully disclosed.*
4. *But for the disclosure rule, we would not cap it at all.*
5. *Supposing nothing was disclosed — what then?*
6. *Failing agreement tonight, the old contract rolls over.*

**Six conditionals, six different jobs. Which one is a threat, which one is a hypothesis, and
which two are almost the same?**

### 2B · Decoding clinic 🔊 Track 5.2

Thirty seconds at speed. Three jobs.

1. One speaker runs a twenty-two-word conditional. Mark where the voice does not fall.
2. Which word tells you the main clause is still coming?
3. Somebody says *it depends what you mean by*. What word are they querying, and why then?

## Part 3 · Grammar Lab 1 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### A condition is a door, and you choose how wide

### 3A · Notice

Look at the six sentences in Part 2 again.

1. Which two could swap places with no change of meaning at all?
2. Which one narrows the door to a single crack, and which word does it?
3. Sentence 4 describes a world that did not happen. **What is it actually telling the other side?**
4. Sentence 6 has no *if* in it. **What is doing the work?**

### 3B · See


![A branching diagram from a single claim event into six conditional connectors, each with the width of opening it allows and an example sentence.](figures/fig_b21_u05_p03_v09.png){width=6.6in}

*One event, six doors — The same claim, and six ways of saying what has to be true before the cap applies.*


### 3C · Know

| | | |
|---|---|---|
| **provided · provided that** | formal, and the condition is a requirement | *provided the piece was disclosed* |
| **as long as** | the same meaning, spoken register | *as long as you tell us first* |
| **on condition that** | explicit, and usually written into a document | *on condition that the cap applies* |
| **only if** | exclusive — nothing else will do | *only if the paper was told* |
| **unless** | the negative condition, and it is not *if not* | *unless the paper was told* |
| **in the event that** | formal, about something that may happen | *in the event that a claim is made* |
| **supposing · assuming that** | a hypothesis, invited rather than imposed | *supposing nothing was disclosed* |
| **but for · were it not for** | a condition on something that already happened | *but for the disclosure rule* |
| **failing · absent** | formal, in the sense *if there is no* | *failing agreement tonight* |
| **otherwise** | the consequence of the condition not being met | *disclose it; otherwise the cap goes* |

> **The difference between provided and only if is the size of the opening.** *Provided* says: this
> is what I need. *Only if* says: this and nothing else, and I am telling you that on purpose.

> **Unless is not the same as if not.** *Unless you tell us, the cap goes* has one exception.
> *If you don't tell us, the cap goes* is a straight consequence. The first is an invitation; the
> second is a threat, and a negotiator who confuses them loses the room.

| | |
|---|---|
| **the formal connectors** | *notwithstanding · insofar as · to the extent that · given that* |
| **the hypothetical frame** | *supposing · assuming that · were it not for · all other things being equal* |
| **what you do under pressure** | *fall back on · hold out for · give in* |

> **Why it matters.** A condition stated loosely is a condition you will not be able to enforce,
> and one stated too tightly is a condition nobody will accept. The grammar is the negotiation.

### 3D · Use — controlled

Complete with the connector the context requires.

> We will cap the indemnity (1) ______ the piece was fully disclosed. *(a requirement)*
> (2) ______ agreement tonight, the old contract rolls over. *(formal, "if there is no")*
> (3) ______ the disclosure rule, we would not have offered a cap at all. *(a condition on the past)*
> (4) ______ nothing was disclosed — what happens then? *(an invited hypothesis)*
> Disclose it before publication; (5) ______ , the cap does not apply. *(the consequence of not)*

### 3E · Use — guided

Rewrite each one with the connector in brackets, keeping the meaning as close as you can.

1. If you tell us first, we will cap it. *(provided)*
2. If you don't tell us, the cap goes. *(unless)*
3. We will cap it, but only in cases where you told us. *(only if)*
4. If there is no agreement tonight, the old contract continues. *(failing)*
5. We offered a cap because the disclosure rule exists. *(but for)*

### 3F · Use — open

**In pairs.** One of you makes an offer with a condition. The other must restate the condition
more narrowly, then more widely. **Four rounds, and nobody may use *if* at any point.**

### 3G · Error autopsy

> ✗ *We will cap it unless you tell us first.*
> ✗ *Provided that you would tell us, we would have capped it.*
> ✗ *But for you disclose it, there is no cap.*

For each, name the rule and say what the writer was reaching for.

### 3H · Check — eight items

> 1. We will cap it ______ the piece was disclosed. *(a requirement, formal)*
> 2. ______ agreement tonight, the old contract rolls over. *(one word, formal)*
> 3. ______ ______ the disclosure rule, there would be no cap. *(two words, about the past)*
> 4. ______ nothing was disclosed — what then? *(one word, an invited hypothesis)*
> 5. Disclose it; ______ , the cap does not apply. *(one word)*
> 6. ✗ *We will cap it unless you tell us first.* → correct it.
> 7. Rewrite *If you don't tell us, the cap goes* so that it sounds like an invitation.
> 8. Rewrite *We'll cap it if you tell us* so that nothing else will satisfy the condition.

## Part 4 · Speaking ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

### 4A · Fluency starter — widen the door

One person states a condition. The next states it one degree more generously. **Five people, five
degrees, and the fifth one has to be something the first person could still live with.**

### 4B · The functional core — setting conditions


![Two drafts of a contract clause side by side, one with an uncapped indemnity and one with a cap conditional on disclosure.](figures/fig_b21_u05_p04_v04.png){width=6.6in}

*Clause seven, twice — The paper's draft and the association's counter-proposal, on the same page.*


**Language Bank**

| | |
|---|---|
| **opening** | *subject to contract · without prejudice · assuming that* |
| **conditioning** | *provided · as long as · on condition that · in the event that* |
| **narrowing** | *only if · unless · to the extent that · that is conditional on* |
| **closing** | *we can live with that · but for that · all other things being equal* |

**Information gap.** Learner A has the paper's draft of clause 7. Learner B has the association's
counter-proposal. **Agree on one wording, and write down what each side gave up.**

### 4C · The performance — three minutes ⏺

Negotiate one clause in three minutes. **You must make three offers, each one conditional, and
each condition narrower than the last.** Your partner says, at the end, which one they would have
accepted.

**Record it.**

### 4D · Observer

One person writes down every condition offered and marks whether it was a requirement, an
exclusion or a hypothesis.

### 4E · Three criteria

> ☐ Was every offer conditional, and was the condition audible?
> ☐ Did I distinguish *unless* from *if not* in the way I meant it?
> ☐ Could my partner state, afterwards, exactly what I would accept?

## Part 5 · Reading ◆ **A · THE WORLD**

### 5A · Predict

The title is **"Four hundred and ten signatures and one envelope."**
**What do you think is in the envelope, and who was not shown it?**

### 5B · Read — two minutes

---

**Four hundred and ten signatures and one envelope**

Four hundred and ten people write for the Herald without working for it. The new contributor
contract runs to nine pages, and eight of them were agreed by lunchtime.

Clause 7 says that a contributor will indemnify the paper, without limit, against any claim
arising from their work. The association's position is that the clause is unenforceable in
practice and corrosive in principle: nobody with four thousand three hundred dollars of annual
freelance income can meet a claim, so the clause does not transfer risk, it transfers fear.

The paper's position is that the clause is standard, that it has never been invoked, and that the
alternative is to stop commissioning anything difficult.

In nine years there have been three claims. All three arose from work by staff reporters. None
arose from a freelance piece.

At eight o'clock a side letter was placed face down on the table. It had been offered, three
months earlier, to eleven named contributors, capping their liability at five thousand dollars.
The association had not been told. Nobody in the room disputed that the eleven are the eleven who
file the hardest stories.

The negotiator read it twice and said one sentence: *That is not what we agreed.*

---

### 5C · Comprehension — eight questions

1. How many contributors are there, and how long is the contract?
2. What does clause 7 require?
3. How many claims have there been, and from whose work?
4. How much does the side letter cap liability at?
5. How many contributors were offered it?
6. **The association says the clause transfers fear, not risk. What is the argument?**
7. **Why does the writer say "nobody in the room disputed" rather than stating it directly?**
8. **The piece ends on four words of quotation. What would a paragraph of explanation have cost?**

### 5D · Vocabulary in context

Infer from the text, then check.

> without working for it · corrosive in principle · has never been invoked ·
> placed face down on the table · the hardest stories

### 5E · The counter-text


![A contract clause annotated to show its conditions, the connector each one uses, what it lets in or keeps out, and the counts for each draft.](figures/fig_b21_u05_p05_v05.png){width=6.6in}

*Clause seven, every condition marked — Two drafts of one clause, and what each conditional connector lets in.*


**Read clause 7 and the counter-proposal, and answer.**

1. How many conditions does each version contain?
2. **Find every conditional connector in both and say what each one lets in or keeps out.**
3. The counter-proposal has a cap. **What is the cap conditional on?**
4. **Which of the two versions could a contributor read once and understand?**

### 5F · Two texts

The paper says the clause is *standard*.
The association says it is *unenforceable in practice*.

**Can both be true?** Write two sentences, and use *to the extent that* in one of them.

---

## Part 6 · Vocabulary Lab 2 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### Connectors that do the work of *if*

### 6A · Sort — a requirement, an exception, or a formal qualifier?

Three columns.

> absent · insofar · notwithstanding · provided · unless · otherwise · supposing ·
> failing

### 6B · Chunk completion

1. We will cap it ______ ______ ______ you tell us first. *(three words, spoken register)*
2. The cap applies ______ ______ ______ the piece was disclosed. *(three words, written)*
3. ______ ______ ______ ______ a claim is made, the cap is the ceiling. *(four words, formal)*
4. ______ ______ the disclosure rule, there would be no cap. *(two words)*
5. ______ ______ ______ ______ ______ the rule, the clause would stand as drafted. *(five words)*
6. ______ ______ the claims log says three, the clause has never been invoked. *(two words)*
7. ______ ______ nothing was disclosed, what is the paper's position? *(two words, a hypothesis)*
8. The cap applies ______ ______ , and not in any other case. *(two words, exclusive)*

### 6C · Three that are not interchangeable

> *unless* · *only if* · *provided*

**Say which one states an exception, which one excludes everything else, and which one states a
requirement you expect to be met.**

### 6D · The phrasal verbs and the fixed expressions

> **fall back on** · **hold out for** · **give in**
>
> **f** *but for that* · **f** *all other things being equal* · **f** *that is conditional on*

**One of these three fixed expressions is almost never true when it is said.** Which, and what is
it protecting the speaker from?

### 6E · Contrast Clinic

Eight items. Rewrite each sentence so that the condition changes in the way the bracket says.

1. If there is no agreement, the old contract continues. *(one formal word)*
2. The cap applies to disclosed pieces. *(make the condition explicit and written)*
3. Tell us first, or there is no cap. *(state the consequence with one adverb)*
4. If you tell us, we will cap it. *(nothing else will do)*
5. The clause stands, although the log says three claims. *(a formal concessive qualifier)*
6. Perhaps nothing was disclosed. *(invite the other side to think it through)*
7. We capped it because the rule exists. *(a condition on the past)*
8. If you don't tell us, the cap goes. *(an exception, not a threat)*

## Part 7 · Pronunciation & Fluency Lab ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**


![Four pitch contours over conditional sentences, showing the level or rising tune across a fronted condition and the fall only on the main clause.](figures/fig_b21_u05_p07_v10.png){width=6.6in}

*The voice does not fall until the condition is finished — Front the condition and it is the news. Put it last and it is a detail.*


### A long conditional does not fall until the end

### 7A · Hear 🔊 Track 5.3

> *Provided the piece was fully disclosed before publication,* ↗ *we will cap it.* ↘
> *We will cap it,* ↘ *provided the piece was fully disclosed.* ↘
> *Unless you tell us first,* ↗ *the cap does not apply.* ↘

### 7B · Say

Say each one three times. Then say this twenty-two-word sentence without letting your voice fall
until the last three words:

> *Provided the piece was fully disclosed to a named editor before publication, and nothing was
> withheld, the cap applies.*

### 7C · Make it mean something

Say these two:

> *We will cap it, provided you tell us.* — the condition is an afterthought, and sounds routine
> *Provided you tell us, we will cap it.* — the condition is the news, and the room hears it

**Front the condition and it becomes the point; put it last and it becomes a detail.** Negotiators
front conditions they intend to enforce.

### 7D · Fluency 30 ⏺

Thirty seconds: **one offer, three conditions, each fronted, and the voice level until the end of
each.** Three times, faster.

---

## Part 8 · Writing ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

### An offer with its conditions · 200 words

### 8A · Read the model

> The paper will cap a contributor's liability at the fee paid for the piece, provided the piece
> was disclosed in full to a named editor before publication. `[the offer, then the condition,
> fronted inside the sentence so it cannot be skimmed]`
>
> Unless that disclosure was made, the cap does not apply. `[unless, with one exception — not a
> threat, and the difference is deliberate]`
>
> In the event that a claim is made, the paper will conduct the defence and the contributor will
> cooperate. `[the formal frame for something that may happen, and both obligations in one
> sentence]`
>
> But for the disclosure rule, this cap would not be on the table; all other things being equal,
> the association would have preferred the clause deleted, and has said so. `[a condition on the
> past, and the other side's position stated by us before they state it]`

**Questions about the writer's choices:**

1. The condition is fronted in sentence one. **What would moving it to the end have done?**
2. Sentence two uses *unless*. **What would *if you don't* have sounded like?**
3. The last sentence states the other side's preference. **Why give that away?**

### 8B · The anti-model

> We are pleased to offer a cap on liability. We would of course expect contributors to act responsibly and to keep us informed. Obviously if something goes wrong we will need to look at the circumstances. We hope this is acceptable.

Four sentences, three conditions, and not one of them stated in a form anybody could enforce.
**Rewrite it with every condition explicit and the consequence of each one named.**

### 8C · Plan

> the offer with its condition fronted → the exception → the formal frame for a future event →
> the condition on the past, and the other side's position

### 8D · Write — 200 words
### 8E · Peer review — three criteria

> ☐ Is every condition stated in a form somebody could check?
> ☐ Does each connector do the job you meant — requirement, exception or hypothesis?
> ☐ Is the consequence of each unmet condition stated, not implied?

### 8F · Write it again · 8G · Publish

Rewrite after review. **Publish both, and underline every conditional connector.**

## Part 9 · The File ◆ **A · THE WORLD + C · THE PERFORMANCE**


![A study sheet setting out four conditions learners place on their own study, who decides whether each is met, and an enforceable version of each.](figures/fig_b21_u05_p09_v05.png){width=6.6in}

*Four conditions a learner sets, and who decides them — A condition only works if somebody other than you can tell whether it has been met.*


# STUDY FILE · The conditions you set on yourself

### 9A · The unenforceable condition 🔊 Track 5.4

Learners set conditions on their own study constantly, and almost never in a form that can be
checked: *I'll do it when I have time*, *as long as work calms down*, *unless something comes up*.
Each one looks like a condition and is in fact a permanent exemption.

| | |
|---|---|
| **the condition** | *as long as work calms down* |
| **who decides it is met** | the person who benefits from it not being met |
| **the enforceable version** | *on Tuesdays and Fridays at seven, unless I am abroad* |
| **the difference** | the second one names the exception in advance |

### 9B · Make one enforceable

Take three conditions you have set on your own study.
**Rewrite each so that somebody else could tell, by looking, whether it had been met.**

### 9C · Role play — the negotiator

In pairs. One of you proposes a study plan with a condition. The other may only ask *and who
decides whether that has happened?* **Four rounds each.**

### 9D · The exception you will actually use

Everybody needs one exception or the plan breaks the first week.
**Name yours in advance, in writing, and put a number on how often it may be used.**

### 9E · Transfer — before next week

**Write one study commitment with its exception stated using *unless*, and give it to somebody.**
Bring back what they said when you tried to use the exception.

## Part 10 · Mediation & Interaction ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**


![An infographic of a contributor contract showing who signs the clause, who was offered a side letter, where the claims actually arose and what the clause costs.](figures/fig_b21_u05_p10_v12.png){width=6.6in}

*Four hundred and ten contributors, one clause — Who signs it, who was offered more, and where the risk actually turned out to be.*


### 10A · Relay

Half the class has clause 7. Half has the counter-proposal.
**Produce one clause both sides will sign, by speaking only.** Ten minutes.

### 10B · Simplify — for one reader

A contributor writes: *What am I actually agreeing to?*
**Reply in four sentences,** with every condition and its consequence named, and no legal words.

### 10C · Online

Write **one message** to the eleven contributors who were offered the side letter. **Three
sentences.** Say what happened and what happens now.

### 10D · Your language

How does your language distinguish *unless* from *if not*?
**Translate all six sentences from Part 2.** Which of the six collapse into one form?

## Part 11 · The Decision ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

# Clause seven

It is twenty past seven and eight of the nine pages are agreed.

**Option one:** keep clause 7 as drafted. It is standard across the industry, it has never been
invoked, and removing it will be read as an admission.

**Option two:** delete it. The paper commissions the work, the paper carries the risk, and the
claims log says the risk is not where the clause puts it.

**Option three:** cap liability at the fee paid for the piece, conditional on full disclosure to a
named editor before publication — and withdraw every side letter, publishing the fact that they
existed.

### 11A · The evidence


![A bar chart comparing four versions of an indemnity clause on the share of contributors willing to file a difficult story and the share of risks flagged before publication.](figures/fig_b21_u05_p11_v06.png){width=6.6in}

*What each version of clause seven actually produced — A survey of 410 contributors, and the disclosure log for the year that followed.*


🔊 **Track 5.6.** The association's negotiator, 30 seconds: *"I have been asked all evening what we
would accept. Here is what we will not accept: a clause that makes eleven people safe and three
hundred and ninety-nine people quiet. Cap it, delete it, I can argue either at a meeting. The side
letter I cannot argue at all, because it is not a term, it is a favour, and a favour can be
withdrawn by the person who gave it."*

### 11B · Talk about it

In threes. Use the frame, and front every condition you state.

> *I would ______ , provided ______ , and unless ______ , I would ______ .*

### 11C · Decide

Write **three sentences**: the decision, the condition it rests on, and what would make you
abandon it.

### 11D · Model decision

> Option three. Deletion is cleaner and I would sign it, but it removes the one thing the clause
> could usefully do, which is make a contributor tell an editor what they know before publication
> rather than after. A cap at the fee is small enough to be survivable and large enough to be felt,
> and it is conditional on the behaviour the paper actually wants. The side letters go in any case:
> whatever they bought, they cost more.

### 11E · The Reveal

> **What happened.** The Herald capped liability at the fee, conditional on disclosure, and
> withdrew the eleven side letters. Two of the eleven resigned from the panel in protest at losing
> them. Nobody else did.
>
> Then something nobody predicted: pre-publication disclosure by freelancers rose from under a
> third to four fifths within a year — not because the cap was generous, but because a condition
> somebody can meet is a condition they think about, and an unlimited liability is one they can
> only try not to think about at all.

**One question. You do not have to answer it out loud.**

> The uncapped clause asked for more and got less. Why?

---

## Part 12 · Landing ◆ **all three lines**

### 12A · Recycle — fifteen items

> 1. We will cap it ______ the piece was disclosed. *(one word, a formal requirement)*
> 2. ______ you tell us first, the cap does not apply. *(one word, an exception)*
> 3. ______ ______ the disclosure rule, there would be no cap. *(two words)*
> 4. ______ agreement tonight, the old contract rolls over. *(one word)*
> 5. ______ nothing was disclosed, what then? *(one word, a hypothesis)*
> 6. Tell us first; ______ , the cap goes. *(one word)*
> 7. The clause stands ______ the claims log. *(one word, formal and concessive)*
> 8. The cap applies ______ ______ ______ ______ the contributor knew. *(four words)*
> 9. The talks are ______ ______ until both boards sign. *(two words)*
> 10. The offer was made ______ ______ , so it cannot be quoted. *(two words)*
> 11. Everything in this room is being done ______ ______ ______ . *(three words)*
> 12. ______ ______ ______ ______ ______ ______ . *(six words — the sentence that ended the evening)*
> 13. It took nine hours to ______ ______ one clause. *(two words)*
> 14. The lawyers will ______ ______ the final text tomorrow. *(two words)*
> 15. The association refused to let them ______ ______ the disclosure rule. *(two words)*

### 12B · Consolidate — one task, everything in it

**Write 150 words offering something with three conditions**, one fronted, one as an exception and
one about the past. Mark the three in the margin.

### 12C · Can-do, with evidence

> ☐ I can use the full conditional system, including *but for* and *failing*. **Evidence:** 3H, 8D
> ☐ I can distinguish a requirement, an exception and an exclusion. **Evidence:** 6C, 6E
> ☐ I can set a condition somebody else could check. **Evidence:** 9B, 10B
> ☐ I can hold the tune across a long conditional. **Evidence:** 7B

### 12D · Replay ⏺

Record 0C again. **Compare.** Is the condition you set the first time one you could enforce?

### 12E · Glossary — fifty items, as a map

> **PARTS OF THE DOCUMENT** · clause · term · undertaking · covenant
> **WHAT ONE SIDE GIVES UP** · waiver · indemnity · concession
> **WHEN IT GOES WRONG** · breach · remedy · arbitration · settlement · precedent
> **THE FIVE PHRASES** · subject to contract · on the understanding that · without prejudice · in good faith · the governing law
> **WHAT IS IN THE PAPERWORK** · a material breach · a break clause · terms and conditions · a side letter · to the extent that
> **WHAT NEGOTIATORS DO** · hammer out · draw up · water down · fall back on · hold out for · give in
> **IN THE ROOM** · that is not what we agreed · we can live with that · it depends what you mean by
> **THE CONNECTORS** · provided · supposing · unless · otherwise · failing · absent · notwithstanding · insofar
> **THE FRAMES** · as long as · on condition that · in the event that · but for · were it not for · given that · assuming that · only if
> **CLOSING IT** · but for that · all other things being equal · that is conditional on

### 12F · Next

> **Unit 6 · Why put it *there*?**



# B2.1 · Unit 6 · What the City Did Was Borrow

> **English Animated** · *Reading Between Lines* · a newsroom, Toronto
> Student's Book pages 116–137 · 22 pp · ~11 hours

## Part 0 · The Big Picture ◆ **A · THE WORLD**


![A newspaper training room with four versions of one paragraph pinned up, a whiteboard carrying one sentence eight times, a readers' survey and a fare-box diagram.](figures/fig_b21_u06_p00_v01.png){width=6.6in}

*The cadet room, five past ten — Twelve things to find. Four of them say the same thing in four different orders.*


# Why put it *there*?

**By the end of this unit you can decide where in a sentence the point goes, and put it there on
purpose. Two hundred words.**

| ◆ **A · THE WORLD** | A cadet course, one financing story, and six hundred readers who took away the opposite point |
|---|---|
| ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM** | cleft and pseudo-cleft for information structure · 28 words for explaining and teaching · 22 focusing adverbs and frames · nuclear stress and theme |
| ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE** | Two hundred words in which the point is where you put it |

---

### 0A · Find it

**Find twelve things in this picture.** Four of them say the same thing in four different orders.

> the four versions of paragraph one, pinned up · the readers' survey, six hundred responses ·
> the whiteboard with one sentence on it eight times · the cadet handbook, open at page 3 ·
> a highlighter that has run out · the style guide, open at FIRST PARAGRAPH ·
> the fare-box diagram · a printed story with one line circled ·
> the standfirst box, drawn in marker · a stack of yesterday's papers ·
> a clock showing 10:05 · a window with the transit line visible

### 0B · Guess it

**One thing on the whiteboard is the version the paper actually printed.** Which, and how can you
tell from the room rather than from the sentence?

### 0C · Say it ⏺

**Record sixty seconds.** *Explain something you understand well to somebody who does not. Notice
where you put the main point.* You will record it again at the end of this unit.

## Part 1 · Vocabulary Lab 1 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**


![A cutaway of an explanation in four layers, from what the reader already has, through what the teacher does and what the explanation aims at, to what gets in the way.](figures/fig_b21_u06_p01_v02.png){width=6.6in}

*One explanation, in section — Twelve words, and the layer each one belongs to. The reader arrives on the bottom floor.*


### 1A · Meet — the words of an explanation

Match each word to one layer of the cutaway.

> **explanation** · **emphasis** · **clarity** · **ambiguity** · **paraphrase** · **analogy** ·
> **scaffolding** · **misconception** · **demonstration** · **prerequisite** · **intuition** ·
> **abstraction**

### 1B · Sort — what the reader brings, what the teacher does, or what you are aiming at?

Three columns. **Two of the twelve are the same thing, judged from the two ends of the room.**

> explanation · ambiguity · scaffolding · intuition · analogy · clarity · paraphrase ·
> abstraction · misconception · prerequisite · demonstration · emphasis

### 1C · Meet — the five phrases that put a point in focus

| | |
|---|---|
| **the key point is** | A promise. Everything after it has to be worth the build-up. |
| **what matters here** | The pseudo-cleft in ordinary clothes. It puts the point at the end. |
| **a worked example** | The thing learners remember when the explanation has gone. |
| **in other words** | A second attempt, and an admission that the first one failed. |
| **the thing to notice** | Directs attention before the reader has decided where to look. |

### 1D · Collocation Lab

Complete each one, and say which of the five is the only one that makes a claim about the reader.

> **to put it another way** · **a common misconception** · **step by step** · **from first
> principles** · **the short answer**

1. ______ ______ ______ is that the city borrowed against future fares.
2. ______ ______ ______ is that a fare rise pays for the line. It does not.
3. The cadets build the whole model ______ ______ ______ on the first morning.
4. Dennis takes them through it ______ ______ ______ , and it takes ninety minutes.
5. ______ ______ ______ ______ : the city sold the next thirty years of ticket income.

### 1E · The phrasal verbs, and the three fixed expressions

> **go over** · **break down** · **spell out**
>
> **f** *let me put it another way* · **f** *the point I am making is* · **f** *that is the whole idea*

**One of these fixed expressions is usually a sign the speaker has lost the room.** Which, and
what should they do instead?

### 1F · Own it

Write one sentence explaining your work or study to somebody who knows nothing about it, and then
underline the four words that carry the point.

## Part 2 · Listening ◆ **A · THE WORLD**


![A subeditor and a trainer at a table with four printed versions of one paragraph between them, each with two speech balloons and a thought balloon.](figures/fig_b21_u06_p02_v07.png){width=6.6in}

*The cadet course, ten past ten — He has thirty-one years. She has six hundred readers.*


### 2A · The cadet course, ten past ten 🔊 Track 6.1

**Before you listen.** Four cadets have written the first paragraph of the same financing story.
The subeditor has thirty-one years. The trainer has the readers' survey.

**Listen once.** What did the city actually do, and which version of the paragraph says so?

**Listen again. Complete the table.**

| | Where the point sits | What the reader meets first | What they remembered |
|---|---|---|---|
| version A | | | |
| version B | | | |
| version C | | | |

**Listen again. True or false?**

1. Six hundred readers were surveyed. ☐ T ☐ F
2. The clearest version was the shortest. ☐ T ☐ F
3. Version A put the point in the first seven words. ☐ T ☐ F
4. The trainer wrote the standfirst herself. ☐ T ☐ F

**Noticing.** Five sentences from the recording.

1. *The city borrowed against the fare box.*
2. *What the city did was borrow against the fare box.*
3. *It was the fare box that the city borrowed against.*
4. *What happened was that the city borrowed against the fare box.*
5. *The thing that nobody mentioned was the fare box.*

**Five sentences, the same facts, five different points. Which one makes *the fare box* the news,
and which one makes *borrowing* the news?**

### 2B · Decoding clinic 🔊 Track 6.2

Thirty seconds at speed. Three jobs.

1. The trainer reads one sentence twice with the stress in different places. Mark both.
2. What changes about the meaning each time?
3. Somebody says *that is the whole idea*. What have they just conceded?
