## Part 3 · Grammar Lab 1 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### Moving the point to where the reader is looking

### 3A · Notice

Look at the five sentences in Part 2 again.

1. In sentence 1, which word carries the point, and how do you know?
2. Sentence 2 begins with four words that contain no information at all. **What are they for?**
3. Sentence 3 puts the fare box early. **What happens to everything after *that*?**
4. Sentences 4 and 5 both delay the point. **Which one delays it longer, and why might you want to?**

### 3B · See


![One sentence drawn twice as chunks, with brackets marking the focus early in an it-cleft and at the end in a pseudo-cleft.](figures/fig_b21_u06_p03_v09.png){width=6.6in}

*Two clefts, two positions for the point — The frame carries no information. What it carries is a position.*


### 3C · Know

| | | |
|---|---|---|
| **the it-cleft** | *It was X that…* — focus early, the rest presupposed | *It was the fare box that they borrowed against.* |
| **the pseudo-cleft** | *What… was X* — focus at the end, where English wants it | *What they did was borrow against the fare box.* |
| **the reversed pseudo-cleft** | *X is what…* — the focus first, emphatically | *The fare box is what they borrowed against.* |
| **the *what happened was* frame** | an empty opening that buys the reader a second | *What happened was that the city borrowed.* |
| **the *thing that* frame** | the most natural spoken version of all of them | *The thing that nobody mentioned was the fare box.* |

> **English puts new information at the end of a sentence.** Every cleft is a device for getting
> something to the end — or, in the it-cleft, for declaring that everything after it is already
> agreed.

> **The frame carries no information, and that is the point.** *What the city did was* contains
> nothing a reader did not already know. It costs five words and buys one position, and the
> position is worth more than the words.

| | |
|---|---|
| **narrowing to the item** | *namely · specifically · precisely* |
| **ranking it** | *chiefly · notably · crucially* |
| **going to the root** | *essentially · fundamentally* |
| **spoken focus frames** | *all I did was · where it goes wrong is · that is why · it was then that* |

> **Why it matters.** Readers do not read sentences; they read positions. A true sentence with the
> point in the wrong position is read as a different sentence, and the correction column cannot
> fix it, because nothing in it was wrong.

### 3D · Use — controlled

Rewrite each one using the frame in brackets.

> The city borrowed against the fare box. (1) *(what … was)*
> The city borrowed against the fare box. (2) *(it was … that)*
> The fare box was never mentioned. (3) *(the thing that … was)*
> The city sold thirty years of ticket income. (4) *(what happened was)*
> They only moved the debt. (5) *(all … was)*

### 3E · Use — guided

Rewrite each one so that the word in brackets ends up in focus.

1. The city borrowed against the fare box in 2016. *(2016)*
2. The council approved it without a vote. *(without a vote)*
3. Nobody told the transit board. *(the transit board)*
4. The fare rise pays the interest, not the line. *(the interest)*
5. Two of the nine members abstained. *(abstained)*

### 3F · Use — open

**In pairs.** One of you states a fact. The other restates it three times, moving the focus to a
different element each time. **The first speaker says which version they would print.**

### 3G · Error autopsy

> ✗ *What the city did was borrowed against the fare box.*
> ✗ *It was the city who borrowed it was in 2016.*
> ✗ *The thing what nobody mentioned was the fare box.*

For each, name the rule and say what the writer was reaching for.

### 3H · Check — eight items

> 1. ______ the city did ______ borrow against the fare box. *(two gaps — the pseudo-cleft)*
> 2. ______ ______ the fare box ______ they borrowed against. *(three gaps — the it-cleft)*
> 3. ______ ______ ______ nobody mentioned was the fare box. *(three words)*
> 4. ______ ______ ______ that the city sold thirty years of income. *(three words)*
> 5. ______ they did ______ move the debt. *(two gaps — all … was)*
> 6. ✗ *What the city did was borrowed.* → correct it.
> 7. Rewrite *They approved it without a vote* so that *without a vote* is the news.
> 8. Rewrite *It was the fare box that they borrowed against* so that the focus falls at the end.

## Part 4 · Speaking ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

### 4A · Fluency starter — move the point

One person says a plain sentence. Everybody else restates it with the focus on a different word.
**Nobody may repeat a position already used.**

### 4B · The functional core — managing information flow


![Two versions of a first paragraph side by side, one in plain order with the point in the middle and one in a pseudo-cleft with the point at the end.](figures/fig_b21_u06_p04_v04.png){width=6.6in}

*The same paragraph, two orders — Every fact identical. One of them six hundred readers could repeat back.*


**Language Bank**

| | |
|---|---|
| **delaying the point** | *what happened was · the thing that · what matters is · the reason is that* |
| **asserting it early** | *it was X that · the key point is · what matters here* |
| **narrowing** | *namely · specifically · precisely · in other words* |
| **repairing** | *to put it another way · what I mean is · it is not that* |

**Information gap.** Learner A has the city's statement. Learner B has the transit board's minutes.
**Explain the financing to each other in four sentences each, and compare where you put the point.**

### 4C · The performance — three minutes ⏺

Explain something technical from your own field in three minutes **to somebody who will be tested
on it afterwards**. Use at least three focusing frames. Your partner takes the test.

**Record it.**

### 4D · Observer

One person writes down every sentence in which the point arrives at the end, and every one in
which it arrives first.

### 4E · Three criteria

> ☐ Did the point of each sentence land where I meant it to?
> ☐ Did I use a frame when the point needed to be delayed, and not otherwise?
> ☐ Could my partner answer the test without me repeating anything?

## Part 5 · Reading ◆ **A · THE WORLD**

### 5A · Predict

The title is **"Six hundred readers, four paragraphs, one story."**
**If readers take the wrong point from a true sentence, whose mistake is it?**

### 5B · Read — two minutes

---

**Six hundred readers, four paragraphs, one story**

In 2016 the city raised money for the east-end line by selling the right to thirty years of ticket
income. The fare box was not the security for a loan. It was the thing that was sold.

The Herald reported this accurately, in a story of nine hundred words, whose first paragraph read:
*The city has finalised a financing package for the east-end line, involving a range of
instruments including a securitisation of future farebox revenue.*

Four years later the readers' editor surveyed six hundred readers who had read the story. Thirty-
eight per cent could state what the city had done. Of the rest, the largest group believed the
line had been paid for by a fare rise.

The cadet course now begins with that paragraph. The cadets rewrite it four ways. The version that
works, every year, is the one that begins *What the city did was*, which the subeditors hate,
because it uses five words to say nothing and is, by any measure they were trained on, worse
writing.

It is also the only version that six hundred readers could repeat back.

---

### 5C · Comprehension — eight questions

1. What did the city sell, and for how long?
2. How long was the original story?
3. How many readers could state what the city had done?
4. What did the largest remaining group believe?
5. How does the cadet course begin?
6. **Why does the writer quote the original first paragraph in full?**
7. **"Worse writing" by whose measure, and what is the writer implying about that measure?**
8. **The last sentence is one line long and stands alone. What is it doing structurally?**

### 5D · Vocabulary in context

Infer from the text, then check.

> the security for a loan · a range of instruments · by any measure they were trained on ·
> repeat back · the version that works

### 5E · The counter-text


![Four versions of a newspaper first paragraph annotated with the position of the main point in each, the frame used and the proportion of readers who understood it.](figures/fig_b21_u06_p05_v05.png){width=6.6in}

*One paragraph, four orders — The same four facts, and where the point arrives in each.*


**Read the four versions of paragraph one and answer.**

1. Where is the point in each one, counted in words from the start?
2. **Which version puts the reader's own likely misconception first, and why might that help?**
3. Two versions are the same length. **What is different about them?**
4. **Which version would you print, and what would you lose?**

### 5F · Two texts

The original says *a securitisation of future farebox revenue*.
Version D says *what the city did was sell the next thirty years of ticket income*.

**Is version D less accurate?** Write two sentences, and use the word *emphasis* in one of them.

## Part 6 · Vocabulary Lab 2 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### Words that point at one part of a sentence

### 6A · Sort — narrowing to the item, ranking it, or going to the root?

Three columns.

> notably · precisely · specifically · essentially · chiefly · namely · crucially ·
> fundamentally

### 6B · Chunk completion

1. ______ ______ ______ the position, not the words. *(three words — the pseudo-cleft frame)*
2. ______ ______ ______ ______ the sentence is true and still misread. *(four words)*
3. ______ ______ ______ ______ move the point. *(four words — all … was)*
4. ______ ______ is the first paragraph. *(two words — the thing that …)*
5. ______ ______ ______ ______ ______ is the second clause. *(five words — where it goes wrong …)*
6. ______ ______ ______ the city sold the income. *(three words — what happened …)*
7. ______ ______ ______ the cadets start with that paragraph. *(three words)*
8. ______ ______ ______ ______ the subeditors stopped arguing. *(four words — it was then …)*

### 6C · Three that are not interchangeable

> *namely* · *notably* · *essentially*

**Say which one lists exactly, which one singles out one case, and which one strips a thing to its
core.**

### 6D · The phrasal verbs and the fixed expressions

> **boil down to** · **come down to** · **point out**
>
> **f** *it is not that* · **f** *what I mean is* · **f** *this is the bit that*

**Two of these three phrasal verbs are nearly interchangeable.** Which two, and what does the
third one do that the others cannot?

### 6E · Contrast Clinic

Eight items. Rewrite each sentence so that the element in brackets is the news.

1. Nobody told the transit board. *(nobody)*
2. The cadets rewrite it four ways. *(four ways)*
3. The subeditors hate the version that works. *(the version that works)*
4. The city borrowed against the fare box in 2016. *(2016)*
5. The council approved it without a vote. *(without a vote)*
6. The city borrowed against the fare box in 2016. *(the fare box)*
7. The fare rise pays the interest. *(the interest)*
8. Six hundred readers could repeat it back. *(repeat it back)*

## Part 7 · Pronunciation & Fluency Lab ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**


![One sentence drawn five times as beats with a different word carrying the nuclear stress in each, and the reading that follows from it.](figures/fig_b21_u06_p07_v10.png){width=6.6in}

*The last stressed word is what the sentence is about — The same facts five times. Move the nuclear stress and you have moved the meaning.*


### The last stressed word is the point

### 7A · Hear 🔊 Track 6.3

> *The city borrowed against the FARE BOX.* — the fare box is the news
> *The CITY borrowed against the fare box.* — and not the province
> *The city BORROWED against the fare box.* — it did not sell it, it borrowed

### 7B · Say

Say each one three times. Then say this:

> *What the city did was SELL it.* — the frame is unstressed, every word of it, and the point
> lands on the last content word, where English wants it.

### 7C · Make it mean something

Say these two:

> *It was the FARE BOX that they sold.* — the focus is early, and everything after is agreed
> *What they sold was the FARE BOX.* — the focus is last, and the reader has been prepared

**A cleft moves the nuclear stress, and the nuclear stress is the sentence's actual meaning.**
Write the stress where you want it and the syntax will follow.

### 7D · Fluency 30 ⏺

Thirty seconds: **one fact, said five ways, with the nuclear stress in a different place each
time.** Three times, faster.

---

## Part 8 · Writing ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

### An explanation that lands · 200 words

### 8A · Read the model

> What the city did in 2016 was sell the next thirty years of ticket income. `[the pseudo-cleft:
> five words of frame, and the point at the end where it is remembered]`
>
> It was not a loan secured against the fare box; it was the fare box itself. `[an it-cleft
> correcting the misconception the reader almost certainly arrived with]`
>
> The thing that nobody mentioned at the time was what happens in year thirty-one. `[the spoken
> frame, which delays the point for a full clause and is the most readable of all of them]`
>
> Essentially, the line was paid for by passengers who have not travelled yet; specifically, by
> everybody who will buy a ticket between now and 2046. `[root first, then the exact set — the two
> adverbs do different jobs and must come in that order]`

**Questions about the writer's choices:**

1. The first sentence spends five words saying nothing. **What did those five words buy?**
2. The second sentence corrects something the reader was never told. **Why is that allowed here?**
3. *Essentially* then *specifically*. **What happens if you swap them?**

### 8B · The anti-model

> The financing package, which involved a securitisation of future revenue streams in conjunction with conventional instruments, was finalised in 2016 following a procurement process that had commenced the previous year and was subject to the usual approvals.

One sentence, forty-two words, every fact true, and the point buried in the middle of a
subordinate clause.
**Rewrite it as four sentences, each with its point at the end.**

### 8C · Plan

> the pseudo-cleft with the main point → the it-cleft correcting the misconception → the spoken
> frame delaying the hardest idea → the root, then the exact set

### 8D · Write — 200 words
### 8E · Peer review — three criteria

> ☐ Does each sentence end on the word you want remembered?
> ☐ Is every frame earning its words, or is one of them just a throat-clearing?
> ☐ Could a reader state the point back to you after one reading?

### 8F · Write it again · 8G · Publish

Rewrite after review. **Publish both, and mark the last stressed word of every sentence.**

---

## Part 9 · The File ◆ **A · THE WORLD + C · THE PERFORMANCE**


![A study sheet of four learner explanations showing where the main point sat in each, how much the listener kept, and a framed version of each.](figures/fig_b21_u06_p09_v05.png){width=6.6in}

*Four explanations, and where the point sat — Learners put the conclusion first. The listener has nothing to attach it to.*


# STUDY FILE · Explaining something in a language you are still learning

### 9A · The point at the wrong end 🔊 Track 6.4

Learners explaining something in English tend to put the point first, because they are not sure
they will be able to finish the sentence. The result is accurate, complete and unmemorable: the
listener meets the hardest idea before they are ready for it.

| | |
|---|---|
| **what you do** | state the conclusion, then the background |
| **why** | insurance, in case the sentence collapses |
| **what it costs** | the listener has nothing to attach the conclusion to |
| **the fix** | one frame — *what happens is…* — which buys you four words of thinking time |

### 9B · Buy yourself the time

Take three things you can already explain in English.
**Rewrite each with a frame at the front, and say the new version out loud.**

### 9C · Role play — the test afterwards

In pairs. One of you explains something for ninety seconds. The other then answers three questions
on it. **Swap, and compare how much each listener kept.**

### 9D · The frame you already have

Every learner has one frame they use constantly, usually *the problem is*.
**Name yours, and learn two more this week.** Two is enough; four sounds like a tic.

### 9E · Transfer — before next week

**Explain one thing to somebody outside the class, twice: once point-first and once point-last.**
Bring back which one they could repeat.

---

## Part 10 · Mediation & Interaction ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**


![An infographic comparing four versions of a first paragraph on reader comprehension and how far readers got into the story.](figures/fig_b21_u06_p10_v12.png){width=6.6in}

*Six hundred readers, four paragraphs — How many understood each version, and how far into the story they got.*


### 10A · Relay

Half the class has the city's statement. Half has the readers' survey.
**Produce one first paragraph, by speaking only.** Ten minutes.

### 10B · Simplify — for one reader

A reader writes: *So did my fare rise pay for the line or not?*
**Reply in four sentences.** Put the answer at the end of the first one.

### 10C · Online

Write **one message** to a colleague whose opening paragraph is accurate and unreadable. **Three
sentences.** Do not rewrite it for them; show them where the point currently is.

### 10D · Your language

Does your language use clefts, or does it move words instead?
**Translate all five sentences from Part 2.** Which of the five come out identical?

---

## Part 11 · The Decision ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

# The first paragraph

Thirty-eight per cent of readers could state what a correct story said.

**Option one:** change nothing. The story was accurate, the words were right, and a newspaper
cannot write down to its readers without insulting them.

**Option two:** a house rule that the point of any complex story appears in the first seven words.

**Option three:** a one-sentence standfirst above every complex story, written in a focus frame —
*What the city did was…* — and written before the story, not after it.

### 11A · The evidence


![A bar chart comparing four versions of a first paragraph on the share of readers who stated the point correctly and the share who read past paragraph three.](figures/fig_b21_u06_p11_v06.png){width=6.6in}

*Four first paragraphs, six hundred readers — The same four facts, four orders, and what readers did with each.*


🔊 **Track 6.6.** Dennis Hoy, subeditor, 30 seconds: *"I have spent thirty-one years taking those
five words out of other people's copy. 'What the city did was.' It is flab, it is a throat-clear,
and I was right every time by the standard I was taught. The survey says I was also removing the
only part of the sentence the reader could hold on to. Both of those things are true and I have
not worked out what to do with that yet."*

### 11B · Talk about it

In threes. Use the frame, and put the point of each turn at the end of your sentence.

> *What I would do is ______ , because ______ , and the cost of it is ______ .*

### 11C · Decide

Write **three sentences**: the decision, the evidence, and what you would be giving up.

### 11D · Model decision

> Option three. The seven-word rule is a blunt instrument that will produce seven bad words at the
> top of every difficult story. The standfirst keeps the story itself intact and gives the point
> one position where it cannot be missed. Writing it first is the part that matters: a writer who
> cannot put the point in one framed sentence does not yet know what the story is, and finding that
> out before writing nine hundred words is worth more than the standfirst.

### 11E · The Reveal

> **What happened.** The Herald adopted the standfirst. Readers' comprehension on complex stories
> rose from thirty-eight per cent to seventy-one within a year.
>
> Then something nobody predicted: the standfirsts began to change the stories. Writers drafting
> the framed sentence first found, often, that they could not finish it — and the thing they could
> not finish was usually the thing they had not yet reported. The desk's query rate to reporters
> rose by a third, before the first word of copy was written.

**One question. You do not have to answer it out loud.**

> The rule was about where a sentence puts its point. Why did it change what got reported?

---

## Part 12 · Landing ◆ **all three lines**

### 12A · Recycle — fifteen items

> 1. ______ the city did ______ sell the income. *(two gaps — the pseudo-cleft)*
> 2. ______ ______ the fare box ______ they sold. *(three gaps — the it-cleft)*
> 3. ______ ______ ______ nobody mentioned was year thirty-one. *(three words)*
> 4. ______ ______ ______ the city sold thirty years of income. *(three words)*
> 5. ______ they did ______ move the debt. *(two gaps)*
> 6. ______ , the passengers paid for it. *(one word — to the root)*
> 7. ______ , everybody buying a ticket before 2046. *(one word — the exact set)*
> 8. ______ , the first paragraph. *(one word — ranking it top)*
> 9. ______ ______ ______ : the city sold the next thirty years. *(three words)*
> 10. ______ ______ ______ ______ is that a fare rise paid for it. *(four words)*
> 11. ______ ______ ______ ______ ______ ______ . *(six words — the speaker starting again)*
> 12. ______ ______ ______ ______ . *(four words — the concession when the point lands)*
> 13. Dennis will ______ ______ it with them line by line. *(two words)*
> 14. Somebody has to ______ ______ what the city actually sold. *(two words)*
> 15. The whole thing ______ ______ ______ one position in one sentence. *(three words)*

### 12B · Consolidate — one task, everything in it

**Write 150 words explaining one thing you know well.** Use three different focus frames and mark
the last stressed word of every sentence.

### 12C · Can-do, with evidence

> ☐ I can use it-clefts and pseudo-clefts to move the point of a sentence. **Evidence:** 3H, 8D
> ☐ I can choose where new information lands. **Evidence:** 6E, 4C
> ☐ I can explain something so that a listener can repeat it. **Evidence:** 9C, 10B
> ☐ I can place the nuclear stress deliberately. **Evidence:** 7C

### 12D · Replay ⏺

Record 0C again. **Compare.** Where did the point sit the first time?

### 12E · Glossary — fifty items, as a map

> **WHAT THE READER BRINGS** · prerequisite · intuition
> **WHAT THE TEACHER DOES** · explanation · demonstration · paraphrase · analogy · scaffolding
> **WHAT YOU AIM AT OR AGAINST** · clarity · emphasis · ambiguity · misconception · abstraction
> **THE FIVE FOCUS PHRASES** · the key point is · what matters here · a worked example · in other words · the thing to notice
> **HOW YOU TAKE IT APART** · to put it another way · a common misconception · step by step · from first principles · the short answer
> **WHAT TEACHERS DO WITH IT** · go over · break down · spell out · boil down to · come down to · point out
> **IN THE ROOM** · let me put it another way · the point I am making is · that is the whole idea
> **THE ADVERBS** · namely · specifically · precisely · chiefly · notably · essentially · fundamentally · crucially
> **THE FRAMES** · what matters is · the reason is that · all I did was · the thing that · where it goes wrong is · what happened was · that is why · it was then that
> **STARTING AGAIN** · it is not that · what I mean is · this is the bit that

## Part 0 · The Big Picture ◆ **A · THE WORLD**


![A newspaper data desk with the city's air-quality chart pinned up beside the same chart redrawn from zero, a monitor-site map with two pins and a printout of the raw figures.](figures/fig_b21_u07_p00_v01.png){width=6.6in}

*The data desk, quarter past three — Twelve things to find. Two of them are the same chart and one of them starts at zero.*


# What does the chart not say?

**By the end of this unit you can write densely, and take a dense sentence apart to find out who
did what. Two hundred words.**

| ◆ **A · THE WORLD** | An air-quality chart, a monitor that moved, and a forty per cent reduction nobody can find |
|---|---|
| ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM** | nominalisation, and how to undo it · 28 words for measurement and charts · 22 abstract nouns and frames · stress in derived nouns |
| ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE** | Two hundred words dense enough to publish and clear enough to argue with |

---

### 0A · Find it

**Find twelve things in this picture.** Two of them are the same chart and one of them starts at
zero.

> the city's chart, pinned up · the same chart redrawn from zero · the monitor site map, two pins ·
> the raw figures, printed out · the 2023 report, open at page 40 ·
> the university's data, emailed · a ruler lying across the y axis ·
> a note that says COMPARED WITH WHAT? · the reporting-period memo ·
> a calibration certificate · a clock showing 15:15 · a window with the ward beyond it

### 0B · Guess it

**Two pins on the map are one point four kilometres apart.** Which document in the room explains
why that matters, and which one does not mention it at all?

### 0C · Say it ⏺

**Record sixty seconds.** *Describe a change you have noticed in your own city, and say how you
would measure it.* You will record it again at the end of this unit.

## Part 1 · Vocabulary Lab 1 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**


![A cutaway of a chart in four layers, from the instrument and its calibration through the processing and the statement of uncertainty to the single figure that is published.](figures/fig_b21_u07_p01_v02.png){width=6.6in}

*One chart, in section — Twelve words, and the layer each one lives on. Only the top layer leaves the building.*


### 1A · Meet — the words under a chart

Match each word to one layer of the cutaway.

> **measurement** · **calibration** · **anomaly** · **benchmark** · **aggregation** ·
> **granularity** · **resolution** · **variance** · **uncertainty** · **proxy** · **metric** ·
> **series**

### 1B · Sort — what you do to a number, how fine or how sure it is, or what you compare it with?

Three columns. **One of the twelve is a number standing in for a number nobody could get, and it
is the one most often reported as if it were the thing itself.**

> series · granularity · anomaly · benchmark · resolution · uncertainty · aggregation ·
> calibration · measurement · proxy · metric · variance

### 1C · Meet — the five things a chart can do to you

| | |
|---|---|
| **the y axis** | Where it starts decides how big the change looks. It is a choice, not a fact. |
| **a truncated scale** | An axis that does not begin at zero, with no mark to say so. |
| **a cherry-picked window** | The three years in which the line went the right way. |
| **a rolling average** | Smooths noise, and smooths away the month everything changed. |
| **the underlying data** | What is left when the chart is taken away. Usually available, rarely asked for. |

### 1D · Collocation Lab

Complete each one, and say which of the five changed in 2021 without being announced.

> **year on year** · **a data point** · **a reporting period** · **a confidence band** ·
> **the raw figures**

1. The city switched ______ ______ ______ from the calendar year to April–March.
2. ______ ______ ______ , exceedances fell by a tenth; over eight years they did not fall at all.
3. Every ______ ______ on the city's chart after 2021 comes from a different place.
4. ______ ______ ______ were released on request and took four days to arrive.
5. The university's series carries ______ ______ ______ ; the city's carries none.

### 1E · The phrasal verbs, and the three fixed expressions

> **smooth out** · **filter out** · **level off**
>
> **f** *the chart does not show* · **f** *that is a different question* · **f** *compared with what?*

**One of these fixed expressions is a question that cannot be answered without naming a baseline.**
Which, and why is that the most useful thing to ask about any percentage?

### 1F · Own it

Write one sentence containing a percentage from your own life, and underneath it write what it is
a percentage of, and when the counting started.

## Part 3 · Grammar Lab 1 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### Turning a verb into a noun, and what goes missing when you do

### 3A · Notice

Look at the five sentences in Part 2 again.

1. In sentence 1, who moved the monitor? In sentence 2, who moved it?
2. Sentence 3 contains no person at all. **How many words would you need to add to put one in?**
3. Sentence 4 keeps the verb. **What does it gain, and what does it give up?**
4. Sentence 5 is six words long and contains two events. **Name both.**

### 3B · See


![Two sentence diagrams of the same event, the first with an agent and a verb, the second with the verb turned into a noun and the agent absent.](figures/fig_b21_u07_p03_v09.png){width=6.6in}

*Where the agent goes when the verb becomes a noun — The same event, twice. Nothing is ungrammatical and three things have gone.*


### 3C · Know

| | | |
|---|---|---|
| **verb → noun** | *reduce → reduction · expand → expansion* | the event becomes a thing |
| **the agent disappears** | *they reduced it → a reduction in it* | and no grammar is broken |
| **the tense disappears** | *a reduction* is not dated | which is why reports are full of them |
| **density rises** | one noun can carry a whole clause | which is why the form exists at all |

> **Nominalisation is not a fault.** It is how English packs a clause into a subject so that the
> next clause can say something about it. *The relocation of the monitor was not announced* is a
> sentence you cannot write any other way without repeating yourself.

> **The cost is always the same three things: who, when, and to whom.** *An improvement in air
> quality has been achieved* has no person, no date and no place. Every one of those can be put
> back, and the test is whether the writer could put them back if asked.

| | |
|---|---|
| **the -tion family** | *reduction · implementation · derivation · acknowledgement* |
| **the -ment family** | *improvement · assessment · acknowledgement* |
| **the irregulars** | *expansion · deterioration · analysis · loss* |
| **the frames they live in** | *the reduction of · an increase in · a deterioration in · the question of* |

> **Why it matters.** A reader cannot argue with a noun. Give them back the verb and they can ask
> who, when, and compared with what — which are the only three questions that ever matter about a
> number.

### 3D · Use — controlled

Turn each verb into the noun the frame needs.

> The (1) ______ (reduce) of exceedances is reported as forty per cent.
> The (2) ______ (implement) of the new period followed in 2021.
> A (3) ______ (deteriorate) in the raw figures is visible at the original site.
> The (4) ______ (assess) was subject to revision in the order of ten per cent.
> No (5) ______ (acknowledge) of the move appears anywhere in the report.

### 3E · Use — guided

Put the people, the dates and the places back into each sentence.

1. A reduction in exceedances of forty per cent has been achieved.
2. The relocation of the monitor took place in 2021.
3. The implementation of the new reporting period followed.
4. An improvement in air quality is reported.
5. No acknowledgement of the change was made.

### 3F · Use — open

**In pairs.** One of you reads a nominalised sentence. The other must ask *who did what to whom?*
and keep asking until every participant is named. **Four sentences each.**

### 3G · Error autopsy

> ✗ *The reduce of exceedances was forty per cent.*
> ✗ *The implementation of the period was implemented in 2021.*
> ✗ *An improvement of air quality has been achieved in the ward.*

For each, name the rule and say what the writer was reaching for.

### 3H · Check — eight items

> 1. The ______ (reduce) of exceedances is reported as forty per cent.
> 2. The ______ (implement) of the new period followed.
> 3. A ______ (deteriorate) in the figures is visible at the old site.
> 4. The ______ (assess) is ______ ______ ______ . *(three words — not final)*
> 5. No ______ (acknowledge) of the move appears in the report.
> 6. ✗ *The reduce of exceedances was forty per cent.* → correct it.
> 7. Rewrite *A reduction in exceedances has been achieved* with a person, a date and a place.
> 8. Rewrite *The city moved the monitor and did not announce it* as one dense sentence.

## Part 4 · Speaking ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

### 4A · Fluency starter — pack it and unpack it

One person says a two-clause sentence. The next packs it into one clause with a noun. The next
unpacks it again, with everybody named. **Four rounds.**

### 4B · The functional core — writing densely


![Two charts of the same air-quality data side by side, one on a truncated axis with a single series and one from zero with both monitors shown.](figures/fig_b21_u07_p04_v04.png){width=6.6in}

*The same eight years, drawn twice — One chart is the city's. One starts at zero and keeps both monitors on it.*


**Language Bank**

| | |
|---|---|
| **packing it** | *the reduction of · an increase in · the implementation of · a deterioration in* |
| **flagging the range** | *subject to revision · in the order of · a matter of · the question of* |
| **unpacking it** | *who did what to whom · the verb has gone missing · that is a nominalisation* |
| **accounting for it** | *account for · allow for · factor in* |

**Information gap.** Learner A has the city's chart. Learner B has the university's series.
**Agree on one sentence that describes both, and count the nouns in it.**

### 4C · The performance — three minutes ⏺

Present a set of figures in three minutes. **Every claim must be dense enough for a report and
every one must survive the question *who did what to whom?*** Your partner asks it twice, without
warning.

**Record it.**

### 4D · Observer

One person writes down every abstract noun used and, beside each, whether the speaker could have
named the agent.

### 4E · Three criteria

> ☐ Was every nominalisation one I could unpack on demand?
> ☐ Did I give a baseline for every percentage?
> ☐ Did the density help the listener, or only me?

## Part 5 · Reading ◆ **A · THE WORLD**

### 5A · Predict

The title is **"Forty per cent, measured somewhere else."**
**If a measuring instrument moves, what happens to the series it was producing?**

### 5B · Read — two minutes

---

**Forty per cent, measured somewhere else**

The city's 2023 air-quality report records a forty per cent reduction in exceedance days in Ward 7
since 2016. The relocation of the monitor in 2021 is not mentioned in the body of the report. It
appears in a footnote on page forty.

The original monitor stood at the junction of Lake and Seventh, between a bus layover and a
distribution depot. The new one stands one point four kilometres away, in a park.

A university air-quality group left a second instrument at the original junction. Its series runs
without a break from 2014. In 2016 it recorded forty-one exceedance days. In 2023 it recorded
thirty-nine.

The city's chart begins its y axis at eighteen. Between 2021 and 2022 the reporting period changed
from the calendar year to April–March, so one year in the series is nine months long. The 2023
report moves the baseline from 2016 to 2018, which removes the two highest years from the
comparison without removing them from the data.

Every one of these decisions is defensible on its own. The city has published a defence of each.
There is no document anywhere in which all four appear together.

---

### 5C · Comprehension — eight questions

1. What reduction does the report record, and since when?
2. Where was the original monitor, and where is the new one?
3. What did the university's instrument record in 2016 and in 2023?
4. Where does the y axis begin?
5. What changed between 2021 and 2022?
6. **Why does the writer describe what stood beside the original monitor?**
7. **"Defensible on its own" — what is the argument the writer is making without stating it?**
8. **The last sentence is about an absence. Why is that the strongest fact in the piece?**

### 5D · Vocabulary in context

Infer from the text, then check.

> is not mentioned in the body of the report · runs without a break · one year is nine months
> long · without removing them from the data · defensible on its own

### 5E · The counter-text


![A city air-quality report page annotated to show its nominalisations, the agent hidden in each, and the four changes to the series that the chart does not display.](figures/fig_b21_u07_p05_v05.png){width=6.6in}

*The chart page, every noun unpacked — Four decisions, each defensible, and no document in which all four appear.*


**Read the city's chart page and answer.**

1. Where does the y axis start, and what does that do to the apparent change?
2. **Find every nominalisation on the page and say who the agent would be in each.**
3. The footnote on page forty is eleven words. **Rewrite it as the body text it should have been.**
4. **Which single change, undone, would alter the headline figure most?**

### 5F · Two texts

The city says *a forty per cent reduction in exceedances has been achieved*.
The university's series says *forty-one days in 2016, thirty-nine in 2023*.

**Are these in conflict?** Write two sentences, and use the phrase *compared with what?* in one of
them.

## Part 6 · Vocabulary Lab 2 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### Making a noun out of what happened

### 6A · Sort — bigger or smaller, better or worse, or somebody did something?

Three columns.

> implementation · acknowledgement · assessment · improvement · expansion · reduction ·
> deterioration · derivation

### 6B · Chunk completion

1. ______ ______ ______ exceedances is reported as forty per cent. *(three words)*
2. ______ ______ ______ the raw figures is visible at the old site. *(four words)*
3. ______ ______ ______ the new period followed in 2021. *(four words)*
4. ______ ______ ______ monitor sites is the thing nobody mentions. *(three words)*
5. The assessment is ______ ______ ______ . *(three words — not final)*
6. The error is ______ ______ ______ ______ ten per cent. *(four words — approximately)*
7. It is ______ ______ ______ where you put the instrument. *(three words — it comes down to)*
8. ______ ______ ______ the baseline has never been put to the board. *(three words)*

### 6C · Three that are not interchangeable

> *an increase in* · *the implementation of* · *subject to revision*

**Say which one names a change, which one names an action with a hidden agent, and which one is a
warning about the number itself.**

### 6D · The phrasal verbs and the fixed expressions

> **account for** · **allow for** · **factor in**
>
> **f** *that is a nominalisation* · **f** *who did what to whom* · **f** *the verb has gone missing*

**Two of these phrasal verbs mean roughly "take into account" and one means "explain".** Which is
which, and which of the three can also mean "make up a proportion of"?

### 6E · Contrast Clinic

Eight items. Rewrite each sentence as the bracket says.

1. The air got worse at the junction. *(pack it, formally)*
2. The city moved the monitor in 2021. *(pack it into a noun phrase)*
3. The reporting period was changed. *(name who changed it and when)*
4. The error is roughly ten per cent. *(use a four-word formal frame)*
5. A reduction in exceedances has been achieved. *(unpack it, with a person and a date)*
6. They did not acknowledge the move. *(pack it into a subject)*
7. The baseline was moved from 2016 to 2018. *(pack it, and keep the dates)*
8. The implementation of the period followed. *(say who implemented what)*

## Part 7 · Pronunciation & Fluency Lab ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**


![Five verb-and-noun pairs drawn as beats, with the stressed syllable marked in each and the stress moving when the noun is formed.](figures/fig_b21_u07_p07_v10.png){width=6.6in}

*The beat moves one place to the right — Turn the verb into a noun and the stress almost always lands before -tion.*


### The stress moves when the word does

### 7A · Hear 🔊 Track 7.3

> *CA-li-brate* → *ca-li-BRA-tion*
> *im-PLE-ment* → *im-ple-men-TA-tion*
> *de-TE-ri-o-rate* → *de-te-ri-o-RA-tion*

### 7B · Say

Say each pair three times. Then say this:

> *They calibrate it, and the calibration was in April.* — the same four syllables, and the beat
> moves one place to the right.

### 7C · Make it mean something

Say these two:

> *a reDUCtion in exCEEdances* — the ordinary report, two stresses, both inside the nouns
> *a reDUCtion in WHAT?* — the stress moves to the question, and the sentence becomes an argument

**In a derived noun the stress almost always lands on the syllable before *-tion*,** whatever the
verb did, which is why a reader who has only seen these words in print says them wrongly.

### 7D · Fluency 30 ⏺

Thirty seconds: **four verb-and-noun pairs, each said twice, with the beat moving.** Three times,
faster.

## Part 8 · Writing ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

### Four figures, densely · 200 words

### 8A · Read the model

> The relocation of the Ward 7 monitor in 2021 is recorded in a footnote on page forty of a
> two-hundred-page report. `[one noun carries a whole clause, and the sentence can then say
> something about it]`
>
> The city moved it one point four kilometres, from a junction between a bus layover and a depot
> to a park. `[the same event with the verb back in — who, what, where, and it is now arguable]`
>
> A university instrument left at the original site recorded forty-one exceedance days in 2016 and
> thirty-nine in 2023. `[no nominalisation at all, because this is the sentence the reader has to
> be able to check]`
>
> The reduction of exceedances reported by the city is therefore a reduction at a different place,
> over a shortened period, against a moved baseline, measured on an axis beginning at eighteen.
> `[four nominalisations in one sentence, and every one of them unpacked in the three sentences
> above it]`

**Questions about the writer's choices:**

1. Sentence one packs an event into a noun; sentence two unpacks the same event. **Why both?**
2. The third sentence has no abstract noun in it at all. **Why that sentence?**
3. The last sentence is the densest. **What has the writer earned the right to do, and how?**

### 8B · The anti-model

> Following the implementation of revised monitoring arrangements and the subsequent reassessment of the baseline, a significant improvement in air quality outcomes has been achieved across the reporting period, subject to ongoing review.

One sentence, thirty-four words, six abstract nouns, no person, no date, no place.
**Rewrite it with every agent named, and then pack it back down to two sentences.**

### 8C · Plan

> the event as a noun → the same event with the verb back → the checkable figures, plainly →
> the dense summary, which is now readable because you unpacked it first

### 8D · Write — 200 words
### 8E · Peer review — three criteria

> ☐ Could you name the agent behind every abstract noun you used?
> ☐ Does every percentage say what it is of, and over what period?
> ☐ Is there at least one sentence a reader could check without you?

### 8F · Write it again · 8G · Publish

Rewrite after review. **Publish both, and circle every abstract noun in each.**

## Part 9 · The File ◆ **A · THE WORLD + C · THE PERFORMANCE**


![A study sheet of five abstract nouns learners use about their own progress, the agent and date missing from each, and a measurable version.](figures/fig_b21_u07_p09_v05.png){width=6.6in}

*Five nouns learners hide behind — Each one is an event with the person, the date and the number removed.*


# STUDY FILE · The nouns you hide behind

### 9A · Progress, improvement, development 🔊 Track 7.4

Learners describe their own learning almost entirely in abstract nouns: *my progress is slow*, *I
need improvement in speaking*, *there is no development*. None of them has a person, a date or a
measure, and none of them can be acted on.

| | |
|---|---|
| **the noun** | *improvement in speaking* |
| **the missing agent** | you |
| **the missing date** | since when |
| **the verb version** | *I spoke for three minutes in October and for eight in March* |

### 9B · Put the verb back

Take three sentences you have said about your own learning that contain an abstract noun.
**Rewrite each with a person, a date and a number.**

### 9C · Role play — who did what to whom?

In pairs. One of you describes their progress. The other may only ask *who did what to whom, and
when?* **Four rounds each.**

### 9D · The one you can measure

Everybody has one thing they can state as a verb with a number.
**Name yours.** If you cannot find one, that is the finding, and it is this week's work.

### 9E · Transfer — before next week

**Take one abstract noun you use about yourself and turn it into a measurement with a date.** Bring
the first data point. One point is not a series, and that is the following week's work.

## Part 10 · Mediation & Interaction ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**


![An infographic of eight years of air-quality monitoring showing both monitors, the relocation, the shortened reporting year and the moved baseline.](figures/fig_b21_u07_p10_v12.png){width=6.6in}

*Eight years, two monitors, four changes — Exceedance days in Ward 7, and everything that changed about the measuring.*


### 10A · Relay

Half the class has the city's chart. Half has the university's series.
**Produce one chart caption both sides accept, by speaking only.** Ten minutes.

### 10B · Simplify — for one reader

A reader writes: *Has the air in Ward 7 got better or not?*
**Reply in four sentences,** with no abstract noun in any of them.

### 10C · Online

Write **one message** to the city's statistician. **Three sentences.** Ask for exactly what you
need, and do not accuse anybody of anything.

### 10D · Your language

Does your language form abstract nouns from verbs as freely as English?
**Translate the five sentences from Part 2.** Which ones force you to name the agent?

## Part 11 · The Decision ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

# What the paper does with other people's charts

**Option one:** reproduce the city's chart as published, with a caption noting the axis and the
move. It is their chart; the reader should see what the city issued.

**Option two:** redraw every chart from the underlying data, from zero, as house style, and say so
in small type.

**Option three:** redraw from zero, and print beside every chart one line headed *What this chart
does not show*.

### 11A · The evidence


![A line chart of exceedance days from 2016 to 2023 comparing the city monitor, which moved in 2021, with a university monitor left at the original junction.](figures/fig_b21_u07_p11_v06.png){width=6.6in}

*Two instruments, one ward — Exceedance days a year, from zero, with both series on the same axis.*


🔊 **Track 7.6.** Ines Fontaine, data desk, 30 seconds: *"Everyone argues about the axis. The axis
is the least of it. You can see a truncated scale. You cannot see a monitor that moved, a year
that was nine months long, or a baseline that quietly lost the two worst years. Those three are
invisible in any redrawing, because they are not in the chart. They are in the footnotes, and the
footnotes do not get published."*

### 11B · Talk about it

In threes. Use the frame, and give a baseline for every number you say.

> *I would ______ , because the reader cannot ______ , and the cost is ______ .*

### 11C · Decide

Write **three sentences**: the decision, the evidence, and the one thing your decision still fails
to catch.

### 11D · Model decision

> Option three. Redrawing from zero is necessary and nowhere near sufficient, because the three
> changes that move this figure most are not visible in any drawing of it. The one-line note is the
> only part of the proposal that can carry a moved monitor, and it costs one line. Its weakness is
> that it depends on somebody having read the footnotes, and nothing in this decision creates the
> time to do that.

### 11E · The Reveal

> **What happened.** The Herald redrew from zero and ran the note. Readers' complaints about charts
> fell; complaints about the note rose, almost all of them from the organisations whose charts they
> were.
>
> Then something nobody predicted: within eighteen months the city's own charts were being
> published from zero, and its 2025 report carried a page headed *Changes to the series*. Nobody
> asked for it. The press office had grown tired of the note.

**One question. You do not have to answer it out loud.**

> The note was about the Herald's own pages. Why did it change what the city drew?

## Part 12 · Landing ◆ **all three lines**

### 12A · Recycle — fifteen items

> 1. The ______ (reduce) of exceedances is reported as forty per cent.
> 2. The ______ (implement) of the new period followed in 2021.
> 3. A ______ (deteriorate) in the raw figures is visible at the old site.
> 4. No ______ (acknowledge) of the move appears in the report.
> 5. The ______ (assess) is subject to revision.
> 6. ______ ______ ______ , exceedances fell by a tenth. *(three words)*
> 7. The chart's ______ ______ begins at eighteen. *(two words)*
> 8. The error is ______ ______ ______ ______ ten per cent. *(four words)*
> 9. ______ ______ ______ ______ were released on request. *(four words)*
> 10. The university's series carries ______ ______ ______ . *(three words)*
> 11. ______ ______ ______ ______ ______ the monitor moved. *(five words — the standing caption)*
> 12. ______ ______ ______ ? *(three words — the question that needs a baseline)*
> 13. A rolling average will ______ ______ the month everything changed. *(two words)*
> 14. You have to ______ ______ the nine-month year. *(two words)*
> 15. The line appears to ______ ______ after 2021. *(two words)*

### 12B · Consolidate — one task, everything in it

**Write 150 words about a set of figures**, with one dense sentence, one unpacked sentence and one
sentence a reader could check alone. Mark the three in the margin.

### 12C · Can-do, with evidence

> ☐ I can form and use abstract nouns from verbs. **Evidence:** 3H, 8D
> ☐ I can unpack a nominalisation and name the agent. **Evidence:** 6E, 3F
> ☐ I can say what a chart does not show. **Evidence:** 5E, 10B
> ☐ I can place the stress in a derived noun. **Evidence:** 7B

### 12D · Replay ⏺

Record 0C again. **Compare.** How many abstract nouns did you use the first time?

### 12E · Glossary — fifty items, as a map

> **WHAT YOU DO TO A NUMBER** · measurement · calibration · aggregation
> **HOW FINE AND HOW SURE** · granularity · resolution · variance · uncertainty
> **WHAT YOU COMPARE IT WITH** · benchmark · proxy · metric · series · anomaly
> **WHAT A CHART CAN DO TO YOU** · the y axis · a truncated scale · a cherry-picked window · a rolling average · the underlying data
> **THE SMALL PRINT** · year on year · a data point · a reporting period · a confidence band · the raw figures
> **WHAT ANALYSTS DO** · smooth out · filter out · level off · account for · allow for · factor in
> **IN THE ROOM** · the chart does not show · that is a different question · compared with what?
> **THE ABSTRACT NOUNS** · reduction · expansion · deterioration · improvement · implementation · acknowledgement · assessment · derivation
> **THE FRAMES** · the reduction of · a deterioration in · the implementation of · an increase in · subject to revision · in the order of · a matter of · the question of
> **TAKING IT APART** · that is a nominalisation · who did what to whom · the verb has gone missing

### 12F · Next

> **Unit 8 · What was left out?**

## Part 0 · The Big Picture ◆ **A · THE WORLD**


![A newspaper subeditors' desk at edition time with the filed copy, the page-one proof, a spike holding four stories and a printout with one paragraph ringed.](figures/fig_b21_u08_p00_v01.png){width=6.6in}

*The stone, two minutes past six — Twelve things to find. Two of them are the same story and one is eight hundred words shorter.*


# What was left out?

**By the end of this unit you can leave things out on purpose, and say what a cut has cost. Two
hundred words.**

| ◆ **A · THE WORLD** | A story filed at fourteen hundred words, a hole of six hundred, and one paragraph of seventy-three |
|---|---|
| ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM** | ellipsis and substitution · 28 words for editing and brevity · 22 substitute words and frames · reduced forms and where you pause |
| ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE** | Two hundred words that imply as much as they say |

---

### 0A · Find it

**Find twelve things in this picture.** Two of them are the same story and one of them is eight
hundred words shorter.

> the filed copy, fourteen hundred words · the page-one proof, six hundred ·
> the word-count box on the screen · the standfirst, written twice ·
> the spike, with four stories on it · the caption that is longer than its picture ·
> the kicker, pencilled in · a printout with one paragraph ringed ·
> the stone, with the page made up · the edition clock showing 18:02 ·
> the cuts file, open · a mug that says CONCISION

### 0B · Guess it

**One paragraph on this desk is ringed and nobody has cut it yet.** Which two people in the room
disagree about it, and how can you tell from where they are standing?

### 0C · Say it ⏺

**Record sixty seconds.** *Tell a story you know well in sixty seconds, then tell the same story in
twenty. Say what you lost.* You will record it again at the end of this unit.

## Part 1 · Vocabulary Lab 1 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**


![A cutaway of a newspaper story in four layers, from the draft and the proof through what you do to it and what you judge it by, to the furniture around it.](figures/fig_b21_u08_p01_v02.png){width=6.6in}

*One story, in section — Twelve words, and the layer each one lives on. The reader sees the top two.*


### 1A · Meet — the words of an edit

Match each word to one layer of the cutaway.

> **draft** · **proof** · **abstract** · **edit** · **cut** · **trim** · **redundancy** ·
> **verbosity** · **concision** · **caption** · **standfirst** · **kicker**

### 1B · Sort — a stage, an action, a judgement, or a thing round the edge?

Four columns. **One of the twelve is both a stage and an action, and the stress tells you which.**

> redundancy · draft · verbosity · cut · kicker · proof · edit · trim · concision ·
> abstract · caption · standfirst

### 1C · Meet — the five things that happen at six o'clock

| | |
|---|---|
| **a first pass** | Twenty minutes, and it finds about half of what has to go. |
| **kill a paragraph** | Faster and kinder than shaving words off six of them. |
| **tighten it up** | The instruction every writer has heard and nobody can act on. |
| **the word count** | The only number on the desk that is not negotiable. |
| **down to length** | Not shorter. A specific number, by a specific time. |

### 1D · Collocation Lab

Complete each one, and say which of the five is the only one the reader ever sees.

> **an edited version** · **a working draft** · **the final cut** · **a hard cut** · **what survives**

1. ______ ______ ______ went up at four and was six hundred words over.
2. ______ ______ is a paragraph removed whole, and it is usually the honest kind.
3. ______ ______ ______ is what the reader gets, and nobody tells them it is one.
4. ______ ______ ______ went to the stone at two minutes past six.
5. ______ ______ is not what was best. It is what fitted.

### 1E · The phrasal verbs, and the three fixed expressions

> **cut down** · **chop out** · **pare back**
>
> **f** *it can go* · **f** *we are over by forty* · **f** *nobody will miss it*

**One of these fixed expressions is a prediction and two are decisions.** Which is the prediction,
and who is in a position to check it afterwards?

### 1F · Own it

Write one sentence of twenty words about your week, then write the same thing in eight, and name
the one thing the short version makes the reader guess.

## Part 2 · Listening ◆ **A · THE WORLD**


![A subeditor and a reporter at a desk with a printout between them, one paragraph ringed in pencil, each with two speech balloons and a thought balloon.](figures/fig_b21_u08_p02_v07.png){width=6.6in}

*Two minutes past six — Eight hundred words have to go and seventy-three of them are ringed.*


### 2A · Two minutes past six 🔊 Track 8.1

**Before you listen.** The Ward 7 story is filed at fourteen hundred words. The hole on page one is
six hundred. One paragraph of seventy-three words names the two councillors who voted for the
contract.

**Listen once.** What does each of them want cut, and what reason does each give?

**Listen again. Complete the table.**

| | What they would cut | How many words | What they say it costs |
|---|---|---|---|
| the subeditor | | | |
| the reporter | | | |
| the agreed version | | | |

**Listen again. True or false?**

1. The story is eight hundred words over. ☐ T ☐ F
2. The ringed paragraph is thirty-seven words. ☐ T ☐ F
3. The subeditor cuts the ringed paragraph. ☐ T ☐ F
4. The first pass finds three hundred words of repetition. ☐ T ☐ F

**Noticing.** Six exchanges from the recording.

1. *— Who's filed? — Dev has.*
2. *— Have you cut the quotes? — I have, both.*
3. *— Can we lose the colour? — We can, but I'd rather not.*
4. *— The second quote can go. — So can the third.*
5. *— I don't want to cut that one. — Neither do I.*
6. *— Someone has to decide. — I will if you will.*

**Every one of these second turns leaves words out. Write each one in full, and count what the

### 2B · Decoding clinic 🔊 Track 8.2

Thirty seconds at speed. Three jobs.

1. One speaker says *we are over by forty* in five syllables. Write what you hear.
2. Where does each speaker pause? Mark the two longest pauses and say what follows each.
3. Somebody says *nobody will miss it*. What is the full sentence, and what has been left out of it?

## Part 3 · Grammar Lab 1 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### Saying less and meaning the same

### 3A · Notice

Look at the six exchanges in Part 2 again.

1. In exchange 1, how many words has the second speaker not said?
2. In exchange 2, *I have, both* — what is *both* doing, and what has gone?
3. In exchange 4, *so can the third* — what does *so* stand for?
4. Exchange 6 contains two ellipses in six words. **Find both.**

### 3B · See


![A proportional bar showing the two words actually spoken in a short answer, the clause the listener recovers from the question, and the information nobody can recover.](figures/fig_b21_u08_p03_v09.png){width=6.6in}

*Two words carrying a four-word clause — What the speaker said, what the listener rebuilt, and what nobody can rebuild at all.*


### 3C · Know

| | | |
|---|---|---|
| **ellipsis** | leaving words out where the reader can recover them | *Dev has. → Dev has filed.* |
| **substitution with *one / ones*** | standing in for a countable noun | *the long version and the short one* |
| **substitution with *do so / do it*** | standing in for a whole verb phrase | *She cut it, and I would have done so too.* |
| **substitution with *so***  | standing in for a clause | *— The quote can go. — So can the colour.* |
| **substitution with *such*** | standing in for a kind of thing | *no such paragraph survived* |
| ***neither / either*** | carrying a negative across to a second speaker | *— I wouldn't. — Neither would I.* |

> **Ellipsis is only possible where recovery is certain.** *Dev has* works because the question
> supplied *filed*. Leave out something the listener cannot rebuild and you have not been brief;
> you have been unclear, and the two are confused daily.

> ***Do so* is formal and written; *do it* is neutral; bare *do* is British and spoken.** *She
> asked me to file and I did* is spoken; *and I did so* is a report; *and I did it* is in between.
> Choosing wrongly is one of the clearest register mistakes an advanced learner makes.

| | |
|---|---|
| **pointing back** | *the former · the latter · the same · as such* |
| **carrying a condition** | *if so · if not* |
| **adding a second speaker** | *likewise · so does mine · neither did I* |
| **pointing somewhere else** | *elsewhere* |

> **Why it matters.** Half of spoken English is what was not said, and every edit is a bet about
> what the reader can rebuild. A good cut leaves a hole the reader fills correctly without noticing.

### 3D · Use — controlled

Complete each second turn with the shortest form that works.

> — Who has filed? — Dev (1) ______ .
> — Have you cut both quotes? — I (2) ______ , ______ .
> — The second quote can go. — (3) ______ can the third.
> — I would not cut that paragraph. — (4) ______ would I.
> — Is it down to length? — If (5) ______ , it goes to the stone now.

### 3E · Use — guided

Rewrite each one, leaving out everything the listener can recover.

1. The second quote can go, and the third quote can go too.
2. I have cut both of the quotes.
3. I would not cut that paragraph, and she would not cut that paragraph either.
4. He asked me to file it and I filed it.
5. The long version is better than the short version.

### 3F · Use — open

**In pairs.** One of you makes a statement. The other agrees or disagrees **in four words or
fewer**, using substitution. **Eight rounds, and nobody may repeat a form.**

### 3G · Error autopsy

> ✗ *— Who has filed? — Dev has filed it the story.*
> ✗ *— I would not cut it. — Either would I.*
> ✗ *She asked me to file and I did so it.*

For each, name the rule and say what the writer was reaching for.

### 3H · Check — eight items

> 1. — Who has filed? — Dev ______ .
> 2. — Have you cut both? — I ______ .
> 3. — The second can go. — ______ can the third.
> 4. — I would not cut it. — ______ would I.
> 5. — Is it down to length? — If ______ , it goes now.
> 6. ✗ *— I would not. — Either would I.* → correct it.
> 7. Rewrite *The long version is better than the short version* using *one*.
> 8. Rewrite *She cut it and I cut it too* three ways: spoken, neutral and formal.

## Part 4 · Speaking ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

### 4A · Fluency starter — half as long

One person says a sentence. The next says the same thing in half the words. **Four rounds, and the
fourth version must still be a sentence somebody could act on.**

### 4B · The functional core — compressing and implying


![Two versions of the same newspaper story side by side, the filed copy and the printed version, with the paragraphs that went marked.](figures/fig_b21_u08_p04_v04.png){width=6.6in}

*The same story, twice — Fourteen hundred words as filed, six hundred as printed.*


**Language Bank**

| | |
|---|---|
| **agreeing short** | *so does mine · neither did I · likewise · the same* |
| **standing in** | *do so · do it · one · ones · such* |
| **pointing back** | *the former · the latter · as such · elsewhere* |
| **conditional short forms** | *if so · if not* |

**Information gap.** Learner A has the filed copy. Learner B has the page-one proof.
**Find the eight hundred missing words by asking questions only.** Ten minutes.

### 4C · The performance — three minutes ⏺

Tell a story for ninety seconds. Then tell the same story in thirty. **Your partner lists, from
the short version alone, three things they inferred that you did not say** — and you say whether
each is right.

**Record it.**

### 4D · Observer

One person writes down every substitution used and what each one stood for.

### 4E · Three criteria

> ☐ Could my listener rebuild everything I left out?
> ☐ Did I choose *do so*, *do it* or bare *do* to match the register?
> ☐ Did the short version imply the same thing, or a different one?

## Part 5 · Reading ◆ **A · THE WORLD**

### 5A · Predict

The title is **"Seventy-three words."**
**If a story has to lose eight hundred words, which paragraph is most likely to go, and why?**

### 5B · Read — two minutes

---

**Seventy-three words**

The story was filed at four o'clock at fourteen hundred words. The hole on page one is six
hundred. That is not a negotiation; it is a measurement.

The first pass takes twenty minutes and finds three hundred words of repetition, two sentences
that say the same thing in different registers, and a quotation that adds nothing the paragraph
above it has not said. Nobody will miss any of it.

That leaves five hundred to go, and five hundred words is four paragraphs.

Three of the four are easy. The description of the junction, which took the reporter an afternoon,
goes first. The second and third quotations go next; the first one carries the argument and the
others agree with it.

The fourth is seventy-three words long and names the two councillors who voted for the contract,
and the dates of the two meetings at which they did so. It is the least readable paragraph in the
story. It is also the only one that could not be written by somebody who had not been there.

At two minutes past six the subeditor rings it, and does not cut it, and says: *If this goes, what
is the story for?*

---

### 5C · Comprehension — eight questions

1. How long was the story as filed, and how big is the hole?
2. What does the first pass find?
3. How many words are left to cut after the first pass?
4. Which three paragraphs go, and in what order?
5. How long is the fourth paragraph?
6. **"That is not a negotiation; it is a measurement." What is the writer establishing early?**
7. **Why does the writer mention that the junction description took an afternoon?**
8. **The piece ends on a question, not a decision. What does that leave the reader to do?**

### 5D · Vocabulary in context

Infer from the text, then check.

> says the same thing in different registers · carries the argument · the least readable
> paragraph · could not be written by somebody who had not been there · rings it

### 5E · The counter-text


![An edited newspaper story annotated to show each cut in the order it was made, its length, and what the reader can no longer find out.](figures/fig_b21_u08_p05_v05.png){width=6.6in}

*What went, in order, and what it cost — Eight hundred words, five cuts, and one of them changes what a reader can know.*


**Read the two versions and answer.**

1. How many words went, and from where?
2. **Find three places in the short version where something has been left out and the reader
   rebuilds it. What do they rebuild it from?**
3. One cut changes what a reader can know. **Which, and how would you prove that?**
4. **Which version would you publish, and what would you put beside it?**

### 5F · Two texts

The filed version names two councillors and two dates.
The printed version says *the contract was approved*.

**Is the printed version untrue?** Write two sentences, and use the word *concision* in one of
them.

## Part 6 · Vocabulary Lab 2 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### Words that stand in for other words

### 6A · Sort — a noun, a clause, a second speaker, or a place?

Four columns.

> neither · so · elsewhere · likewise · one · such · ones · either

### 6B · Chunk completion

1. She cut it, and I would have ______ ______ in her place. *(two words, formal)*
2. He asked me to file and I ______ ______ . *(two words, neutral)*
3. The two versions are not ______ ______ . *(two words)*
4. Is it down to length? ______ ______ , send it. *(two words)*
5. ______ ______ , we hold it for the second edition. *(two words)*
6. The paragraph is unreadable and, ______ ______ , the first to go. *(two words)*
7. Of the two quotes, ______ ______ carries the argument. *(two words — the first)*
8. ______ ______ merely agrees with it. *(two words — the second)*

### 6C · Three that are not interchangeable

> *do so* · *do it* · *did*

**Say which one belongs in a report, which one in conversation, and which one in between.**

### 6D · The phrasal verbs and the fixed expressions

> **leave out** · **stand in for** · **do without**
>
> **f** *so does mine* · **f** *neither did I* · **f** *I will if you will*

**One of these fixed expressions is an offer disguised as a condition.** Which, and what is the
speaker actually asking for?

### 6E · Contrast Clinic

Eight items. Rewrite each one as short as it can be and still be rebuilt.

1. She would not cut it, and I would not cut it either.
2. The first quotation carries the argument; the second quotation agrees with the first quotation.
3. I have cut both of the two quotations.
4. If it is down to length, send it to the stone.
5. The second quote can go and the third quote can go as well.
6. The paragraph is unreadable and because it is unreadable it goes first.
7. He asked me to file the story and I filed the story.
8. The long version is better than the short version.

## Part 7 · Pronunciation & Fluency Lab ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**


![Four spoken exchanges written out and then transcribed as they are said, with auxiliaries and modals reduced to single sounds.](figures/fig_b21_u08_p07_v10.png){width=6.6in}

*Four words, two beats — Written in full above, said at half the length below.*


### What you leave out, and where you stop

### 7A · Hear 🔊 Track 8.3

> *I will if you will.* → /aɪl ɪf juː ˈwɪl/
> *We are over by forty.* → /wɪər ˈəʊvə baɪ ˈfɔːti/
> *Nobody will miss it.* → /ˈnəʊbədi l ˈmɪs ɪt/

### 7B · Say

Say each one three times, fast. Then say this, pausing only where the slash is:

> *The second can go, / the third can go, / and the fourth stays.* — three clauses, two pauses,
> and the last one is where the meaning is.

### 7C · Make it mean something

Say these two:

> *If this goes — what is the story for?* — the pause before the question does the work
> *If this goes what is the story for?* — no pause, and it is a complaint, not a question

**A pause is a punctuation mark you can hear,** and in an elliptical sentence it is often the only
thing telling the listener that something has been left out.

### 7D · Fluency 30 ⏺

Thirty seconds: **four exchanges, each second turn in four words or fewer, each with one pause.**
Three times, faster.

## Part 8 · Writing ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

### Two versions and one note · 200 words

### 8A · Read the model

> The contract was approved on 14 March by seven votes to two. `[the plain sentence, and nothing
> has been left out that a reader would want]`
>
> Two councillors voted for it twice: once in committee and once in the chamber. The former had
> declared an interest; the latter had not. `[the former and the latter carry two whole noun
> phrases, and the sentence is half the length it would otherwise be]`
>
> The reporter asked both to comment. One did; one did not. `[two clauses of three words each,
> and the reader rebuilds comment from the sentence before]`
>
> The printed version of this story says only that the contract was approved. It is not untrue, and
> nobody will miss the seventy-three words unless they go looking — which, as such, is the whole
> argument. `[as such points back to the entire preceding clause, which is what it is for]`

**Questions about the writer's choices:**

1. The second sentence uses *the former* and *the latter*. **What would repeating the names cost?**
2. *One did; one did not.* **What is the reader rebuilding, and from where?**
3. The last sentence calls its own brevity the argument. **Is that fair? Say why in one sentence.**

### 8B · The anti-model

> The report was published and the report was criticised. The councillors who voted for the contract were the councillors who had attended the committee meeting. The committee meeting took place in March and the chamber vote also took place in March.

Three sentences, every noun phrase repeated in full, and not one substitution anywhere.
**Rewrite it using *one*, *the former*, *the latter*, *do so* and *likewise*, and count the words
you saved.**

### 8C · Plan

> the plain sentence → the two-part sentence held together by *the former* and *the latter* →
> two short clauses the reader completes → the point, pointed back at with *as such*

### 8D · Write — 200 words
### 8E · Peer review — three criteria

> ☐ Can every ellipsis be rebuilt, and from something earlier in your own text?
> ☐ Is each substitution pointing at exactly one thing?
> ☐ Did you cut anything a reader needed, and how would you know?

### 8F · Write it again · 8G · Publish

Rewrite after review. **Publish both, and mark every place where something has been left out.**

---

## Part 9 · The File ◆ **A · THE WORLD + C · THE PERFORMANCE**


![A study sheet of five learner sentences with repeated noun phrases, the substitution a speaker would use, and the number of words saved.](figures/fig_b21_u08_p09_v05.png){width=6.6in}

*Five sentences a learner says in full — Accurate, long, and audible as learner speech from across the room.*


# STUDY FILE · The words you are afraid to leave out

### 9A · Saying all of it 🔊 Track 8.4

Learners repeat whole noun phrases where a native speaker would substitute, because the full form
feels safer and the short one feels like a gap in their English. The result is accurate, long, and
immediately audible as learner speech.

| | |
|---|---|
| **what you say** | *I like the blue book more than the red book* |
| **what a speaker says** | *I like the blue one better* |
| **what it signals** | not an error — a register |
| **the fix** | four forms, learned as chunks: *one · so · neither · the same* |

### 9B · Halve it

Take three sentences you have said this week with a repeated noun phrase.
**Rewrite each with a substitution, and say both out loud.**

### 9C · Role play — four words or fewer

In pairs. One of you makes a statement. The other agrees or disagrees in **four words or fewer**.
**Twelve rounds, and no form twice.**

### 9D · The one you avoid

Most learners use *one* freely and avoid *so* and *neither* entirely.
**Name the one you avoid, and use it four times today.**

### 9E · Transfer — before next week

**Record ninety seconds of yourself, then say the same thing in thirty.** Bring both, and the list
of what the short one left for the listener to rebuild.

## Part 10 · Mediation & Interaction ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**


![An infographic of a newspaper edit showing the filed length, the printed length, each cut in order and what readers could say afterwards.](figures/fig_b21_u08_p10_v12.png){width=6.6in}

*Fourteen hundred words, and where they went — The whole edit on one scale, from the filed copy to the hole on page one.*


### 10A · Relay

Half the class has the filed copy. Half has the printed version.
**Produce a list of what was cut, by speaking only.** Ten minutes.

### 10B · Simplify — for one reader

A reader writes: *Who actually voted for this?*
**Reply in four sentences,** using at least three substitutions.

### 10C · Online

Write **one message** to a reporter whose story is six hundred words over. **Three sentences.** Name
what you would cut and what it would cost.

### 10D · Your language

Does your language leave out the verb in a short answer, as English does in *Dev has*?
**Translate all six exchanges from Part 2.** Which second turns get longer?
