## Part 11 · The Decision ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

# What happens to the eight hundred words

**Option one:** nothing. The printed story is the story, every edition since 1911 has been an
edited version, and telling readers what they are not getting is a strange way to sell a newspaper.

**Option two:** publish the full filed version online and link to it from the print story.

**Option three:** publish the full version online **with every cut marked**, and say in the print
story that it is there.

### 11A · The evidence


![A bar chart comparing four publication formats on the share of readers who could name who decided and the share who finished the story.](figures/fig_b21_u08_p11_v06.png){width=6.6in}

*Four ways of publishing the same story — What readers could say afterwards, and how many of them got to the end.*


🔊 **Track 8.6.** Dennis Hoy, subeditor, 30 seconds: *"I have cut about four hundred thousand words
out of other people's stories. Most of them deserved it. The ones that did not were never the
beautiful sentences; writers fight for those. They were always seventy-three words of names and
dates that nobody fights for, because fighting for them sounds like vanity, and because they are
genuinely the dullest thing on the page."*

### 11B · Talk about it

In threes. Use the frame, and keep every turn under fifteen words.

> *I would ______ , because the reader cannot ______ . — So would I. / Neither would I.*

### 11C · Decide

Write **three sentences**: the decision, the evidence, and the cut you would defend hardest.

### 11D · Model decision

> Option three. The full version alone is unread — six hundred words is what people have time for,
> and a link to fourteen hundred is a link nobody follows. Marking the cuts turns the long version
> into something you can skim for what is missing, which is a different and much shorter act of
> reading. The cost is that the desk's judgement becomes public, and the desk is right most of the
> time and will be visibly wrong occasionally.

### 11E · The Reveal

> **What happened.** The Herald published the marked version. Readers of it rose over a year from
> almost nobody to about a fifth of the print readership — far more than the plain full version had
> ever drawn.
>
> Then something nobody predicted: reporters began filing shorter. Seeing, week after week, which
> of their paragraphs survived, they stopped writing the ones that did not. Average filed length
> fell by nineteen per cent in a year, and the number of paragraphs the desk cut for repetition
> fell by half.

**One question. You do not have to answer it out loud.**

> Nobody told the reporters to write less. What did marking the cuts actually show them?

---

## Part 12 · Landing ◆ **all three lines**

### 12A · Recycle — fifteen items

> 1. — Who has filed? — Dev ______ .
> 2. — Have you cut both? — I ______ .
> 3. — The second can go. — ______ can the third.
> 4. — I would not cut it. — ______ would I.
> 5. — Is it down to length? — If ______ , send it.
> 6. She cut it and I would have ______ ______ . *(two words, formal)*
> 7. Of the two, ______ ______ carries the argument. *(two words)*
> 8. ______ ______ merely agrees with it. *(two words)*
> 9. The paragraph is unreadable and, ______ ______ , goes first. *(two words)*
> 10. ______ ______ ______ took twenty minutes. *(three words)*
> 11. ______ ______ ______ ______ . *(four words — the subeditor, at 17:40)*
> 12. ______ ______ ______ ______ ______ . *(five words — the subeditor, at 18:00)*
> 13. It is four hundred words over; we must ______ ______ ______ . *(three words)*
> 14. Two whole paragraphs will have to be ______ ______ . *(two words)*
> 15. The colour can be ______ ______ to a single line. *(two words)*

### 12B · Consolidate — one task, everything in it

**Write 150 words, then cut it to 75 without losing any fact.** Mark every substitution and
ellipsis in the short version.

### 12C · Can-do, with evidence

> ☐ I can leave out what a reader can rebuild. **Evidence:** 3H, 8D
> ☐ I can substitute with *one*, *so*, *such*, *do so* and *neither*. **Evidence:** 6E, 9C
> ☐ I can say what a cut has cost. **Evidence:** 5E, 10C
> ☐ I can pause where the ellipsis is. **Evidence:** 7C

### 12D · Replay ⏺

Record 0C again. **Compare.** What did your twenty-second version leave the listener to rebuild?

### 12E · Glossary — fifty items, as a map

> **THE TEXT, AT A STAGE** · draft · proof · abstract
> **WHAT YOU DO TO IT** · edit · cut · trim
> **WHAT YOU JUDGE IT BY** · redundancy · verbosity · concision
> **WHAT SITS AROUND IT** · caption · standfirst · kicker
> **SIX O'CLOCK** · a first pass · kill a paragraph · tighten it up · the word count · down to length
> **WHAT YOU END UP WITH** · an edited version · a working draft · the final cut · a hard cut · what survives
> **WHAT SUBEDITORS DO** · cut down · chop out · pare back · leave out · stand in for · do without
> **IN THE ROOM** · it can go · we are over by forty · nobody will miss it
> **THE SUBSTITUTES** · one · ones · so · such · neither · either · likewise · elsewhere
> **THE FRAMES** · do so · do it · the same · if so · if not · as such · the former · the latter
> **AGREEING SHORT** · so does mine · neither did I · I will if you will

### 12F · Next

> **Unit 9 · Who is this written for?**

> Student's Book pages 182–203 · 22 pp · ~11 hours

## Part 0 · The Big Picture ◆ **A · THE WORLD**


![A newsroom conference area with a 214-page style guide on the table, a newsletter draft on a screen, an app preview held up and a page proof showing the fold.](figures/fig_b21_u09_p00_v01.png){width=6.6in}

*The style guide meeting, half past eleven — Twelve things to find. Three of them are the same sentence written for three readers.*


# Who is this written for?

**By the end of this unit you can guide a reader through a text, and choose the signposts that
match where they are reading it. Two hundred words.**

| ◆ **A · THE WORLD** | One style guide written in 1911, three channels, and an argument about the word *so* |
|---|---|
| ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM** | discourse markers, the whole system · 28 words for desks and deadlines · 22 connectors and frames · what a marker does to the tune |
| ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE** | Two hundred words a reader never gets lost in |

---

### 0A · Find it

**Find twelve things in this picture.** Three of them are the same sentence written for three
different readers.

> the style guide, 214 pages · the newsletter draft on a screen · the app preview, held up ·
> the page proof, above the fold · the news list for the day · the running order, pinned ·
> the spike, with two stories on it · the wire feed, scrolling ·
> a note that says WHO IS THIS FOR? · the conference table, empty ·
> a clock showing 11:30 · a subscriber count: 61,000

### 0B · Guess it

**One screen in this room has more readers than the newspaper.** Which, and which document on the
table does not mention it anywhere?

### 0C · Say it ⏺

**Record sixty seconds.** *Explain how you got to work or college today, and signpost it so the
listener always knows where in the story you are.* You will record it again at the end of this
unit.

## Part 1 · Vocabulary Lab 1 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**


![A cutaway of a newsroom in four layers, from where copy arrives through who touches it and where it ends up, to the documents that govern all of it.](figures/fig_b21_u09_p01_v02.png){width=6.6in}

*One newsroom, in section — Twelve words, and the layer each one lives on. Copy falls from the top.*


### 1A · Meet — the words of a newsroom

Match each word to one layer of the cutaway.

> **desk** · **deadline** · **spike** · **slot** · **splash** · **subeditor** · **copytaster** ·
> **galley** · **conference** · **style guide** · **house style** · **wire**

### 1B · Sort — a person or a group, a place or a time, or a document?

Three columns. **One of the twelve is also a verb, and it is the verb nobody wants done to their
story.**

> galley · desk · wire · splash · subeditor · copytaster · conference · spike · slot ·
> house style · style guide · deadline

### 1C · Meet — the five places a story can be

| | |
|---|---|
| **above the fold** | The top half of page one. The only part visible on a newsstand. |
| **the back of the book** | The pages nobody turns to, where good writing goes to be unread. |
| **on the stone** | Made up, laid out, and almost impossible to change. |
| **the second edition** | Another chance, four hours later, for a fifth of the readers. |
| **a slow news day** | When a story runs that would not have run on Tuesday. |

### 1D · Collocation Lab

Complete each one, and say which of the five is a measure of a person rather than a story.

> **a late change** · **hold the front page** · **the news list** · **a running order** ·
> **filed on time**

1. ______ ______ ______ goes round at nine and is argued about until eleven thirty.
2. The newsletter has ______ ______ ______ ; the paper has a page.
3. Nobody has said ______ ______ ______ ______ in this building since 1994.
4. ______ ______ ______ after six costs money and is sometimes worth it.
5. She has ______ ______ ______ every day for nine years, which is rarer than it sounds.

### 1E · The phrasal verbs, and the three fixed expressions

> **send through** · **turn around** · **sit on**
>
> **f** *we are going with it* · **f** *that is a splash* · **f** *it will not make the paper*

**Two of these fixed expressions are decisions and one is a judgement.** Which is the judgement,
and who in the room is allowed to make it?

### 1F · Own it

Write three sentences about your week, and put one signpost at the front of each so a listener
knows what kind of sentence is coming.

## Part 2 · Listening ◆ **A · THE WORLD**


![A subeditor and a newsletter writer across a conference table with the style guide and a phone between them, each with two speech balloons and a thought balloon.](figures/fig_b21_u09_p02_v07.png){width=6.6in}

*The style guide meeting — He has the guide. She has sixty-one thousand people reading on a bus.*


### 2A · The style guide meeting 🔊 Track 9.1

**Before you listen.** The style guide was written in 1911 and last fully revised in 1998. The
newsletter has sixty-one thousand subscribers. The app has more readers than the paper.

**Listen once.** What does each of them want the guide to say about discourse markers?

**Listen again. Complete the table.**

| | Markers per thousand words | Who the reader is | What they say it costs |
|---|---|---|---|
| the paper | | | |
| the newsletter | | | |
| the app | | | |

**Listen again. True or false?**

1. The guide was last fully revised in 1998. ☐ T ☐ F
2. The newsletter has more subscribers than the paper has readers. ☐ T ☐ F
3. The print desk wants markers banned entirely. ☐ T ☐ F
4. The app version is made by cutting the print version. ☐ T ☐ F

**Noticing.** Six sentences from the recording.

1. *However, the figures do not support that.*
2. *That said, the figures do not support that.*
3. *The figures, as it happens, do not support that.*
4. *Having said that, the figures do not support that.*
5. *By contrast, the figures do not support that.*
6. *In any case, the figures do not support that.*

**Six markers, one sentence. Which two concede something, which one announces a comparison, and
which one says the argument no longer matters?**

### 2B · Decoding clinic 🔊 Track 9.2

Thirty seconds at speed. Three jobs.

1. One speaker puts *however* in three different positions in three sentences. Write all three.
2. What happens to the pauses around it each time?
3. Somebody says *the point being*. What has just been left unsaid, and why does it work?

## Part 3 · Grammar Lab 1 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### Signposts, and what each one promises

### 3A · Notice

Look at the six sentences in Part 2 again.

1. Which two could swap with no change of meaning?
2. Which one implies the speaker has just agreed to something?
3. Sentence 3 puts the marker in the middle. **What does that do to the emphasis?**
4. Sentence 6 dismisses the previous point rather than answering it. **How do you know?**

### 3B · See


![A branching diagram from one clause into six discourse markers, each with the relationship it promises and an example of the clause that must follow.](figures/fig_b21_u09_p03_v09.png){width=6.6in}

*One sentence, six promises — Each marker tells the reader what kind of clause is coming. Break the promise and they stop.*


### 3C · Know

| | | |
|---|---|---|
| **contrast** | *however · nevertheless · by contrast · on the other hand* | the next clause pushes back |
| **concession** | *that said · having said that · in the first place* | you grant something, then continue |
| **result** | *accordingly · hence · thereby · therefore* | the next clause follows from the last |
| **sequence** | *to begin with · thereafter · meanwhile · which brings me to* | position in a list or a timeline |
| **dismissal** | *in any case · for one thing · as it happens* | the previous point is set aside |
| **legal and formal** | *whereby · thereby · thereafter* | written only, and mostly in contracts |

> **A marker is a promise about the next clause.** If you write *however* and the next clause does
> not contrast with anything, the reader has been misdirected, and they will not trust the next
> one either.

> **Position changes force, not meaning.** *However, the figures do not support that* is a formal
> announcement. *The figures, however, do not support that* is quieter and more confident. *The
> figures do not support that, however* sounds like an afterthought, which is sometimes exactly
> what you want.

| | |
|---|---|
| **managing a conversation** | *move on · come back to · go on to* |
| **holding the floor** | *the point being · which brings me to · for one thing* |
| **conceding and continuing** | *having said that · that said · in any case* |

> **Why it matters.** A reader who always knows what kind of sentence is coming next will follow
> you a long way. One who has been promised a contrast and given an example will stop reading, and
> will not be able to tell you why.

### 3D · Use — controlled

Complete with the marker the relationship requires.

> The app has more readers than the paper. (1) ______ , it has no style guide of its own. *(contrast)*
> The guide was revised in 1998. (2) ______ , it says nothing about newsletters. *(result)*
> (3) ______ , the markers were counted. Then the readers were surveyed. *(first in a sequence)*
> The newsletter is informal. (4) ______ , it is the most read thing the paper produces. *(concession)*
> You may disagree with the count. (5) ______ , the survey is independent of it. *(dismissal)*

### 3E · Use — guided

Rewrite each one, moving the marker to the middle and then to the end, and say how the force changes.

1. However, the figures do not support that.
2. Nevertheless, the newsletter is read to the end.
3. Accordingly, the guide will be rewritten.
4. Meanwhile, the app version went out unchanged.
5. By contrast, the print version carries nine markers.

### 3F · Use — open

**In pairs.** One of you makes a claim. The other must respond beginning with a marker that
promises a specific relationship, **and then keep that promise**. **Eight rounds, no marker twice.**

### 3G · Error autopsy

> ✗ *However, the figures do not support that, and also they do not support it.*
> ✗ *The newsletter is informal, hence it is popular, hence people read it, hence it works.*
> ✗ *Whereby the guide was revised in 1998.*

For each, name the rule and say what the writer was reaching for.

### 3H · Check — eight items

> 1. ______ , it has no style guide of its own. *(one word, contrast)*
> 2. ______ , the guide says nothing about newsletters. *(one word, result)*
> 3. ______ ______ ______ , the markers were counted. *(three words, first in a sequence)*
> 4. ______ ______ , it is the most read thing the paper produces. *(two words, concession)*
> 5. ______ ______ ______ , the survey is independent of it. *(three words, dismissal)*
> 6. ✗ *Whereby the guide was revised in 1998.* → correct it.
> 7. Move *however* to all three positions in one sentence and rank them by force.
> 8. Write one sentence in which *nevertheless* would be wrong, and say why.

## Part 4 · Speaking ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

### 4A · Fluency starter — keep the promise

One person says a marker. The next must produce a sentence that the marker correctly introduces.
**Anybody may challenge; the challenge is settled by the rest of the group.**

### 4B · The functional core — guiding a reader


![Two versions of the same paragraph side by side, the print version with few markers and the newsletter version with many.](figures/fig_b21_u09_p04_v04.png){width=6.6in}

*The same paragraph, two readers — One is read on paper, where the shape is visible. One is read on a phone.*


**Language Bank**

| | |
|---|---|
| **opening** | *to begin with · in the first place · for one thing* |
| **turning** | *however · by contrast · on the other hand · that said* |
| **continuing** | *meanwhile · thereafter · which brings me to · go on to* |
| **closing it off** | *in any case · accordingly · the point being* |

**Information gap.** Learner A has the print version. Learner B has the newsletter version.
**Agree on one version for the app, and say which markers you kept and why.** Ten minutes.

### 4C · The performance — three minutes ⏺

Explain an argument with three stages in three minutes. **Every stage must open with a marker, and
every marker must be kept.** Your partner raises a hand the moment a promise is broken.

**Record it.**

### 4D · Observer

One person writes down every marker and ticks it if the next clause did what it promised.

### 4E · Three criteria

> ☐ Did every marker promise something the next clause delivered?
> ☐ Did I vary the position, or put them all at the front?
> ☐ Could my partner say, at any moment, which stage we were in?

## Part 5 · Reading ◆ **A · THE WORLD**

### 5A · Predict

The title is **"The word *so*, and who is allowed to use it."**
**Why might a rule written in 1911 have trouble with a newsletter?**

### 5B · Read — two minutes

---

**The word *so*, and who is allowed to use it**

The Herald's style guide runs to two hundred and fourteen pages. It was written in 1911 and last
fully revised in 1998. It contains no entry for *newsletter*, *app*, or *push alert*.

Its entry for discourse markers is four lines long and says that they should be used sparingly.
Counted across a week, the paper averages nine per thousand words. The newsletter averages
thirty-one. The app sits between them at fourteen, not by policy but because the app version is
produced by cutting the print version.

The newsletter has sixty-one thousand subscribers, three quarters of whom open it. The paper
prints a hundred and twelve thousand copies. The app had three hundred and forty thousand readers
last month.

In the meeting the print desk's argument was that *so* at the start of a sentence is a verbal tic.
The newsletter writer's argument was that it is a signpost, that her readers are reading on a
phone between two other things, and that a reader who loses their place in a newsletter does not
come back.

Both are right, and the guide cannot say so, because it has one voice and the paper now has three
readers.

---

### 5C · Comprehension — eight questions

1. How long is the style guide, and when was it last fully revised?
2. What does it say about discourse markers?
3. What are the three marker densities?
4. Why does the app sit between the other two?
5. How many people open the newsletter?
6. **Why does the writer point out that the app density is not a policy?**
7. **"Both are right, and the guide cannot say so." What is the structural problem being described?**
8. **The last sentence has three readers and one voice in it. What is the writer proposing without proposing it?**

### 5D · Vocabulary in context

Infer from the text, then check.

> used sparingly · not by policy but because · reading on a phone between two other things ·
> does not come back · has one voice

### 5E · The counter-text


![Three versions of the same paragraph annotated with their discourse markers, the kind of each, and the one marker whose promise is broken.](figures/fig_b21_u09_p05_v05.png){width=6.6in}

*One paragraph, three channels — The same facts, three densities, and one marker that promises what it does not deliver.*


**Read the three versions of the same paragraph and answer.**

1. How many markers are in each, and of which kinds?
2. **Find one marker in the newsletter version that promises something the next clause does not
   deliver.**
3. The app version was made by cutting the print version. **What happened to its signposting?**
4. **Which version would you read on a phone, and which on paper, and are they the same one?**

### 5F · Two texts

The guide says markers should be used *sparingly*.
The newsletter writer says a reader who loses their place *does not come back*.

**Can both be house style?** Write two sentences, and use *house style* in one of them.

## Part 6 · Vocabulary Lab 2 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### The connectors, and what each one promises

### 6A · Sort — contrast, result, time, or formal only?

Four columns.

> however · nevertheless · meanwhile · thereafter · thereby · whereby · accordingly ·
> hence

### 6B · Chunk completion

1. ______ ______ ______ ______ , the guide was written for one reader. *(four words — first)*
2. ______ ______ , it has lasted a hundred and fourteen years. *(two words — concession)*
3. ______ ______ ______ , the newsletter outsells the paper. *(three words — an aside)*
4. ______ ______ ______ ______ , nobody has read all of it. *(four words — one reason among several)*
5. ______ ______ ______ , the app has no guide at all. *(three words — the other side)*
6. ______ ______ , the count was done independently. *(two words — comparison)*
7. ______ ______ ______ , the meeting is on Thursday. *(three words — it does not matter now)*
8. ______ ______ ______ ______ , we have no policy for phones. *(four words — restating the start)*

### 6C · Three that are not interchangeable

> *however* · *nevertheless* · *that said*

**Say which one simply contrasts, which one contrasts in spite of something, and which one grants
the other person a point first.**

### 6D · The phrasal verbs and the fixed expressions

> **move on** · **come back to** · **go on to**
>
> **f** *having said that* · **f** *the point being* · **f** *which brings me to*

**One of these fixed expressions is incomplete on purpose.** Which, and what does leaving it
unfinished let the speaker do?

### 6E · Contrast Clinic

Eight items. Rewrite each pair as one sentence with the marker the relationship requires.

1. You may dispute the count. The survey is separate. *(dismissal)*
2. The guide was revised in 1998. It says nothing about newsletters. *(result)*
3. The rule was agreed. Every channel now states its own density. *(formal result, written)*
4. The print desk objected. The newsletter went out unchanged. *(simultaneous)*
5. The guide has one voice. The paper has three readers. *(formal contrast, written)*
6. The newsletter is informal. It is the most read thing the paper makes. *(concession)*
7. The markers were counted. The readers were surveyed. *(sequence)*
8. The app has more readers. It has no style guide. *(contrast)*

## Part 7 · Pronunciation & Fluency Lab ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**


![Four pitch contours showing however at the front, in the middle and at the end of a sentence, and so said as a signpost and as a challenge.](figures/fig_b21_u09_p07_v10.png){width=6.6in}

*Where the marker sits, and what the voice does — The same word in three positions, and the same word as a challenge.*


### A marker has its own tune

### 7A · Hear 🔊 Track 9.3

> *However,* ↗ *the figures do not support that.* ↘ — the marker rises, then a pause
> *The figures, however,* → *do not support that.* ↘ — level, and almost swallowed
> *The figures do not support that, however.* ↘↘ — tacked on, and lower than everything

### 7B · Say

Say all three. Then say this:

> *That said — and I do take the point — the figures do not support it.* — two markers, three
> pauses, and the voice does not fall until *it*.

### 7C · Make it mean something

Say these two:

> *So, the guide was written in 1911.* — a signpost: here comes the background
> *SO the guide was written in 1911?* — a challenge: you are summarising to object

**An initial marker said on a level tone is a signpost; said with a jump, it is an argument.**
The word is identical; the tune decides which one the listener hears.

### 7D · Fluency 30 ⏺

Thirty seconds: **one argument in three stages, each opened by a marker, each marker in a different
position.** Three times, faster.

---

## Part 8 · Writing ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

### A guided argument · 200 words

### 8A · Read the model

> To begin with, the guide was written for one reader, who bought a paper on the way to work in
> 1911. `[the opening marker sets up a list, and the reader now expects at least a second item]`
>
> It has been revised eleven times since; nevertheless, it still has one voice. `[nevertheless,
> not however: the contrast holds in spite of the eleven revisions, which is a different claim]`
>
> The newsletter, as it happens, is read by more people than buy the paper — which brings me to
> the question the guide cannot answer. `[an aside in mid-position, then a marker that hands the
> reader forward instead of back]`
>
> Accordingly, the proposal is not a second guide but one rule: each channel states its own marker
> density, and no marker may promise a relationship the text does not have. `[accordingly earns
> its place because what follows really does follow]`

**Questions about the writer's choices:**

1. *Nevertheless* rather than *however*. **What does the extra meaning buy?**
2. *As it happens* sits in the middle of its sentence. **Why not at the front?**
3. The last sentence uses *accordingly*. **Would the paragraph survive without it?**

### 8B · The anti-model

> So, the guide is old. However, it is still used. Moreover, it has been revised. Nevertheless, people ignore it. Furthermore, the newsletter is popular. Therefore, something should be done.

Six sentences, six markers, and not one of them describes the relationship it sits between.
**Rewrite it with three markers, each one true.**

### 8C · Plan

> an opening marker that starts a list → a concession that holds in spite of something → an aside
> in mid-position, then a marker that hands the reader forward → a result that really follows

### 8D · Write — 200 words
### 8E · Peer review — three criteria

> ☐ Does every marker describe the relationship it actually sits between?
> ☐ Is at least one marker somewhere other than the front of its sentence?
> ☐ Could a reader say, at any point, what kind of sentence is coming next?

### 8F · Write it again · 8G · Publish

Rewrite after review. **Publish both, and mark every broken promise in the first version.**

## Part 9 · The File ◆ **A · THE WORLD + C · THE PERFORMANCE**


![A study sheet showing the three discourse markers most learners use, the four relationships they cannot express, and one marker to add from each group.](figures/fig_b21_u09_p09_v05.png){width=6.6in}

*And, but, so - and the four groups they do not cover — Fluent and flat: the listener follows the words and not the shape.*


# STUDY FILE · The three markers you own

### 9A · And, but, so 🔊 Track 9.4

Most learners at this level run a whole argument on three markers, and the three are nearly always
*and*, *but* and *so*. Everything else is understood when heard and never produced. The result is
fluent and flat: the listener can follow the words and not the shape.

| | |
|---|---|
| **what you have** | *and · but · so* |
| **what it signals** | addition, contrast, result — the three coarsest relationships |
| **what is missing** | concession, sequence, aside, dismissal |
| **the smallest useful fix** | one marker from each missing group, used this week |

### 9B · Add four

Choose **one** marker from each of the four missing groups.
**Write one true sentence with each, about your own week, and say them aloud.**

### 9C · Role play — the promise

In pairs. One of you speaks for a minute. The other raises a hand every time a marker promises
something the next clause does not deliver. **Swap.**

### 9D · The one you overuse

Everybody has one. Record yourself for two minutes and count.
**If any single marker is more than a third of your total, that is this month's work.**

### 9E · Transfer — before next week

**Take a paragraph you have written and re-signpost it with four different markers.** Bring both
versions and ask somebody which one they could summarise.

---

## Part 10 · Mediation & Interaction ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**


![An infographic comparing a newspaper, its newsletter and its app on readership, marker density and completion, against a style guide that covers only one of them.](figures/fig_b21_u09_p10_v12.png){width=6.6in}

*One guide, three channels, three readers — Who reads what, how dense the signposting is, and how many get to the end.*


### 10A · Relay

Half the class has the print version. Half has the newsletter.
**Produce one app version both sides accept, by speaking only.** Ten minutes.

### 10B · Simplify — for one reader

A subscriber writes: *Why does the newsletter sound like a different newspaper?*
**Reply in four sentences,** using one marker from each of the four groups.

### 10C · Online

Write **one message** to a colleague whose draft has eleven markers in three hundred words.
**Three sentences.** Keep two of theirs and say which.

### 10D · Your language

Where do markers sit in your language — front, middle, or end?
**Translate all six sentences from Part 2.** Which positions does your language not allow?

---

## Part 11 · The Decision ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

# One voice or three

**Option one:** one house style, as now. The paper's voice is the paper's voice, and a reader who
meets it in three places should recognise it in all three.

**Option two:** three style guides, one for each channel, each written by the people who write that
channel.

**Option three:** one guide, with a single new rule: each channel states its own marker density,
and no marker anywhere may promise a relationship the text does not have.

### 11A · The evidence


![A bar chart comparing four discourse-marker densities on the share of readers who finished the text and the share who could state its main point.](figures/fig_b21_u09_p11_v06.png){width=6.6in}

*Four signposting densities, one paragraph — Six hundred readers, the same four hundred words, four marker counts.*


🔊 **Track 9.6.** Nkechi Adeyemi, newsletter, 30 seconds: *"I am not asking to write badly. I am
asking for permission to tell sixty-one thousand people, on a phone, on a bus, which bit is
coming next. On paper you can see the shape of the thing — you know there are four more
paragraphs because you can see them. On a phone there is no shape. The markers are the shape."*

### 11B · Talk about it

In threes. Use the frame, and open every turn with a marker you then keep.

> *To begin with, ______ . That said, ______ . Which brings me to ______ .*

### 11C · Decide

Write **three sentences**: the decision, the evidence, and the thing it will not fix.

### 11D · Model decision

> Option three. Three guides is three voices and, within two years, three papers. One guide with
> one voice is already failing, because the voice was designed for a reader who could see how long
> the article was. The density clause is administrative and will be argued about; the second
> clause is the one that matters, and it applies identically on paper and on a phone, because a
> broken promise costs a reader everywhere.

### 11E · The Reveal

> **What happened.** The Herald adopted the single rule. The density bands were set at nine, fourteen
> and thirty-one, and were quietly ignored within six months.
>
> Then something nobody predicted: print marker density rose, from nine to seventeen. Writers who
> had assumed markers were forbidden discovered that what was forbidden was the false ones, and
> print comprehension scores rose with the count — on paper, where everybody had agreed the
> problem did not exist.

**One question. You do not have to answer it out loud.**

> The rule was written to give the newsletter permission. Why did the newspaper change more?

> 7. ______ ______ , it has lasted a hundred and fourteen years. *(two words, concession)*
> 8. ______ ______ ______ , the newsletter outsells the paper. *(three words, an aside)*
> 9. ______ ______ ______ , the meeting is on Thursday. *(three words, it no longer matters)*
> 10. ______ ______ ______ ______ ______ . *(five words — handing the reader forward)*
> 11. ______ ______ ______ ______ . *(four words — conceding and continuing)*
> 12. ______ ______ ______ . *(three words — holding the floor, unfinished)*
> 13. Can you ______ ______ the file before six? *(two words)*
> 14. We can ______ ______ two thousand words by Thursday. *(two words)*
> 15. They will ______ ______ it until the second edition. *(two words)*

**Write 150 words of argument with four markers**, one from each group, one of them in
mid-position. Mark each promise and tick it if you kept it.

> **PLACES AND TIMES** · slot · splash · spike · galley · deadline

> **WHERE A STORY CAN BE** · above the fold · the back of the book · on the stone · the second edition · a slow news day
> **THE DAY'S PAPERWORK** · a late change · hold the front page · the news list · a running order · filed on time
> **WHAT DESKS DO** · send through · turn around · sit on · move on · come back to · go on to
> **IN THE ROOM** · we are going with it · that is a splash · it will not make the paper
> **THE CONNECTORS** · however · nevertheless · meanwhile · accordingly · hence · thereby · whereby · thereafter
> **THE FRAMES** · in the first place · that said · for one thing · as it happens · to begin with · by contrast · in any case · on the other hand
> **HOLDING THE FLOOR** · having said that · the point being · which brings me to

*The news desk, ten to ten — Twelve things to find. Two of them are the same ward and one of them is not named.*


# Whose city is in the headline?

**By the end of this unit you can put a sentence in marked order for effect, and say what naming a
place in a headline does to it. Two hundred words.**

| ◆ **A · THE WORLD** | Eighty-one headlines, one ward, and a naming rule nobody ever wrote down |
|---|---|
| ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM** | inversion for emphasis, the full range · 28 words for place, tenure and framing · 22 negative and restrictive adverbs and frames · emphatic contours |
| ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE** | Two hundred words in marked order, on purpose |

named.

> the style guide, open at DATELINES · the front page from Tuesday ·
> the culture page from the same week · a photograph of the main street ·
> a tally chart in two colours · the tenure figures from the census ·
> a clock showing 09:50 · a window with the ward in it

**Two pages in this room describe the same six streets in the same week.** Which two, and which one
names them?

would. Say what changed.* You will record it again at the end of this unit.

*One neighbourhood, in section — Twelve words, and the layer each one belongs to. Only the top layer is written by the paper.*

Match each word to one layer of the cutaway.

> **zoning** · **tenure** · **boundary** · **ward** · **demographic** · **stereotype** ·
> **resident**

Three columns. **Two of the twelve are words residents almost never use about the place they live,
and both of them are used about it constantly.**

> framing · displacement · neighbourhood · headline · resident · stereotype · zoning ·
> gentrification · boundary · tenure · ward · demographic

### 1C · Meet — the five phrases a place acquires

| | |
|---|---|
| **the north end** | A name used by everybody except the people who live there. |
| **a problem neighbourhood** | A phrase that describes coverage, not a place. |
| **long-term residents** | Used in planning stories; never in crime stories. |
| **the same six streets** | What eighty-one pieces about a ward of forty thousand actually cover. |
| **an outsider's view** | The only kind most readers have, and the only kind most pieces are. |

Complete each one, and say which of the five decides what a reader remembers.

> **move in** · **price out** · **build over**
>
> **f** *nobody from there was asked* · **f** *that is not what it is called* ·
> **f** *whose city is this?*

**One of these fixed expressions is a correction and two are accusations.** Which is the

## Part 2 · Listening ◆ **A · THE WORLD**


![A news editor and a long-standing resident across a desk with a headline log and a ward map between them, each with two speech balloons and a thought balloon.](figures/fig_b21_u10_p02_v07.png){width=6.6in}

*Ten to ten, with the letter on the table — She has counted eighty-one headlines. He has never seen the rule written down.*


### 2A · The naming rule 🔊 Track 10.1

**Before you listen.** A resident has written to the paper. She has counted eighty-one headlines
over two years. The news editor has never seen the rule written down, because it never was.

**Listen once.** What has she counted, and what does the paper say its rule is?

**Listen again. Complete the table.**

| | Ward named in the headline | Resident quoted | What the section is |
|---|---|---|---|
| crime | | | |
| planning | | | |
| culture | | | |

**Listen again. True or false?**

1. She counted eighty-one headlines. ☐ T ☐ F
2. The rule is written in the style guide. ☐ T ☐ F
3. The ward is named in most planning headlines. ☐ T ☐ F
4. The culture pages have never named it. ☐ T ☐ F

**Noticing.** Six sentences from the recording.

1. *Never before had anybody counted them.*
2. *Not once did the culture pages name it.*
3. *Only when the story is about crime do we use the ward.*
4. *No sooner had the letter arrived than the editor asked for the figures.*
5. *So rarely does the ward appear in good news that readers assume there is none.*
6. *Little did we know that the rule had never been written down.*

**Every one of these puts the verb before the subject. Write each one in ordinary order, and say
what is lost.**

### 2B · Decoding clinic 🔊 Track 10.2

Thirty seconds at speed. Three jobs.

1. Two sentences begin with a negative adverb. Mark where the voice jumps in each.
2. What happens to the stress on the fronted word?

## Part 3 · Grammar Lab 1 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### Putting the verb first, and why you would

### 3A · Notice

Look at the six sentences in Part 2 again.

1. In sentence 1, what comes before the subject, and what has happened to the auxiliary?
2. Sentence 3 inverts the second clause, not the first. **Why that one?**
3. Sentence 4 has two clauses and only one inversion. **Which, and what joins them?**
4. Sentence 6 is a fixed expression. **Could you say *little did they know* about yourself?**

### 3B · See


![Two sentence diagrams of the same statement, the first in ordinary order and the second with the negative adverb fronted and the auxiliary inverted.](figures/fig_b21_u10_p03_v09.png){width=6.6in}

*The verb comes forward, and the sentence changes weight — The same fact, twice. Nothing is added and one word has moved to the front.*


### 3C · Know

| | | |
|---|---|---|
| **negative adverb first** | *never · rarely · seldom · nowhere · little* | *Never had she seen it.* |
| **near-negative first** | *hardly · scarcely · no sooner* | *Hardly had it arrived when…* |
| **restrictive first** | *only when · only then · only if · not until* | *Only then did we count them.* |
| **absolute negatives** | *at no point · under no circumstances · not once* | *Not once did they name it.* |
| ***so* + adjective or adverb** | *so rarely does · so great was* | *So rarely does it appear that…* |
| ***such*** | *such was the* | *Such was the reaction that…* |

> **Inversion is the written language's way of shouting.** It costs nothing in words and it cannot
> be used twice in a paragraph without sounding theatrical, which is the whole constraint.

> **The auxiliary does the inverting, and if there isn't one, *do* is supplied.** *They never
> counted them* becomes *Never did they count them*, not *Never counted they them*. This is the
> same machinery as a question, which is why the pattern feels familiar and the register does not.

| | |
|---|---|
| **the fixed frames** | *never before had · not once did · only when* |
| **what writers do with a place** | *set out · single out · point up* |
| **the degree words** | *rarely · seldom · scarcely · hardly · little · only* |

> **Why it matters.** Marked order tells a reader that this sentence is the one to remember. Use it
> where the emphasis is real and it is the strongest device in the language; use it where it is not
> and the reader hears a performance.

### 3D · Use — controlled

Rewrite each one with the bracketed word at the front and the verb inverted.

> Anybody had counted them before. (1) *(never before)*
> The culture pages did not name it. (2) *(not once)*
> We use the ward when the story is about crime. (3) *(only when)*
> The letter arrived and the editor asked for figures. (4) *(no sooner)*
> The ward appears in good news so rarely that readers assume there is none. (5) *(so rarely)*

### 3E · Use — guided

Put each one back into ordinary order and say what is lost.

1. Never before had anybody counted them.
2. Not once did the culture pages name it.
3. Only then did the editor ask for the figures.
4. At no point was a resident consulted.
5. Such was the reaction that the rule was rewritten.

### 3F · Use — open

**In pairs.** One of you states a fact about your own town. The other restates it inverted, **and
then says whether the emphasis is deserved.** **Six rounds each.**

### 3G · Error autopsy

> ✗ *Never before anybody had counted them.*
> ✗ *Not once the culture pages named it.*
> ✗ *Only when the story is about crime we use the ward.*

For each, name the rule and say what the writer was reaching for.

### 3H · Check — eight items

> 1. ______ ______ ______ anybody counted them. *(three words + inversion)*
> 2. ______ ______ ______ the culture pages name it. *(three words + inversion)*
> 3. ______ ______ the story is about crime ______ we use the ward. *(restrictive)*
> 4. ______ ______ ______ the letter arrived than the editor asked. *(three words)*
> 5. ______ ______ ______ it appear in good news that readers assume there is none. *(three words)*
> 6. ✗ *Never before anybody had counted them.* → correct it.
> 7. Rewrite *They never asked a resident* with *at no point*.
> 8. Write one inverted sentence about your own town that you could defend in print.

## Part 4 · Speaking ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

### 4A · Fluency starter — one inversion each

Everybody states one fact. Everybody else may invert it **once**. **The group votes on whether the
emphasis was earned.**

### 4B · The functional core — writing for rhetorical effect


![Two newspaper pages side by side from the same week, a crime story naming the ward in the headline and a culture story about the same streets that does not.](figures/fig_b21_u10_p04_v04.png){width=6.6in}

*The same week, two sections — Both pieces are about the same six streets. One of them says so.*


**Language Bank**

| | |
|---|---|
| **opening with a negative** | *never before had · not once did · at no point* |
| **restricting** | *only when · only then · under no circumstances* |
| **intensifying** | *so rarely does · such was the · little did* |
| **naming a place** | *the local paper · an outsider's view · that is not what it is called* |

**Information gap.** Learner A has the crime headlines. Learner B has the culture headlines.
**Agree on one sentence describing both, and use exactly one inversion in it.**

Argue a case about your own town in three minutes. **You may use inversion exactly twice.** Your
partner notes where you used it and whether a third would have helped or hurt.

**Record it.**

### 4D · Observer

One person marks each inverted sentence and writes beside it what was being emphasised.

### 4E · Three criteria

> ☐ Did each inversion mark the sentence that mattered most?
> ☐ Did I supply *do* where there was no auxiliary?
> ☐ Did two inversions sound deliberate, or did they sound like a speech?

## Part 5 · Reading ◆ **A · THE WORLD**

### 5A · Predict

The title is **"Eighty-one headlines."**
**If a newspaper names a neighbourhood in its crime headlines and not elsewhere, what does a
reader learn about the neighbourhood?**

### 5B · Read — two minutes

---

**Eighty-one headlines**

The letter ran to a page and a half and contained a table. Over two years the Herald published
eighty-one pieces mentioning Ward 7. The writer, a resident of thirty-one years, had sorted them
by section and counted how often the ward appeared in the headline.

In crime stories it appeared in seventy-one per cent of headlines. In planning stories, eighteen.
In business, six. Not once did it appear in a culture headline, although eleven culture pieces
over the two years were about events in the ward, including the reopening of a hall the paper
itself had campaigned to save.

There is no rule in the style guide about naming a ward. Nobody in the building could produce one.
Asked, three editors gave three answers, and all three were about specificity: a crime happens
somewhere, and readers want to know where.

The letter's last paragraph points out that a concert also happens somewhere.

The ward is forty thousand people and eleven thousand households, of which fifty-eight per cent
are rented. The six streets around the Lake and Seventh junction account for sixty-two of the
eighty-one pieces. The writer lives four streets away and has never been quoted in any of them.

---

### 5C · Comprehension — eight questions

1. How many pieces mentioned the ward, and over how long?
2. What were the four percentages?
3. How many culture pieces were about events in the ward?
4. What did three editors give as the reason?
5. How many people live in the ward?
6. **Why does the writer mention the hall the paper campaigned to save?**
7. **"A concert also happens somewhere." What argument is being made in six words?**
8. **The last sentence is about the letter-writer herself. What does ending there do?**

### 5D · Vocabulary in context

Infer from the text, then check.

> sorted them by section · all three were about specificity · account for sixty-two of the
> eighty-one · four streets away · has never been quoted in any of them

### 5E · The counter-text


![A headline log annotated by section showing how often a ward is named in the headline, how often a resident is quoted, and the names used in place of the ward.](figures/fig_b21_u10_p05_v05.png){width=6.6in}

*Eighty-one headlines, sorted by section — Where the ward is named, where it is not, and what it is called instead.*


**Read the headline log and answer.**

1. How many headlines name the ward, and in which sections?
2. **Find the two headlines about the same week and say which one names the place.**
3. One headline uses a postcode. **What does that do that a ward name does not?**
4. **Rewrite three headlines so that the naming is consistent across sections.**

### 5F · Two texts

The style guide has no rule about naming a ward.
The log shows seventy-one per cent in crime and nought in culture.

**Is there a rule?** Write two sentences, and use the word *framing* in one of them.

## Part 6 · Vocabulary Lab 2 ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**

### The words that trigger marked order

### 6A · Sort — how often, how nearly not, where, or how restricted?

Four columns.

> only · little · rarely · nowhere · seldom · never · hardly · scarcely

### 6B · Chunk completion

1. ______ ______ had anybody counted them. *(two words + had)*
2. ______ ______ did the culture pages name it. *(two words + did)*
3. ______ ______ the story is about crime do we use the ward. *(two words)*
4. ______ ______ had the letter arrived than the editor called. *(two words)*
5. ______ ______ ______ was a resident consulted. *(three words)*
6. ______ ______ ______ ______ will a postcode be used alone. *(four words)*
7. ______ ______ ______ it appear in good news that readers assume there is none. *(three words)*
8. ______ ______ ______ reaction that the rule was rewritten in a week. *(three words)*

### 6C · Three that are not interchangeable

> *hardly* · *rarely* · *only*

**Say which one is about frequency, which one is about degree, and which one restricts the whole
clause.**

### 6D · The phrasal verbs and the fixed expressions

> **set out** · **single out** · **point up**
>
> **f** *never before had* · **f** *not once did* · **f** *only when*

**One of these three phrasal verbs is what the letter-writer accuses the paper of doing to her
ward.** Which, and what would the paper say it was doing instead?

### 6E · Contrast Clinic

Eight items. Rewrite each one in marked order, beginning with the word in brackets.

1. The culture pages did not name it once. *(not once)*
2. A resident was not consulted at any point. *(at no point)*
3. The letter arrived and the editor called immediately. *(no sooner)*
4. We use the ward only when the story is about crime. *(only when)*
5. We did not know the rule had never been written. *(little)*
6. Nobody had counted them before. *(never before)*
7. The reaction was so great that the rule changed. *(such)*
8. It appears in good news so rarely that readers assume there is none. *(so rarely)*

## Part 7 · Pronunciation & Fluency Lab ◆ **B · THE SYSTEM**


![Four pitch contours over inverted and uninverted sentences, showing where the voice jumps on the fronted adverb and where a flat delivery loses the emphasis.](figures/fig_b21_u10_p07_v10.png){width=6.6in}

*Marked order needs the jump that goes with it — Inversion without the pitch jump sounds like a mistake. The two travel together.*


### Marked order has a marked tune

### 7A · Hear 🔊 Track 10.3

> *NEVer before had anybody counted them.* — the jump is on the first word
> *Not ONCE did the culture pages name it.* — and here on the second
> *Only when it is CRIME do we name it.* — the jump waits for the content word

### 7B · Say

Say each one three times. Then say this:

> *Never before had anybody counted them — and not once did anybody ask why.* — two inversions in
> one breath, two jumps, and the second must be lower than the first or it sounds like a speech.

### 7C · Make it mean something

Say these two:

> *We rarely name it.* — flat, ordinary, and forgettable
> *RARELY do we name it.* — the jump on *rarely*, the verb inverted, and it is now the sentence
> the reader repeats

**Inversion without the pitch jump sounds like a mistake,** and the jump without inversion sounds
like emphasis in the wrong place. The two travel together or not at all.

### 7D · Fluency 30 ⏺

Thirty seconds: **three inverted sentences, each with one jump, each jump lower than the last.**
Three times, faster.

## Part 8 · Writing ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

### A case about a place · 200 words

### 8A · Read the model

> Over two years the Herald published eighty-one pieces mentioning Ward 7, of which sixty-two
> concerned the same six streets. `[ordinary order, and the figures do the work; nothing is marked
> yet]`
>
> Not once did the ward appear in a culture headline. `[the first inversion, on the sentence that
> is the point of the piece]`
>
> Eleven culture pieces over the same period were about events there, including the reopening of a
> hall this paper campaigned to save. `[back to ordinary order immediately; the marked sentence
> only works if it is surrounded by unmarked ones]`
>
> So rarely does a neighbourhood appear in good news that its readers come to believe there is
> none — and such was the response to this letter that the rule, which had never been written down,
> was written down in a week. `[two marked structures in the last sentence, where the piece is
> allowed to raise its voice because it has not done so before]`

**Questions about the writer's choices:**

1. The first sentence is unmarked. **What does that buy the second one?**
2. The third sentence returns to ordinary order. **Why not keep going?**
3. The last sentence uses two marked structures. **Would three have worked?**

### 8B · The anti-model

> Never before has a community been so ignored. Not only is the coverage unfair, but so biased is the framing that no resident can recognise themselves in it. Rarely has a newspaper so comprehensively failed. At no point has anybody listened.

Four sentences, five inversions, and not one number anywhere.
**Rewrite it with one inversion and four facts.**

### 8C · Plan

> the figures, unmarked → one inversion, on the point → back to ordinary order, with the
> concrete example → the close, where you may raise your voice once

### 8D · Write — 200 words
### 8E · Peer review — three criteria

> ☐ Is every inversion on a sentence that deserves the emphasis?
> ☐ Are the marked sentences surrounded by unmarked ones?
> ☐ Does the auxiliary invert correctly, with *do* supplied where needed?

### 8F · Write it again · 8G · Publish

Rewrite after review. **Publish both, and mark every inversion in each.**

---

## Part 9 · The File ◆ **A · THE WORLD + C · THE PERFORMANCE**


![A study sheet comparing the version learners give a stranger with the version they would give a neighbour, and what is lost from the first.](figures/fig_b21_u10_p09_v05.png){width=6.6in}

*Two versions of where you are from — The stranger's version is efficient, accurate, and the only one most people keep.*


# STUDY FILE · How you describe where you are from

### 9A · The outsider's version 🔊 Track 10.4

Asked where they are from, most learners give the version a stranger needs: the country, the
nearest big city, and a comparison. It is efficient, it is accurate, and after four years abroad it
is often the only version they still have.

| | |
|---|---|
| **the outsider's version** | *a small city, about two hours from the capital* |
| **what it leaves out** | the name, the street, the thing only you would say |
| **why it survives** | it has never once been questioned |
| **the test** | describe it to somebody who has been there |

### 9B · Say it twice

Describe your place twice: **once for a stranger and once for a neighbour.**
Write down three things that appear only in the second.

### 9C · Role play — the follow-up question

In pairs. One describes a place. The other may only ask *what is it actually called?* and *who
lives there?* **Four rounds each.**

### 9D · The sentence you have said two hundred times

Everybody has one fluent, worn sentence about home.
**Say yours, then replace every general word in it with a specific one** and say it again.

### 9E · Transfer — before next week

**Write four sentences about where you are from, one of them inverted.** Read all four to somebody
and ask which one they remember.

---

## Part 10 · Mediation & Interaction ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**


![An infographic of two years of coverage of one ward showing how often it was named in headlines by section, how often residents were quoted, and what changed afterwards.](figures/fig_b21_u10_p10_v12.png){width=6.6in}

*Eighty-one headlines, four sections, six streets — Where the ward is named, who is quoted, and what readers took from it.*


### 10A · Relay

Half the class has the crime headlines. Half has the culture headlines.
**Produce one naming rule both halves accept, by speaking only.** Ten minutes.

### 10B · Simplify — for one reader

The letter-writer asks: *So is there a rule or isn't there?*
**Reply in four sentences,** with one inversion and three numbers.

### 10C · Online

Write **one message** to a colleague whose headline names a ward in a crime story. **Three
sentences.** Ask about the other sections, not about their headline.

### 10D · Your language

Does your language mark emphasis by moving the verb, or by something else?
**Translate all six sentences from Part 2.** What does your language move instead?

---

## Part 11 · The Decision ◆ **C · THE PERFORMANCE**

# The naming rule

There is no rule. There has been a practice since at least 1994.

**Option one:** keep it. A crime happens in a place and readers are entitled to know which place.
Specificity is the job.

**Option two:** name a neighbourhood only when the story is about the neighbourhood, and not when
it is about one event that happened inside it.

**Option three:** name it everywhere — in culture, in business, in planning, in sport — at the same
rate as in crime, and write the rule down.

### 11A · The evidence


![A bar chart comparing four newspaper sections on how often a ward is named in the headline and how often a resident is quoted in the piece.](figures/fig_b21_u10_p11_v06.png){width=6.6in}

*Naming and quoting, by section — Two years, eighty-one pieces, four sections of the same newspaper.*


🔊 **Track 10.6.** Priya Raghavan, news editor, 30 seconds: *"I have defended the crime practice
for eleven years and I would defend it again, because it is right. A crime happens somewhere. What
I cannot defend is the other four sections, and nobody has ever asked me to, because nobody ever
noticed that the defence only works if you apply it everywhere. We applied it in one place. That
is not a rule. That is a habit with a reason attached afterwards."*

### 11B · Talk about it

In threes. Use the frame, and invert exactly once per person.

> *I would ______ , because ______ . Only when ______ would I ______ .*

### 11C · Decide

Write **three sentences**: the decision, the evidence, and one inverted sentence you would stand
behind in print.

### 11D · Model decision

> Option three. Option two is the comfortable answer and it makes the ward disappear, which is what
> the letter is complaining about in the first place. The defence of naming is sound and it has
> only ever been applied where the news was bad. Applying it everywhere costs nothing, writes the
> rule down, and is the only version that can be checked a year from now by counting, which is how
> this started.

### 11E · The Reveal

> **What happened.** The Herald wrote the rule down and applied it to every section. In the
> following year Ward 7 appeared in a hundred and forty headlines instead of eighty-one, and the
> crime share of those headlines fell from seventy-one per cent to twenty-nine.
>
> Then something nobody predicted: readers outside the ward, asked to free-associate on its name,
> produced a list in which crime words fell from first to fourth. Nothing had changed in the ward.
> Six hundred more headlines had changed, and all of them were true.

**One question. You do not have to answer it out loud.**

> Every one of the eighty-one original headlines was accurate. What exactly was wrong?

---

## Part 12 · Landing ◆ **all three lines**

### 12A · Recycle — fifteen items

> 1. ______ ______ had anybody counted them. *(two words + had)*
> 2. ______ ______ did the culture pages name it. *(two words + did)*
> 3. ______ ______ the story is about crime do we name it. *(two words)*
> 4. ______ ______ had the letter arrived than the editor called. *(two words)*
> 5. ______ ______ ______ was a resident consulted. *(three words)*
> 6. ______ ______ ______ ______ will a postcode stand alone. *(four words)*
> 7. ______ ______ ______ it appear in good news. *(three words)*
> 8. ______ ______ ______ reaction that the rule changed in a week. *(three words)*
> 9. ______ ______ ______ we know it had never been written. *(three words)*
> 10. ______ ______ ______ ______ ______ ______ . *(six words — the resident's letter, line one)*
> 11. ______ ______ ______ ______ ______ ______ . *(six words — the correction nobody can answer)*
> 12. ______ ______ ______ ? *(three words — the question in the headline)*
> 13. The piece will ______ ______ three streets as the worst. *(two words)*
> 14. The figures ______ ______ what the headline hides. *(two words)*
> 15. Families have been ______ ______ of the six streets since 2016. *(two words)*

### 12B · Consolidate — one task, everything in it

**Write 150 words about how your own area is written about.** Use exactly two inversions and mark
them. Say, underneath, why those two sentences and not two others.

### 12C · Can-do, with evidence

> ☐ I can use the full range of inversion after negative and restrictive adverbs. **Evidence:** 3H, 8D
> ☐ I can judge when marked order is earned. **Evidence:** 4C, 6E
> ☐ I can say what naming a place does to a reader's picture of it. **Evidence:** 5E, 10B
> ☐ I can produce the pitch jump that marked order requires. **Evidence:** 7C

### 12D · Replay ⏺

Record 0C again. **Compare.** Which version did you give first — the stranger's or your own?

### 12E · Glossary — fifty items, as a map

> **THE PLACE AND ITS EDGES** · neighbourhood · ward · boundary · zoning
> **WHO LIVES THERE** · resident · tenure · demographic
> **WHAT HAPPENS TO THEM** · gentrification · displacement
> **WHAT THE PAPER ADDS** · framing · headline · stereotype
> **WHAT A PLACE GETS CALLED** · the north end · a problem neighbourhood · long-term residents · the same six streets · an outsider's view
> **THE DECISIONS** · named in the headline · a postcode in a headline · who is quoted · the first paragraph · the local paper
> **WHAT HAPPENS TO STREETS** · move in · price out · build over · set out · single out · point up
> **IN THE ROOM** · nobody from there was asked · that is not what it is called · whose city is this?
> **THE TRIGGER WORDS** · rarely · seldom · scarcely · hardly · nowhere · never · little · only
> **THE FRAMES** · not only · no sooner · only then · at no point · under no circumstances · so rarely does · such was the · little did
> **THE FIXED OPENINGS** · never before had · not once did · only when

### 12F · Next

> **B2.2 · *Holding the Floor* — a port and its logistics chain, Rotterdam**



# Answer Key

> Open tasks — speaking, writing, discussion — are not keyed. Their
> success criteria are in the unit, at 4E and 8E.

## B2.1 · Unit 1 · Forty Minutes Behind

**0B** · Guess it
The second screen showing the rival's site and the lawyer's number on the note. Both exist only because of the market ticker: a claim that moves a share price creates a race (somebody else may publish first) and an exposure (if it is wrong, the damage is measurable in money). The chain runs ticker → price-sensitive claim → rival screen and lawyer. Accept the wall clock in place of the ticker if the learner says why the two are the same object here.

**1B** · Sort — before publication, after publication, or both?
> **after publication** — correction · retraction
> **before publication** — source · embargo · corroboration · provenance · verification · evidence
> **both** — claim · attribution
**The two that move:** *claim* and *attribution*. A claim exists before publication and is what a correction is about afterwards; an attribution is decided before and defended after. *Embargo* is arguably both — accept it if the learner can describe a post-publication embargo.

**1D** · Collocation Lab
1 stand a story up · 2 run a correction · 3 off the record · 4 on the record · 5 hold the line  
**Not about publishing:** *stand a story up*. It describes work done on the evidence, and it is done whether or not anything is ever printed. Accept *off the record* with an argument: it describes the terms on which something was said, not what the paper does with it.

**1E** · The phrasal verbs, and the three fixed expressions
***Who else has it?*** It is not a request for information; everybody in the room already knows the answer. It is a request for permission. If a rival has the story, being wrong is shared and being late is not — so the question converts a judgement about evidence into a judgement about competitors, which is a different and much easier question.

**2A** · Fifteen forty 🔊 Track 1.1
**1** T · **2** F · **3** T · **4** F
**Table** — *the job cuts*: claimed, 400; corroborated, a screenshot and one caller; assumed, that the screenshot is of a genuine board paper. *the timing*: claimed, this week; corroborated, nothing; assumed, that a decision taken is a decision announced. *the board meeting*: claimed, Tuesday; corroborated, the date only; assumed, that the cuts were on the agenda.

**Noticing** — Sentence 2, *The company cuts four hundred jobs*, makes the strongest factual claim: the simple aspect presents the event as whole, closed and known. Sentence 3, *has been cutting jobs since March*, commits the paper to least: it asserts a process, not a number, and a process cannot be wrong by four hundred minus three hundred and ten.

**3D** · Use — controlled
1 is cutting · 2 is considering · 3 has been moving · 4 has been losing · 5 was going to announce

**3E** · Use — guided
1 The company is closing the Thunder Bay plant. *(a process under way, not a completed act)* · 2 The regulator is rejecting the application. *(no final decision asserted)* · 3 The minister is resigning. *(the resignation is happening, not finished)* · 4 Three directors were selling shares in the week before the announcement. *(activity, with no total claimed)* · 5 The union is accepting the offer. *(under way, and still reversible)*

**3H** · Check — eight items
1 has cut · 2 has been cutting · 3 has been changing · 4 had been going to announce · 5 *The report contains three errors.* *(contain is stative)* · 6 *We have known this since Tuesday.* *(know is stative; the duration goes on the perfect)* · 7 *Was cutting* presents the number as a process still running, and a running process has no final figure. The sentence asserts both that the number may still move and that it cannot. · 8 *The minister is resigning.* **or** *The minister is understood to be resigning.*

**5C** · Comprehension — eight questions
1 A single source, a screenshot, and a claim that four hundred jobs were going at a plant in Thunder Bay.  
2 16.00.  
3 Eleven years.  
4 15.52 — eight minutes before the close.  
5 Four per cent, in the last eight minutes of trading.  
6 Three hundred and ten, ninety fewer than the four hundred that was published.  
7 Because a record of being right is a fact about a person, and corroboration is a fact about a claim. The stringer's eleven years tell you how likely the claim is; they tell you nothing about this one. Treating the first as the second is the commonest failure in the room.  
8 The new question is whether being right a day late is a product anybody is buying. Nobody can answer it because the paper has never measured it: it knows what Tuesday's traffic was worth and has no figure at all for what its accuracy on Wednesday is worth.

**5D** · Vocabulary in context
**going at a plant** — being cut, in the informal sense of jobs disappearing · **filed it** — sent the story in to the desk · **a second account** — an independent version from somebody with no connection to the first · **no decision had been taken** — the passive with the decider removed, which leaves open that one was about to be · **two column centimetres** — the physical size of the correction, and the real measure of how seriously it was meant

**5E** · The counter-text
1 That the figure of four hundred was incorrect and the company has confirmed three hundred and ten. It is on page eleven, two column centimetres, below the fold.  
2 The original used *is cutting* — a process asserted as under way, with a number attached. The correction uses *had been reported as* — a past perfect passive, which places the error in the reporting rather than in the company, and attributes it to nobody.  
3 The original claim is nineteen words; the correction is thirty-one. The correction is longer and reaches a twentieth of the readers.  
4 It does not say who was wrong, when the paper knew, what the source was, or that the share price moved. It corrects a number and nothing else, which is the only part that cannot be argued with.

**6A** · Sort — closed, running, or unsettled?
> **closed** — settled
> **running** — ongoing · iterative · habitual · unfolding
> **unsettled** — emergent · provisional · momentary
*Habitual* is the awkward one: it describes something that recurs rather than something that runs, and a learner who puts it in a column of its own has seen the point. *Momentary* is unsettled in the sense that matters here — a momentary figure is one you cannot publish.

**6B** · Chunk completion
1 stands · 2 writing · 3 treated · 4 understood · 5 going · 6 thought

**6C** · Three that are not interchangeable
*is understood to* — attributes to an unnamed source the writer has actually spoken to. *is thought to have* — attributes to nobody at all; the thinking has no thinker. *is being treated as* — attributes to an institution's procedure: somebody has classified it and the writer is reporting the classification, not endorsing it.

**6D** · The phrasal verbs and the fixed expressions
***The situation is developing*** and ***we understand that***. The first asserts only that time is passing; the second attributes without naming, and can be written the moment anybody says anything. ***It now appears that*** requires a previous position to have been held and abandoned — it is a claim about the paper, not about the world, and a reader is entitled to ask what it appeared to be before.

**6E** · Contrast Clinic
> **1** The board **had been going to announce** it on Thursday.
> **2** The company **has been losing** money since March.
> **3** The company **has cut** four hundred jobs.
> **4** The plant **closed** last March.
> **5** The company **has been restructuring** since 2019.
> **6** The number **was changing** all day on Monday.
> **7** The regulator **has rejected** the application.
> **8** The company **is understood to be cutting** jobs. *(no aspect alone will carry this one — it needs the attribution frame)*

**12A** · Recycle — fifteen items
1 has cut · 2 has been cutting · 3 was changing · 4 had been going to announce · 5 As … stands · 6 is being treated · 7 is thought to · 8 stand a story up · 9 checks out · 10 run a correction · 11 bear out · 12 plays out · 13 hold the line · 14 off the record · 15 understands


---

## B2.1 · Unit 2 · We Have Always Said

**0B** · Guess it
The style guide, open at page 9, is the reason the leader-page proof carries no byline. The causation runs from the rule to the page, and not the other way: the rule was written first and the page is produced by it daily. The bound archive is what makes the rule expensive, because an unsigned institution can say *always* and an archive can date it.

**1B** · Sort — a person, an institution, or a group of readers?
> **an institution** — editorial · outlet · bureau · voice · masthead
> **a person** — columnist · correspondent
> **a group of readers** — constituency · audience · readership
**The two that sit in two columns:** *voice* — a columnist has one and so does the paper, and the unit is about the second borrowing the first; and *constituency* — a group of readers, and also a group somebody claims to speak for, which is the political sense and the reason the word is in this list at all.

**1D** · Collocation Lab
1 an unsigned piece · 2 parachute journalism · 3 local to the story · 4 a named writer · 5 whose paper is it  
**The one that could be checked:** *local to the story*. Where somebody lives is a fact with an address. The other four are judgements, and two of them are judgements about judgements.

**1E** · The phrasal verbs, and the three fixed expressions
***We have always said*** and ***this paper believes*** assert. ***Who is the we here?*** interrogates. What the third one does to the other two is make them answerable: both assert a position on behalf of a set, and neither states the set. The question does not dispute the position; it asks for the membership list, which is the one thing neither sentence can supply.

**2A** · The leader conference 🔊 Track 2.1
**1** F · **2** F · **3** T · **4** T
**Table** — *2017*: the claim, the plan deserves a hearing; the archive, a leader of 14 March 2017 supporting it; the *we*, an editorial board of nine, two of whom are still in post. *2021*: the claim, no position stated; the archive, silence; the *we*, nobody. *today*: the claim, we have always opposed it; the archive, the opposite in 2017; the *we*, the institution since 1911, unspecifiable.

**Noticing** — Sentence 1, *We have always said this*, makes the claim about continuity: the present perfect with *always* asserts an unbroken state and supplies no date. Sentence 2, *We said this in 2017*, makes a claim about a point: the simple past requires the date, which is exactly why an institution with an archive prefers the first.

**3D** · Use — controlled
1 has said · 2 said · 3 will have held · 4 had not yet discussed · 5 might say

**3E** · Use — guided
1 *We have held this position since 2019.* **(point → continuity)** · 2 *We opposed the plan in a leader on 14 March 2019.* **(continuity → point)** · 3 *The paper supported it in 2018 and changed position at the inquiry.* **(past perfect → two dated points)** · 4 *By next spring we will have held this view for a decade.* **(the future perfect, correctly formed)** · 5 *The board has held that decision since March.* **(point → continuity)**

**3H** · Check — eight items
1 have said · 2 said · 3 had not discussed · 4 will have held · 5 *We said this in 2017.* *(a dated point takes the simple past; a present perfect cannot carry a finished-time adverbial)* · 6 *The paper has believed this since 1911.* *(believe is stative; the duration goes on the simple perfect)* · 7 *We have said this since March 2019* — or, better, *We said this in a leader on 14 March 2019 and have not changed position since.* · 8 *The editorial board decided on 14 March 2019 that…* — a person, or a body with a membership, and a date.

**5C** · Comprehension — eight questions
1 Eighty-one times in two years.  
2 Four kilometres.  
3 Nine.  
4 Two of the nine are the same person.  
5 That the ward has been neglected.  
6 Because *neglected* is a passive with the agent removed, and the paper is one of the candidates for that agent. The sentence is the whole argument of the piece, made by noticing an absence rather than by asserting anything.  
7 *Has now been quoted in four pieces* imports a past series into the present: it is not reporting four events, it is reporting a state the shopkeeper is now in, which is what makes the phrase *a local resident* read as a job description.  
8 Because the gap between what people think and what will happen to the figure is the subject. Ending on it leaves the reader holding the distinction rather than the conclusion, and the piece has no conclusion to offer.

**5D** · Vocabulary in context
**a bureau four kilometres away** — a staffed office near the ward but not in it · **described as a local resident** — labelled that way by the paper, repeatedly, without the label being checked · **neglected** — a passive with no agent, and the agent is the point · **filed from the bureau** — written without going to the place · **what it will be used as** — the readership figure will be cited as an argument against sending anybody again

**5E** · The counter-text
1 2017: the plan deserves a hearing. Today: we have always opposed it.  
2 2017 — *our readers in the east end* (a set that could be counted), *we will be asking* (the newsroom, about nine people). Today — *we have always opposed* (the institution, since 1911), *we have said so throughout* (unspecifiable; no set exists that makes it true).  
3 2017: none. Today: four. Each perfect imports a past position into the present argument without dating it, which is how a change of position becomes a continuity.  
4 A byline would make the sentence *we have always opposed* either false or absurd, because a named person who joined in 2020 cannot have always said anything. The byline does not improve the argument; it makes one sentence unwritable.

**6A** · Sort — includes the reader, excludes the reader, or avoids the question?
> **excludes the reader** — institutional · exclusive · corporate
> **includes the reader** — collective · inclusive · generic
> **avoids the question** — anonymous · impersonal
*Collective* is the arguable one: it says the group acts as one and says nothing about who is in it. Accept it in the third column if the learner makes that argument.

**6B** · Chunk completion
1 royal · 2 might · 3 held · 4 writer · 5 personally · 6 view

**6C** · Three that are not interchangeable
*in this paper* claims institutional authority — the paper is speaking. *as a reader* claims none — it puts the writer on the other side of the page. *the general view* claims a consensus, and is the only one of the three that could be checked and never is.

**6D** · The phrasal verbs and the fixed expressions
***We take the view that***. The plural commits an institution to a position, so only somebody entitled to speak for it can say it. You know because of what happens if the wrong person says it: *that is not our position* is the sentence that follows, and it can only be said by somebody further up.

**6E** · Contrast Clinic
> **1** **The business desk**, which made it, regrets the error.
> **2** **Dr Sollis**, who compiled them, says the figures are unreliable.
> **3** **Three residents** of the six streets were asked, by telephone, on one afternoon.
> **4** **I think** the plan will fail.
> **5** **The news desk** made two errors in the 14 March edition.
> **6** The board **decided on 14 March 2019** to oppose it.
> **7** The **editorial board** adopted a transparency policy on **9 May 2021**.
> **8** **Sixty-one per cent of 480 readers surveyed in March** said they expected better.

**12A** · Recycle — fifteen items
1 have said · 2 said · 3 had not discussed · 4 will have held · 5 royal · 6 One · 7 held · 8 writer · 9 Speaking · 10 believes · 11 Who is the we here · 12 our position · 13 stand in · 14 own up · 15 written up


---

## B2.1 · Unit 3 · A Doubling of Risk

**0B** · Guess it
The whiteboard with the base rate on it — four in ten thousand. It is the one figure that makes the headline decidable, and nobody has opened it because the release arrived at nine with a ratio already in its first line, and a ratio reads like a finding. Accept the preprint on screen if the learner argues that page eleven carries the same number and is forty pages in.

**1B** · Sort — counted in people, a relation between numbers, or a measure of doubt?
> **a measure of doubt** — confidence · significance · interval
> **a relation between numbers** — risk · hazard · correlation · causation
> **counted in people** — incidence · prevalence · baseline · exposure · cohort
**The two that are swapped daily:** *correlation* and *causation*. Both are relations between two numbers; only one of them licenses a sentence with *because* in it. *Baseline* is arguable — it is a count, and it exists only in order to be divided into, which is the second column's business. Accept either with the argument.

**1D** · Collocation Lab
1 a press release · 2 a preprint · 3 peer reviewed · 4 a causal link · 5 the margin of error  
**Checked by somebody with no stake in it:** *peer reviewed* — and the point of item 3 is that nothing here has been. Everything else on the sheet has been produced by people who benefit from it being interesting.

**1E** · The phrasal verbs, and the three fixed expressions
