***The evidence does not support that*** closes the door. The two that leave it open are *it may well be* and *we cannot rule it out*. Of those, *it may well be* is the more honest: it places the claim somewhere on the scale, low but real. *We cannot rule it out* says nothing about probability at all — it reports the absence of an exclusion, which is true of almost every claim ever made, and reads as support.

**2A** · The four o'clock conference 🔊 Track 3.1
**1** F · **2** F · **3** T · **4** T
**Table** — *the release*: the number, a doubling; what it is out of, not stated; how sure, highly significant and demonstrably linked. *the preprint*: the number, four to eight per ten thousand per year; what it is out of, ten thousand person-years; how sure, statistically significant, no causal claim, interval 1.2 to 3.3. *the epidemiologist*: the number, the same; what it is out of, the same; how sure, it may well be real and we cannot rule it out.

**Noticing** — The paper could defend sentence 3, *It may well be a real effect*, in court: it asserts possibility and the interval supports it. Sentence 1, *It must be a real effect*, is the bar sentence: it is the strongest inference on the scale and the data at an interval of 1.2 to 3.3 cannot carry it.

**3D** · Use — controlled
1 may / might / could · 2 must · 3 cannot have / could not have · 4 should · 5 may … or may not

**3E** · Use — guided
1 *Night shifts may be associated with the condition; this cohort cannot show that they cause it.* · 2 *The release must have been written by somebody who had not read the paper.* · 3 *It could hardly have been chance, at that interval.* · 4 *A replication could settle it, if anybody funds one.* *(a hope, not a forecast)* · 5 *The authors are said to disagree with the release.* *(sourced elsewhere, because you have not asked them)*

**3H** · Check — eight items
1 may / might / could · 2 must · 3 cannot have / could not have · 4 could hardly have · 5 may or may not · 6 *It must be a real effect.* *(a modal is followed by the bare infinitive)* · 7 *Night shifts are associated with the condition in this cohort; no causal claim is made.* · 8 *About four extra cases per ten thousand people a year, on a baseline of four.*

**5C** · Comprehension — eight questions
1 Sixty-one thousand Danish adults, followed for nine years.  
2 Four cases per ten thousand people per year.  
3 From 1.2 to 3.3.  
4 Three times.  
5 That they cannot establish a causal link, and that the cohort was not designed to.  
6 Because three hundred and ten words is enough room to give the base rate four times over. The length turns an omission into a choice.  
7 Both describe the same data. The ratio says how much the risk changed; the absolute figure says how much risk there was to change. Neither is wrong, and only the second lets a reader decide what to do.  
8 The constraint is not space — a headline can hold either figure. It is that only one of them will be read, and the desk is choosing which fact the reader walks away with.

**5D** · Vocabulary in context
**consistent with an effect of almost nothing** — the lower end of the interval is close to no effect at all · **just** — barely; the result scrapes the threshold · **designed to** — set up in advance to answer that question, which this one was not · **what it is out of** — the denominator; the base rate · **room for one of them** — the headline will carry one figure, and the choice is editorial

**5E** · The counter-text
1 Nowhere. The release contains no base rate and no absolute figure at all.  
2 *demonstrably* — claims a causal link the authors refuse; *highly significant* — uses *significant* in the lay sense of large, which is not what it means; *is said to be* — attributes to an unnamed somebody; *is bound to prompt* — a forecast at the top of the scale; *has yet to be peer reviewed* — true, accurate, and in the last line.  
3 The release leaves out the base rate, the absolute figures and the interval. The preprint leaves out nothing of substance; what it does not supply is a sentence a newsroom can use.  
4 A press office, which is measured on coverage obtained. Nobody in that building is measured on whether a reader could act on the number.

**6A** · Sort — the writer is unsure, somebody else said it, or the writer is committed?
> **the writer is unsure** — arguably · conceivably · presumably
> **the writer is committed** — demonstrably · unequivocally
> **somebody else said it** — reportedly · allegedly · ostensibly
*Ostensibly* is the one worth arguing about: it reports an appearance and quietly disputes it, so it is somebody else's claim plus the writer's doubt. *Presumably* is the other: it is an inference the writer is making, not a hedge about somebody else's.

**6B** · Chunk completion
1 should well / is likely to · 2 must surely be · 3 might well have · 4 could hardly have · 5 must surely be · 6 may or may not · 7 is said to · 8 has yet to be  
*(1 takes* is likely to *; 5 takes* must surely be *— accept* there is bound to be *for 5 if the learner keeps the word count.)*

**6C** · Three that are not interchangeable
*is likely to* gives a probability — it places the claim on the scale. *is said to* gives a source, and no probability at all. *has yet to be* gives a date that has not arrived: it says nothing about whether the thing is true, only that the step which would settle it has not happened.

**6D** · The phrasal verbs and the fixed expressions
*on the balance of probability* is the legal standard — more likely than not, and the civil test. *to the best of our knowledge* is the institutional limit — true as far as this organisation has looked. *that is overstating it* is the correction. A scientist would use the second about their own work, because it is the only one of the three that reports the boundary of what was checked.

**6E** · Contrast Clinic
> **1** Night shifts **are associated with** the condition in this cohort; no causal claim can be made from it.
> **2** The release **must have been written** by the press office.
> **3** Night work **is demonstrably associated** with four extra cases per ten thousand a year in one cohort.
> **4** The effect **could hardly have been** chance, at that interval.
> **5** The study **may be wrong**; I have read only the abstract.
> **6** The effect **may or may not** be real: the interval runs from almost nothing to three times the reported size.
> **7** It **could be replicated** next year, if anybody funds it.
> **8** The authors **are said to have approved** the release.

**12A** · Recycle — fifteen items
1 may / might / could · 2 must · 3 could hardly have · 4 may or may not · 5 has yet to be · 6 Presumably · 7 necessarily *(accept* demonstrably *with an argument)* · 8 The base rate · 9 a preprint · 10 On the balance of probability · 11 To the best of our knowledge · 12 That is overstating it · 13 scale up · 14 come down on · 15 hedge about


---

## B2.1 · Unit 4 · It Was Going to Open in 2019

**0B** · Guess it
The framed 2014 announcement and the budget board. Both were made on the same day in October 2014; the announcement still says $2.4 billion and spring 2019, and the board has been crossed out four times. The board is the one that has been quietly replaced — four times — which is why the two no longer agree and why only one of them is framed.

**1B** · Sort — made beforehand, the shape of the thing, or counted afterwards?
> **made beforehand** — forecast · scenario · projection · contingency · assumption
> **the shape and the distance** — trajectory · horizon · foresight
> **counted afterwards** — revision · hindsight · overrun · shortfall
**The pair looking in opposite directions:** *foresight* and *hindsight*. Same faculty, same person, and only one of them is ever claimed in advance. *Trajectory* is arguable in the first column — accept it if the learner says that a trajectory is drawn before anything has happened.

**1D** · Collocation Lab
1 The plan was to · 2 As things turned out · 3 With hindsight · 4 The gap between · 5 A missed deadline  
**Writable in 2014:** *the plan was to* — and only just, because the past tense already implies the plan has been superseded. The other four all require an ending the writer has already seen.

**1E** · The phrasal verbs, and the three fixed expressions
***Nobody saw it coming.*** It is almost always false when it is said, because somebody usually did and wrote it down — here, on page eleven of the 2016 contingency documents. What it does for the speaker is convert a failure of attention into a property of the world: if the thing was unforeseeable, nobody is answerable for not having foreseen it.

**2A** · The forecast audit 🔊 Track 4.1
**1** T · **2** F · **3** T · **4** F
**Table** — *the line*: it was going to open in spring 2019; it has been revised to 2023, 2027 and 2031; nothing has opened. *the budget*: $2.4 billion; revised downwards once in 2016 and upwards three times; the central estimate is now $6.1 billion. *the paper's forecast*: forty forecasts in five years; eleven came off; none of the thirty-one dated pieces mentioned the forecast it replaced.

**Noticing** — Sentence 3, *It would have opened in 2019, but for the harbour*, blames something: *but for* names the obstacle. Sentence 4, *It was to have opened in 2019*, is the most formal, and carries a reproach the writer never has to state. Sentence 2, *It was due to open in 2019*, is the plain report: a schedule, stated, with nobody blamed.

**3D** · Use — controlled
1 was due to · 2 was to have been · 3 was about to · 4 would have · 5 would later

**3E** · Use — guided
1 *The line was going to open in 2019.* · 2 *They were about to sign in March.* · 3 *The budget would later reach six point one billion.* · 4 *The paper would get one forecast in four right.* · 5 *The station model was to have been moved into the finished concourse.*

**3H** · Check — eight items
1 was due to · 2 was to have been · 3 were about to · 4 would have · 5 would later · 6 *It was going to open in 2019.* *(going to takes the bare infinitive)* · 7 *They were about to sign when the survey came back.* · 8 *The line would not open until 2031.* — or, standing in 2014, *It was to open in 2019 and would in fact open in 2031.*

**5C** · Comprehension — eight questions
1 October 2014; it was due to open in the spring of 2019.  
2 Two point four billion dollars; the central estimate is now six point one billion.  
3 Thirty-one.  
4 None.  
5 The harbour problem is modelled on page eleven, with a worst case of 2029.  
6 Because the model is the only part of the 2014 announcement that was actually delivered. It is a photograph of a thing that exists standing in for a thing that does not, and it is in a lobby where it will be seen daily.  
7 The writer sets a 2023 quotation against a 2016 document and lets the dates do the work. Saying it outright would be a judgement the reader could argue with; the two dates are facts the reader draws the judgement from, which is harder to dispute and impossible to forget.  
8 *Revised four times* is a count of events and sounds like diligence. *Downwards once and upwards three times* is a direction, and the direction is the finding.

**5D** · Vocabulary in context
**it was to have been** — formal future in the past, carrying a reproach the writer never states · **has since been revised** — changed more than once, with the revisers unnamed · **released under access law** — obtained by legal request, not volunteered · **of the second delay** — the quotation is about one delay out of several · **model the harbour problem** — set it out as a scenario, with numbers

**5E** · The counter-text
1 Read from today, every main verb in it is a future in the past: *will open* → was going to open; *is to be* → was to have been; *is to begin* → was due to begin; *will fall* → was going to fall.  
2 Spring 2019, March 2015, forty years, 11.3 km. Only the tunnel length still stands.  
3 *Subject to the completion of the harbour survey.* Eleven words, at the end of the second quotation. It was protecting the construction start date, and it is the one clause that has done its job.  
4 Four signatories. None of them is still in post, which is why the announcement can be quoted against the city and not against anybody in it.

**6A** · Sort — at the start, somewhere along the way, or looking from one end?
> **looking from one end** — retrospectively · prospectively
> **somewhere along the way** — eventually · ultimately · latterly · subsequently
> **at the start** — originally · formerly
*Eventually* and *ultimately* are arguably a column of their own — both name the end rather than a point along the way, and *ultimately* carries a judgement that *eventually* does not. Accept the split if the learner can state the difference.

**6B** · Chunk completion
1 was going to · 2 was due to · 3 were about to · 4 would have been · 5 had intended to · 6 was to have been · 7 would later · 8 As it would turn out

**6C** · Three that are not interchangeable
*was going to* reports a plan — somebody intended it. *was due to* reports a commitment — a schedule existed, often contractual, and somebody can be held to it. *was to have been* carries a complaint: it is formal, written, and says the thing did not happen without ever having to say so.

**6D** · The phrasal verbs and the fixed expressions
*back out* and *pull out* describe leaving an agreement; *call off* describes ending an event. Between the first two: *back out* implies a commitment already made and suggests fault; *pull out* is neutral about fault and is the word a company uses about itself.

**6E** · Contrast Clinic
> **1** Nobody **was expecting** a second delay, although the 2016 documents **had modelled** one.
> **2** **Retrospectively**, the paper got one forecast in four right.
> **3** It **was not going to open** for another seventeen years.
> **4** The line **was to have opened** before the harbour survey was completed.
> **5** The budget **would later reach** six point one billion.
> **6** The line **was due to open** in 2019.
> **7** The contractor **had intended to** stay on the project.
> **8** They **were about to sign**.

**12A** · Recycle — fifteen items
1 was due to · 2 was to have been · 3 were about to · 4 would have · 5 would later · 6 Originally · 7 Eventually · 8 Retrospectively · 9 As it would turn out · 10 It would be years before · 11 That was the plan · 12 In the event · 13 pulled out · 14 called off · 15 fell through


---

## B2.1 · Unit 5 · Clause Seven

**0B** · Guess it
The side letter, face down. It caps eleven named contributors' liability at five thousand dollars and the association was never told, so the clause the room has spent nine hours arguing about has already been suspended for the eleven people who file the hardest stories. It is face down because it was placed on the table by somebody who had just decided to stop defending it.

**1B** · Sort — part of the document, something one side gives up, or what happens when it goes wrong?
> **when it goes wrong** — remedy · arbitration · breach · settlement · precedent
> **something one side gives up** — concession · indemnity · waiver
> **part of the document** — undertaking · covenant · clause · term
**The one that is not in the document:** *precedent*. It sits outside the contract, in what courts have already decided, and it is the reason the other eleven are worded as they are. An *indemnity* is arguably in the first column too — it is a clause as well as a thing given up; accept it with the argument.

**1D** · Collocation Lab
1 a side letter · 2 terms and conditions · 3 a break clause · 4 a material breach · 5 to the extent that  
**What a court looks at first:** *the governing law* — which is not in this list, and that is the point of the question. Of the five, *a material breach* is the one a court reaches first, because it decides whether there is a case at all.

**1E** · The phrasal verbs, and the three fixed expressions
***That is not what we agreed*** ends a negotiation: it accuses the other side of bad faith and leaves nothing to discuss until that is answered. ***We can live with that*** restarts it — it accepts a term without endorsing it, and signals there is more to trade. ***It depends what you mean by*** buys time and a definition: it refuses the word rather than the position, which is the cheapest way of declining something.

**2A** · Clause seven, at seven twenty 🔊 Track 5.1
**1** T · **2** F · **3** T · **4** F
**Table** — *the paper*: offers a cap at the fee, conditional on full disclosure; it costs the paper an uncapped indemnity it has never invoked; it wants disclosure before publication. *the association*: offers acceptance of a disclosure duty; it costs contributors a reporting obligation; it wants the cap and the deletion of the side letters. *the side letter*: offers eleven people a five-thousand-dollar cap; it costs the paper nothing; it bought silence, and has just stopped working.

**Noticing** — Sentence 6, *Failing agreement tonight, the old contract rolls over*, is the threat: it states what happens if the other side does not move, and the roll-over favours the paper. Sentence 5, *Supposing nothing was disclosed — what then?*, is the hypothesis: it invites the other side to think a case through rather than imposing anything. Sentences 1 and 2 are almost the same: *provided* and *as long as* differ in register, not in meaning.

**3D** · Use — controlled
1 provided · 2 Failing · 3 But for · 4 Supposing · 5 otherwise

**3E** · Use — guided
1 *We will cap it provided you tell us first.* · 2 *Unless you tell us, the cap goes.* *(one exception named, not a consequence threatened)* · 3 *We will cap it only if you told us.* · 4 *Failing agreement tonight, the old contract continues.* · 5 *But for the disclosure rule, we would not have offered a cap.*

**3H** · Check — eight items
1 provided · 2 Failing · 3 But for · 4 Supposing · 5 otherwise · 6 *We will cap it provided you tell us first* — or, keeping *unless*: *Unless you tell us first, we will not cap it.* The original says the opposite of what it means. · 7 *Unless you tell us, the cap goes.* *(one exception, named in advance, instead of a consequence aimed at the other side)* · 8 *We will cap it only if you tell us.*

**5C** · Comprehension — eight questions
1 Four hundred and ten; nine pages.  
2 That a contributor will indemnify the paper, without limit, against any claim arising from their work.  
3 Three, all from work by staff reporters.  
4 Five thousand dollars.  
5 Eleven.  
6 That a clause only transfers risk if the person it transfers to could meet the claim. Nobody earning four thousand three hundred dollars a year can meet one, so the clause moves no money; what it moves is the willingness to file a story that might attract a claim.  
7 Because it is a fact about the room rather than about the eleven, and it is the only part of the paragraph nobody present was willing to contradict. Stating it directly would make it the writer's claim; stating it as an absence of dispute makes it everybody's.  
8 A paragraph of explanation would have turned a negotiating position into an argument the paper could answer. Four words of quotation leave the accusation unanswered and end the evening, which is what happened.

**5D** · Vocabulary in context
**without working for it** — as freelances, with no employment relationship · **corrosive in principle** — damaging to something other than the people it applies to · **has never been invoked** — never once used, in nine years · **placed face down on the table** — put into the room without being announced · **the hardest stories** — the ones most likely to attract a claim, and the reason the eleven were chosen

**5E** · The counter-text
1 Draft 4 contains one condition; the counter-proposal contains two.  
2 *notwithstanding* — keeps the clause alive after an editorial amendment, so an edit the paper made cannot be used against it. *provided* — a requirement the contributor can meet. *unless* — one exception, named in advance. *without limit* — not a condition at all; it is a refusal to set one, written as though it were a term.  
3 On full disclosure to a named editor before publication — a behavioural condition, not a financial one.  
4 The counter-proposal. Forty-one words, two conditions, one subclause, and it names the person who decides whether the condition has been met — which is the only part of either draft that tells a contributor what to actually do.

**6A** · Sort — a requirement, an exception, or a formal qualifier?
> **an exception** — absent · unless · otherwise · failing
> **a formal qualifier** — insofar · notwithstanding
> **a requirement** — provided · supposing
*Supposing* is the odd one in the first column: it states a requirement the way a hypothesis does, by inviting the other side to imagine it rather than imposing it. Accept a fourth column for it. *Failing* and *absent* are near-synonyms; *absent* is the more legal.

**6B** · Chunk completion
1 as long as · 2 on condition that · 3 In the event that · 4 But for · 5 Were it not for · 6 Given that · 7 Assuming that · 8 only if

**6C** · Three that are not interchangeable
*unless* states an exception — everything holds except in this one case. *only if* excludes everything else — this case and no other will satisfy it. *provided* states a requirement you expect to be met, which is why it is the connector a negotiator uses when they intend to sign.

**6D** · The phrasal verbs and the fixed expressions
***All other things being equal.*** They almost never are, and the speaker knows it: the phrase is a way of stating a preference while reserving the right to abandon it when the first other thing turns out to be unequal. It protects the speaker from being held to the sentence they have just said.

**6E** · Contrast Clinic
> **1** **Failing** agreement, the old contract continues.
> **2** The cap applies **on condition that** the piece was disclosed in full to a named editor before publication.
> **3** Tell us first; **otherwise** there is no cap.
> **4** We will cap it **only if** you tell us.
> **5** **Notwithstanding** the claims log, the clause stands.
> **6** **Supposing** nothing was disclosed — what is the paper's position then?
> **7** **But for** the disclosure rule, we would not have capped it.
> **8** **Unless** you tell us, the cap goes.

**12A** · Recycle — fifteen items
1 provided · 2 Unless · 3 But for · 4 Failing · 5 Supposing · 6 otherwise · 7 notwithstanding · 8 to the extent that · 9 subject to *(accept* without prejudice *only with an argument)* · 10 without prejudice · 11 in good faith · 12 That is not what we agreed · 13 hammer out · 14 draw up · 15 water down


---

## B2.1 · Unit 6 · What the City Did Was Borrow

**0B** · Guess it
Version A — the one with the point at word nineteen. You can tell from the room rather than from the sentence: it is the version in the bound file of back numbers, and it is the only one of the four on the whiteboard that is not in a cadet's handwriting. Accept any answer that reasons from where a version is rather than from how it reads.

**1B** · Sort — what the reader brings, what the teacher does, or what you are aiming at?
> **what the teacher does** — explanation · scaffolding · analogy · paraphrase · demonstration
> **what you are aiming at** — ambiguity · clarity · abstraction · emphasis
> **what the reader brings** — intuition · misconception · prerequisite
**The two seen from opposite ends of the room:** *intuition* and *misconception*. They are the same object — a belief the learner arrived with — judged by whether it happens to agree with the answer. *Abstraction* is in the last column as something you aim at and against: accept it in the second column if the learner argues it is a tool.

**1D** · Collocation Lab
1 The short answer · 2 A common misconception · 3 from first principles · 4 step by step · 5 To put it another way  
**The one that makes a claim about the reader:** *a common misconception*. It asserts what the reader probably believes before they have said anything, which is useful when it is right and insulting when it is not.

**1E** · The phrasal verbs, and the three fixed expressions
***Let me put it another way.*** It is a second attempt, and by the time it is said the first attempt has already failed in front of everybody. What the speaker should do instead is ask a question — find out which part did not land, rather than restating the whole thing in different words and hoping.

**2A** · The cadet course, ten past ten 🔊 Track 6.1
**1** T · **2** T · **3** F · **4** F
**Table** — *version A*: the point sits at word nineteen, inside a subordinate clause; the reader meets a nominalisation first; thirty-eight per cent could restate it. *version B*: the point sits at word four; the reader meets the fare box first; sixty-one per cent. *version C*: the point sits at word nine; the reader meets five words of empty frame first; seventy-four per cent.

**Noticing** — Sentence 3, *It was the fare box that the city borrowed against*, makes the fare box the news: the it-cleft puts it in focus and makes everything after *that* presupposed. Sentence 2, *What the city did was borrow against the fare box*, makes the borrowing the news, because the pseudo-cleft puts the whole verb phrase at the end.

**3D** · Use — controlled
1 *What the city did was borrow against the fare box.* · 2 *It was the fare box that the city borrowed against.* · 3 *The thing that was never mentioned was the fare box.* · 4 *What happened was that the city sold thirty years of ticket income.* · 5 *All they did was move the debt.*

**3E** · Use — guided
1 *It was in 2016 that the city borrowed against the fare box.* · 2 *It was without a vote that the council approved it.* · 3 *It was the transit board that nobody told.* · 4 *What the fare rise pays is the interest, not the line.* · 5 *What two of the nine members did was abstain.*

**3H** · Check — eight items
1 What … was · 2 It was … that · 3 The thing that · 4 What happened was · 5 All … was · 6 *What the city did was borrow.* *(the pseudo-cleft takes the bare infinitive after* was*)* · 7 *It was without a vote that they approved it.* · 8 *What they borrowed against was the fare box.*

**5C** · Comprehension — eight questions
1 The right to thirty years of ticket income.  
2 Nine hundred words.  
3 Thirty-eight per cent.  
4 That the line had been paid for by a fare rise.  
5 With that paragraph, which the cadets rewrite four ways.  
6 Because the paragraph is the evidence. Summarising it would let the reader assume it was badly written; printing it shows that it is not — every word is accurate and the sentence is properly built, which is the whole problem.  
7 By the standard the subeditors were trained on: no wasted words, no empty frames, no throat-clearing. The writer is implying that the measure is a measure of the sentence and the job is to move something into a reader's head, and that nobody has ever reconciled the two.  
8 It is the verdict, held back until the arguments have all been made and then given one line of its own so that it cannot be read as part of anything else.

**5D** · Vocabulary in context
**the security for a loan** — the asset a lender can seize if the loan is not repaid · **a range of instruments** — several different financial arrangements, unnamed · **by any measure they were trained on** — judged by the rules the subeditors learned · **repeat back** — restate from memory, which is the only test that matters here · **the version that works** — the one readers could restate, not the one that reads best

**5E** · The counter-text
1 A: word nineteen. B: word four. C: word nine. D: word fourteen.  
2 Version D, which opens *A fare rise did not pay for the east-end line*. It helps because most readers arrive holding exactly that belief, and a reader spending the first clause disagreeing with you cannot hear the second.  
3 B and D are both twenty-two words. B puts the focus early with an it-cleft and presupposes the rest; D spends its first clause clearing a misconception and then uses a pseudo-cleft. Same length, opposite strategies.  
4 Open. Version C has the best numbers and the worst reputation on the desk; version D is the one to choose if you believe the misconception is widespread, and it costs you five words and a flat opening sentence.

**6A** · Sort — narrowing to the item, ranking it, or going to the root?
> **ranking it** — notably · chiefly · crucially
> **narrowing to the item** — precisely · specifically · namely
> **going to the root** — essentially · fundamentally
*Notably* is the one to argue about: it singles a case out rather than ranking the whole set, so a learner who gives it a column of its own has seen something real. *Crucially* ranks by importance to the argument, not by size, which is why it can be used about a small thing.

**6B** · Chunk completion
1 What matters is · 2 Where it goes wrong is · 3 All I did was · 4 The thing that · 5 Where it goes wrong is · 6 What happened was · 7 That is why · 8 It was then that  
*(2 and 5 take the same frame at different lengths — accept* the reason is that *for 2 if the learner keeps the word count.)*

**6C** · Three that are not interchangeable
*namely* lists exactly — it names the members of the set it has just referred to. *notably* singles out one case from several, and implies the others exist. *essentially* strips a thing to its core, and always loses something in doing so, which is why it is the one that most often precedes an oversimplification.

**6D** · The phrasal verbs and the fixed expressions
*boil down to* and *come down to* are nearly interchangeable; *boil down to* implies a process of reduction the speaker has carried out, *come down to* implies the thing was always that simple. *point out* does what neither can: it directs somebody's attention to something already present, without reducing anything.

**6E** · Contrast Clinic
> **1** **It was nobody that** told the transit board. *(or, more naturally:* **The one body nobody told was** the transit board.*)*
> **2** **What the cadets do is** rewrite it four ways. *(or:* **It is four ways that** the cadets rewrite it.*)*
> **3** **What the subeditors hate is** the version that works.
> **4** **It was in 2016 that** the city borrowed against the fare box.
> **5** **It was without a vote that** the council approved it.
> **6** **It was the fare box that** the city borrowed against in 2016.
> **7** **What the fare rise pays is** the interest.
> **8** **What six hundred readers could do was** repeat it back.

**12A** · Recycle — fifteen items
1 What … was · 2 It was … that · 3 The thing that · 4 What happened was · 5 All … was · 6 Essentially · 7 Specifically · 8 Chiefly · 9 The short answer · 10 A common misconception · 11 Let me put it another way · 12 That is the whole idea · 13 go over · 14 spell out · 15 comes down to


---

## B2.1 · Unit 7 · A Reduction in Exceedances

**0B** · Guess it
The university's data, emailed. Its series runs without a break from 2014 at the original junction, so it is the only document in the room that can say whether the air changed or the instrument moved. The 2023 report does not mention the move at all in its body; eleven words on page forty are the whole of it.

**1B** · Sort — what you do to a number, how fine or how sure it is, or what you compare it with?
> **what you compare it with** — series · anomaly · benchmark · proxy · metric
> **how fine or how sure** — granularity · resolution · uncertainty · variance
> **what you do to a number** — aggregation · calibration · measurement
**The number standing in for one nobody could get:** *proxy*. Exceedance days are a proxy for what people breathe, and they are reported as though they were it. *Anomaly* is arguable — it is a departure from a comparison, so it lives in the third column only because a comparison is what makes it one.

**1D** · Collocation Lab
1 a reporting period · 2 Year on year · 3 data point · 4 The raw figures · 5 a confidence band  
**What changed in 2021 without being announced:** *a reporting period* — from the calendar year to April–March, which makes one year in the series nine months long.

**1E** · The phrasal verbs, and the three fixed expressions
***Compared with what?*** It cannot be answered without naming a baseline, and a percentage with no baseline is not a fact about the world — it is a fact about two numbers, one of which has been withheld. It is the most useful question because it is the only one that works on every figure, including the honest ones.

**2A** · Two monitors 🔊 Track 7.1
**1** T · **2** F · **3** F · **4** T
**Table** — *the city's monitor*: 41 exceedance days in 2016, 22 in 2023; it moved one point four kilometres in 2021, from a junction between a bus layover and a depot to a park. *the university's monitor*: 41 in 2016, 39 in 2023; it has not moved. *the reporting period*: the calendar year until 2021, April to March thereafter; one year in the series is nine months long.

**Noticing** — Sentences 1 and 4 say who did it: *They moved the monitor* and *Exceedances fell by forty per cent at a site that changed* both keep a verb with a subject. In 2, 3 and 5 the verb has become a noun — *relocation*, *reduction*, *implementation* — and the agent has gone with it, without a single rule being broken.

**3D** · Use — controlled
1 reduction · 2 implementation · 3 deterioration · 4 assessment · 5 acknowledgement

**3E** · Use — guided
1 *The city reports that exceedance days fell by forty per cent in Ward 7 between 2016 and 2023.* · 2 *The city moved the monitor one point four kilometres in 2021.* · 3 *The city changed the reporting period from the calendar year to April–March in 2021.* · 4 *The city reports that the air in Ward 7 improved between 2018 and 2023.* · 5 *The city has not acknowledged, anywhere in the body of the report, that it moved the monitor.*

**3H** · Check — eight items
1 reduction · 2 implementation · 3 deterioration · 4 assessment … subject to revision · 5 acknowledgement · 6 *The reduction of exceedances was forty per cent* — or, better, *Exceedances fell by forty per cent.* *(reduce is a verb; the noun is reduction)* · 7 *The city reports that exceedance days at the Ward 7 monitor fell from 41 a year in 2016 to 22 in 2023.* · 8 *The relocation of the monitor in 2021 was not announced.*

**5C** · Comprehension — eight questions
1 Forty per cent, since 2016.  
2 At the junction of Lake and Seventh, between a bus layover and a distribution depot; the new one is one point four kilometres away, in a park.  
3 Forty-one exceedance days in 2016 and thirty-nine in 2023.  
4 At eighteen.  
5 The reporting period changed from the calendar year to April–March, so one year in the series is nine months long.  
6 Because a bus layover and a distribution depot are the reason the original site read high. Naming them lets the reader work out, without being told, that moving the instrument to a park would lower the number whatever happened to the air.  
7 That four defensible decisions, each argued separately, can produce a result nobody would defend as a set. The writer never says anybody acted in bad faith, and does not have to.  
8 Because it is the one thing nobody chose. Each decision has an author who can be asked about it; the absence of a document listing all four has no author, so there is nobody to answer for the only fact that matters.

**5D** · Vocabulary in context
**is not mentioned in the body of the report** — it appears, but only in a footnote · **runs without a break** — no gaps, no changes of site or method · **one year is nine months long** — the reporting period changed mid-series · **without removing them from the data** — the two worst years are still there and no longer count · **defensible on its own** — each decision survives examination separately, which is the only way any of them has been examined

**5E** · The counter-text
1 At eighteen. The whole visible change is four units tall on a chart whose range is four units wide, so a fall of eighteen days reads as a collapse.  
2 *a reduction … has been achieved* — agent: nobody named, no place, no date. *the implementation of revised monitoring arrangements* — agent: the city, in 2021. *monitoring location revised* — agent: the city, which moved it to a park. *the assessment is subject to revision* — agent: whoever will revise it, unnamed, and the only honest phrase on the page.  
3 Suggested: *In 2021 the city moved the Ward 7 monitor one point four kilometres, from a junction between a bus layover and a depot to a park. Figures before and after that date are not comparable.*  
4 The relocation. The university's instrument at the original junction recorded thirty-nine days in 2023 against forty-one in 2016 — a fall of two, not eighteen.

**6A** · Sort — bigger or smaller, better or worse, or somebody did something?
> **somebody did something** — implementation · acknowledgement · assessment · derivation
> **better or worse** — improvement · deterioration
> **bigger or smaller** — expansion · reduction
The first two columns are measurements and the third is an action with the actor removed — which is why the third column is the one that appears in official reports and the first two appear in charts. *Improvement* is arguable in the first column: accept it if the learner says that better and bigger are the same word in a report about emissions.

**6B** · Chunk completion
1 The reduction of · 2 A deterioration in · 3 The implementation of · 4 An increase in · 5 subject to revision · 6 in the order of · 7 a matter of · 8 The question of

**6C** · Three that are not interchangeable
*an increase in* names a change — something got bigger, and the sentence can say by how much. *the implementation of* names an action with a hidden agent — somebody implemented something and the noun does not say who. *subject to revision* is a warning about the number itself: it says the figure may move, and it is the only one of the three that tells a reader how much to trust what follows.

**6D** · The phrasal verbs and the fixed expressions
*allow for* and *factor in* both mean "take into account"; *account for* means "explain". *Account for* is also the one that can mean "make up a proportion of" — *night workers account for one in eight of the workforce* — which is why it is the most ambiguous of the three in a sentence about data.

**6E** · Contrast Clinic
> **1** **A deterioration in air quality at the junction**
> **2** **The relocation of the monitor in 2021**
> **3** **The city changed the reporting period in 2021.**
> **4** The error is **in the order of** ten per cent.
> **5** **The city reports that exceedance days at the Ward 7 monitor fell by forty per cent between 2016 and 2023.**
> **6** **No acknowledgement of the move** appears in the report.
> **7** **The movement of the baseline from 2016 to 2018**
> **8** **The city implemented the new reporting period in 2021.**

**12A** · Recycle — fifteen items
1 reduction · 2 implementation · 3 deterioration · 4 acknowledgement · 5 assessment · 6 Year on year · 7 y axis · 8 in the order of · 9 The raw figures · 10 a confidence band · 11 The chart does not show · 12 Compared with what · 13 smooth out · 14 allow for *(accept* factor in*)* · 15 level off


---

## B2.1 · Unit 8 · Seventy-Three Words

**0B** · Guess it
The subeditor and the reporter. He is standing over the printout with the pencil; she is beside the spike rather than at her own desk, which is where a reporter stands when she has no intention of leaving until the paragraph is settled. The paragraph is ringed and uncut because ringing it is what you do when you have decided not to cut something and cannot yet say why.

**1B** · Sort — a stage, an action, a judgement, or a thing round the edge?
> **a judgement** — redundancy · verbosity · concision
> **a stage** — draft · proof · abstract
> **an action** — cut · edit · trim
> **round the edge** — kicker · caption · standfirst
**Both a stage and an action:** *edit* — and *cut* and *abstract* behave the same way. The stress tells you which in two of the three: *an ABstract* and *to abSTRACT*. *Edit* and *cut* do not move their stress, which is why the noun senses are the ones learners meet last.

**1D** · Collocation Lab
1 A working draft · 2 A hard cut · 3 An edited version · 4 The final cut · 5 What survives  
**The one the reader sees:** *an edited version* — and the point of the item is that nothing in the paper tells them it is one.

**1E** · The phrasal verbs, and the three fixed expressions
***Nobody will miss it*** is the prediction; the other two are decisions. Nobody in the room is in a position to check it, which is the whole problem: the readers who would have missed it are, by definition, the ones who never find out it was there. A paper that marks its cuts is the only kind that can ever test the sentence.

**2A** · Two minutes past six 🔊 Track 8.1
**1** T · **2** F · **3** F · **4** T
**Table** — *the subeditor*: would cut the second and third quotations and the junction description, five hundred and ten words, at the cost of two voices and the place. *the reporter*: would cut the junction description and one quotation, three hundred and thirty words, at the cost of the afternoon she spent there. *the agreed version*: three hundred of repetition, ninety of duplicate quotation, two hundred and forty of description and one hundred and eighty of quotation — eight hundred and ten words, and the ringed paragraph stays.

**Noticing** — 1 *Dev has filed.* 2 *I have cut both of the quotes.* 3 *We can lose the colour, but I would rather not lose the colour.* 4 *The third quote can go too.* 5 *I do not want to cut that one either.* 6 *I will decide if you will decide.* Words saved: one, three, six, three, five and two.

**3D** · Use — controlled
1 has · 2 have, both · 3 So · 4 Neither · 5 so

**3E** · Use — guided
1 *The second quote can go, and so can the third.* · 2 *I have cut both.* · 3 *I would not cut that paragraph, and neither would she.* · 4 *He asked me to file it and I did.* · 5 *The long version is better than the short one.*

**3H** · Check — eight items
1 has · 2 have · 3 So · 4 Neither · 5 so · 6 *— I would not. — Neither would I.* *(either is used after a negative clause, not instead of one:* I would not either *is the alternative)* · 7 *The long version is better than the short one.* · 8 spoken *and I did* · neutral *and I did it* · formal *and I did so*

**5C** · Comprehension — eight questions
1 Fourteen hundred words; the hole is six hundred.  
2 Three hundred words of repetition, two sentences saying the same thing in different registers, and a quotation that adds nothing the paragraph above has not said.  
3 Five hundred.  
4 The description of the junction, then the second quotation, then the third.  
5 Seventy-three words.  
6 Because the hole is a physical fact about a page and the story is a judgement. Establishing that one of the two is not negotiable makes every sentence that follows a choice rather than a complaint.  
7 Because the cost of cutting it is otherwise invisible. Every reader can see that a description is replaceable; only somebody who knows an afternoon went into it can price it, and the writer is putting that price on the page.  
8 It leaves the reader to make the cut. The piece has given the hole, the arithmetic and the five candidates, and the question is the one the subeditor has not answered either.

**5D** · Vocabulary in context
**says the same thing in different registers** — the same content written twice at different levels of formality · **carries the argument** — contains the point the piece is making, so the others only agree with it · **the least readable paragraph** — the driest, and here that is a description rather than a criticism · **could not be written by somebody who had not been there** — it contains names and dates that required attendance · **rings it** — draws a circle round it in pencil, which marks it as a decision not yet made

**5E** · The counter-text
1 Eight hundred and eighty-three words: three hundred of repetition, ninety of duplicate quotation, two hundred and forty of description, one hundred and eighty of quotation and seventy-three of names and dates.  
2 *approved* — the reader rebuilds a body, a meeting and a date from general knowledge of how votes happen. *seven votes to two* — the reader rebuilds nine people, from the arithmetic. *the contract* — the reader rebuilds which contract, from the headline. In each case the rebuilding material is already on the page or in the reader's head.  
3 The seventy-three words. A reader cannot rebuild two names and two dates from anything, and the proof is that no reader of the printed version could supply them when asked: nineteen per cent against sixty-three for the marked version.  
4 Open. The defensible answer is the short version with a link to the marked long one, because six hundred words is what most readers have time for and the names are then retrievable by anybody who wants them.

**6A** · Sort — a noun, a clause, a second speaker, or a place?
> **a second speaker** — neither · either
> **a clause** — so · likewise
> **a place** — elsewhere
> **a noun** — one · such · ones
*Likewise* is the arguable one: it stands in for a whole clause and it also marks a second speaker agreeing, so accept it in either of those two columns. *Such* stands in for a kind of thing rather than a thing, which is why *no such paragraph* works and *no such it* does not.

**6B** · Chunk completion
1 done so · 2 did it · 3 the same · 4 If so · 5 If not · 6 as such · 7 the former · 8 The latter

**6C** · Three that are not interchangeable
*do so* belongs in a report — formal, written, and it points at a verb phrase with no ambiguity. *do it* belongs in conversation, and in between in print: it is neutral and slightly more concrete. Bare *did* is the spoken British form — *he asked me to file and I did* — and is the one advanced learners avoid, which is what marks their register.

**6D** · The phrasal verbs and the fixed expressions
***I will if you will.*** It looks like a condition and is an offer: the speaker has already decided and is asking for cover. What they want is not the other person's action but their co-signature, so that the decision has two names on it.

**6E** · Contrast Clinic
> **1** She would not cut it, **and neither would I**.
> **2** **The former** carries the argument; **the latter** merely agrees with it.
> **3** I have cut **both**.
> **4** **If so**, send it to the stone.
> **5** The second quote can go, **and so can the third**.
> **6** The paragraph is unreadable and, **as such**, goes first.
> **7** He asked me to file the story **and I did**.
> **8** The long version is better than **the short one**.

**12A** · Recycle — fifteen items
1 has · 2 have · 3 So · 4 Neither · 5 so · 6 done so · 7 the former · 8 The latter · 9 as such · 10 The first pass · 11 It can go · 12 We are over by forty · 13 cut it down · 14 chopped out · 15 pared back


---

## B2.1 · Unit 9 · Three Readers, One Style Guide

**0B** · Guess it
The app preview, held up. The app had three hundred and forty thousand readers last month against a print run of a hundred and twelve thousand. The style guide on the table — two hundred and fourteen pages — has no entry for *app*, *newsletter* or *push alert*, so the channel with the most readers is the one with no written rules at all.

**1B** · Sort — a person or a group, a place or a time, or a document?
> **a place or a time** — galley · splash · spike · slot · deadline
> **a person or a group** — desk · subeditor · copytaster · conference
> **a document** — wire · house style · style guide
**The one that is also a verb:** *spike* — to spike a story is to kill it, and the object on the desk is where killed stories used to go. *Desk* and *conference* are in the first column because in a newsroom both are groups of people before they are furniture; accept either in the second with that argument. *Wire* is a source rather than a document in the strict sense — accept a fourth column.

**1D** · Collocation Lab
1 The news list · 2 a running order · 3 hold the front page · 4 A late change · 5 filed on time  
**A measure of a person:** *filed on time* — every day for nine years. The other four are properties of the day's paper.

**1E** · The phrasal verbs, and the three fixed expressions
***That is a splash*** is the judgement; the other two are decisions. The news editor makes it, and only the news editor: it says a story is strong enough for page one before anybody has decided to run it there, and it commits nothing. The decisions — *we are going with it* and *it will not make the paper* — can be reversed; the judgement is quoted back at you for a fortnight.

**2A** · The style guide meeting 🔊 Track 9.1
**1** T · **2** F · **3** F · **4** T
**Table** — *the paper*: nine markers per thousand words; the reader is somebody holding a page and able to see how long the article is; the desk says more markers cost precision. *the newsletter*: thirty-one; the reader is on a phone between two other things; the writer says fewer markers cost the reader their place. *the app*: fourteen; the reader is the same as the newsletter's; nobody has decided anything, because the app version is made by cutting the print version.

**Noticing** — Sentences 2 and 4, *that said* and *having said that*, concede: both grant the previous point before continuing. Sentence 5, *by contrast*, announces a comparison. Sentence 6, *in any case*, says the argument no longer matters — it sets the previous point aside rather than answering it.

**3D** · Use — controlled
1 However · 2 Accordingly · 3 To begin with · 4 That said · 5 In any case

**3E** · Use — guided
1 *The figures, however, do not support that* — quieter, more confident. *The figures do not support that, however* — an afterthought. · 2 *The newsletter is, nevertheless, read to the end* / *… read to the end, nevertheless.* · 3 *The guide will, accordingly, be rewritten* / *… be rewritten accordingly.* *(at the end,* accordingly *stops being a marker and becomes a manner adverb)* · 4 *The app version, meanwhile, went out unchanged* / *… went out unchanged meanwhile.* *(the end position is barely possible, which tells you something about* meanwhile*)* · 5 *The print version, by contrast, carries nine markers* / *… carries nine markers, by contrast.* Force falls from front to middle to end in every case.

**3H** · Check — eight items
1 However · 2 Accordingly · 3 To begin with · 4 That said · 5 In any case · 6 *The guide was revised in 1998, whereby the newsletter entry was added* — or simply *The guide was revised in 1998.* *Whereby* cannot open a sentence; it joins a clause to a noun phrase before it. · 7 Front: *However, the figures do not support that* — most force, a formal announcement. Middle: *The figures, however, do not support that* — less force, more confidence. End: *The figures do not support that, however* — least force, an afterthought. · 8 Any sentence where the second clause does not hold in spite of the first — e.g. *The guide is long; nevertheless, it is detailed.* Length and detail do not oppose each other, so *nevertheless* promises a contrast that never arrives.

**5C** · Comprehension — eight questions
1 Two hundred and fourteen pages; last fully revised in 1998.  
2 Four lines, saying that discourse markers should be used sparingly.  
3 Nine per thousand words in print, thirty-one in the newsletter, fourteen in the app.  
4 Because the app version is produced by cutting the print version, so its density is whatever survived the cut.  
5 Three quarters of sixty-one thousand — about forty-six thousand.  
6 Because a number that looks like a decision and is not is the most misleading thing on the page. Fourteen reads as a compromise between nine and thirty-one; it is an accident, and the paper has been citing it as evidence of a middle way.  
7 One document with one voice cannot hold two true statements that contradict each other about different readers. The guide is not wrong; it is a single-valued answer to a question that has three values.  
8 That the guide should state one rule and let each channel set its own figure under it — which is option three, proposed by the structure of the sentence rather than by the writer.

**5D** · Vocabulary in context
**used sparingly** — rarely, with no figure attached, which is why the entry has lasted · **not by policy but because** — the result of a process, not a decision · **reading on a phone between two other things** — interrupted, with no sense of how much is left · **does not come back** — a reader who loses the thread abandons the piece permanently · **has one voice** — the guide describes a single register, for a single reader

**5E** · The counter-text
1 Print: none. Newsletter: five — *so*, *here is the thing*, *however*, *and the guide?*, *which brings me to*. App: one — *however*.  
2 *Which brings me to the awkward bit*, which promises a next item and is followed by nothing. The paragraph ends there.  
3 It kept whichever markers happened to survive the cut, which is why *however* is doing work in the app version that it was not doing in print: it now joins two facts that were separate sentences.  
4 Open. Most readers choose the newsletter version on a phone and the print version on paper — which is the finding, because they are not the same version and the guide says they should be.

**6A** · Sort — contrast, result, time, or formal only?
> **contrast** — however · nevertheless
> **time** — meanwhile · thereafter
> **result** — thereby · accordingly · hence
> **formal only** — whereby
*Thereby* is the one to argue about: it marks a result and it is also formal-only, so accept it in either column with the argument. *Hence* is formal but not formal-only — it survives in speech, where *whereby* does not.

**6B** · Chunk completion
1 In the first place · 2 That said · 3 As it happens · 4 For one thing · 5 On the other hand · 6 By contrast · 7 In any case · 8 To begin with

**6C** · Three that are not interchangeable
*however* simply contrasts: the next clause pushes back. *nevertheless* contrasts in spite of something: it concedes the first clause and holds the second anyway. *that said* grants the other person a point first, which is why it is the one that keeps a negotiation open.

**6D** · The phrasal verbs and the fixed expressions
***The point being.*** It is left unfinished on purpose: the speaker states the relationship and lets the listener supply the clause, which keeps the floor without committing to a form of words. Finish it and you have made a claim somebody can answer; leave it and you have made one they have to complete for you.

**6E** · Contrast Clinic
> **1** You may dispute the count. **In any case**, the survey is separate.
> **2** The guide was revised in 1998; **accordingly**, it says nothing about newsletters. *(or* hence*)*
> **3** The rule was agreed, **thereby** requiring every channel to state its own density.
> **4** The print desk objected; **meanwhile**, the newsletter went out unchanged.
> **5** The guide has one voice, **whereas** the paper has three readers. *(formal written contrast; accept* by contrast *at the front of the second clause)*
> **6** The newsletter is informal. **That said**, it is the most read thing the paper makes.
> **7** **To begin with**, the markers were counted; **thereafter**, the readers were surveyed.
> **8** The app has more readers; **however**, it has no style guide.

**12A** · Recycle — fifteen items
1 However · 2 Accordingly *(accept* hence*)* · 3 Meanwhile · 4 Nevertheless · 5 Thereafter · 6 In the first place · 7 That said · 8 As it happens · 9 In any case · 10 Which brings me to the point · 11 Having said that · 12 The point being · 13 send through · 14 turn around · 15 sit on


---

## B2.1 · Unit 10 · Never Once in the Culture Pages

**0B** · Guess it
Tuesday's front page and the culture page from the same week. Both are about the six streets around the Lake and Seventh junction; only the front page names the ward, because the Tuesday story is about a crime and the Friday one is about a hall reopening. Nothing in the style guide requires that, and the difference has been consistent since at least 1994.

**1B** · Sort — the place and its edges, who lives there, or what the paper adds?
> **what the paper adds** — framing · displacement · headline · stereotype · gentrification
> **the place and its edges** — neighbourhood · zoning · boundary · ward
> **who lives there** — resident · tenure · demographic
**The two residents never use about themselves:** *demographic* and *gentrification*. Both are words for a place said from outside it. *Gentrification* and *displacement* are processes happening in the ward rather than things the paper adds — accept them in a column of their own, which is how the cutaway in 1A sets them out.

**1D** · Collocation Lab
1 named in the headline · 2 A postcode in a headline · 3 who is quoted / the first paragraph · 4 The first paragraph · 5 The local paper  
**What decides what a reader remembers:** the headline — and that is the point of item 4. The first paragraph repeats it, which is why it so rarely corrects it.

**1E** · The phrasal verbs, and the three fixed expressions
***That is not what it is called*** is the correction; the other two are accusations. It is the hardest to answer because it is a fact rather than a judgement: there is a name residents use and a name the paper uses, and the paper cannot defend its own name without arguing that it knows the place better than the people in it.

**2A** · The naming rule 🔊 Track 10.1
**1** T · **2** F · **3** F · **4** T
**Table** — *crime*: the ward is named in seventy-one per cent of headlines; a resident is quoted in eleven per cent; thirty-four pieces. *planning*: eighteen per cent named, thirty-four per cent quoting a resident; twenty-two pieces. *culture*: nought per cent named, fifty-two per cent quoting a resident; eleven pieces.

**Ordinary order** — 1 *Nobody had ever counted them before.* 2 *The culture pages did not name it once.* 3 *We only use the ward when the story is about crime.* 4 *As soon as the letter arrived, the editor asked for the figures.* 5 *The ward appears in good news so rarely that readers assume there is none.* 6 *We did not know the rule had never been written down.* What is lost each time is the emphasis: the fronted word is the one the reader repeats, and in ordinary order nothing is.

**3D** · Use — controlled
1 *Never before had anybody counted them.* · 2 *Not once did the culture pages name it.* · 3 *Only when the story is about crime do we use the ward.* · 4 *No sooner had the letter arrived than the editor asked for the figures.* · 5 *So rarely does the ward appear in good news that readers assume there is none.*

**3E** · Use — guided
1 *Nobody had counted them before.* — the count becomes background. · 2 *The culture pages did not name it once.* — *once* now reads as a quantity, not as a refusal. · 3 *The editor only then asked for the figures.* — the sequence survives and the turning point is gone. · 4 *A resident was not consulted at any point.* — the absolute becomes an ordinary negative. · 5 *The reaction was so great that the rule was rewritten.* — the sentence is now about the rule rather than about the reaction. In every case the information is identical and the sentence is no longer the one the reader repeats.

**3H** · Check — eight items
1 Never before had · 2 Not once did · 3 Only when … do · 4 No sooner had · 5 So rarely does · 6 *Never before had anybody counted them.* *(the auxiliary must come before the subject)* · 7 *At no point was a resident asked.* · 8 Open. A defensible example: *Not once in two years was my street named in anything but a crime report.*

**5C** · Comprehension — eight questions
1 Eighty-one, over two years.  
2 Crime seventy-one per cent, planning eighteen, business six, culture nought.  
3 Eleven.  
4 Specificity — a crime happens somewhere and readers want to know where.  
5 Forty thousand.  
6 Because it removes the defence that the paper has no interest in the ward. The paper campaigned for the hall, won, and then reported the reopening without saying where it was.  
7 That the specificity argument, if it is sound, applies to every event; a concert has a location in exactly the way a crime does. Six words are enough because the reader supplies the rest.  
8 It converts a table into a person. Everything before it is a pattern; the last sentence is somebody who lives four streets from the junction and has been written about eighty-one times without once being asked anything.

**5D** · Vocabulary in context
**sorted them by section** — grouped the eighty-one pieces by which part of the paper they appeared in · **all three were about specificity** — all three reasons were versions of the same argument · **account for sixty-two of the eighty-one** — make up that many of the total · **four streets away** — close enough to be inside every one of the stories and outside all of them · **has never been quoted in any of them** — the paper has written about her street eighty-one times and never asked her anything

**5E** · The counter-text
1 Thirty-one of the eighty-one: twenty-four of thirty-four crime pieces, four of twenty-two planning, one of fourteen business, and none of eleven culture.  
2 *POLICE SEEK WITNESSES IN WARD 7* (news, Tuesday) and *HALL REOPENS AFTER FOUR YEARS* (culture, Friday). The first names the place.  
3 A postcode names a smaller area than a ward and sounds administrative rather than communal. It locates a reader's own address inside the story, which a ward name does not, and it is the one form residents object to most.  
4 Open. A consistent set: *HALL REOPENS IN WARD 7 AFTER FOUR YEARS*; *WARD 7 FIRM WINS CONTRACT*; *POLICE SEEK WITNESSES AFTER WARD 7 ASSAULT*. The test is that no section can be identified from the naming alone.

**6A** · Sort — how often, how nearly not, where, or how restricted?
> **how restricted** — only
> **how nearly not** — little · hardly · scarcely
> **how often** — rarely · seldom · never
> **where** — nowhere
*Little* is the one to argue about: in *little did we know* it is a degree word doing the work of a negative, and it survives almost nowhere else. *Never* is a frequency word that behaves as an absolute — accept a fourth column for it with that argument.

**6B** · Chunk completion
1 Never before · 2 Not once · 3 Only when · 4 No sooner · 5 At no point · 6 Under no circumstances · 7 So rarely does · 8 Such was the

**6C** · Three that are not interchangeable
*rarely* is about frequency — how many times out of how many. *hardly* is about degree — how nearly something did not happen at all, and it says nothing about how often. *only* restricts the whole clause: it does not describe the event, it rules out every case except the one named.

**6D** · The phrasal verbs and the fixed expressions
***Single out.*** The letter accuses the paper of singling the ward out — naming it when the news is bad and not otherwise. The paper would say it was *setting out* the facts: giving the location because a location is a fact. The disagreement is not about any one headline; it is about whether a practice applied in one section only is specificity or selection.

**6E** · Contrast Clinic
> **1** **Not once did** the culture pages name it.
> **2** **At no point was** a resident consulted.
> **3** **No sooner had** the letter arrived **than** the editor called.
> **4** **Only when** the story is about crime **do we** use the ward.
> **5** **Little did we know** that the rule had never been written down.
> **6** **Never before had** anybody counted them.
> **7** **Such was the** reaction **that** the rule changed.
> **8** **So rarely does** it appear in good news **that** readers assume there is none.

**12A** · Recycle — fifteen items
1 Never before · 2 Not once · 3 Only when · 4 No sooner · 5 At no point · 6 Under no circumstances · 7 So rarely does · 8 Such was the · 9 Little did · 10 Nobody from there was asked · 11 That is not what it is called · 12 Whose city is this · 13 single out · 14 point up · 15 priced out
